Select-then-differentiate: Solving Bilevel Optimization with Manifold Lower-level Solution Sets
Saeed Masiha, Zebang Shen, Negar Kiyavash, Niao He

Abstract
We study optimistic bilevel optimization when the lower-level problem has a non-isolated manifold of minimizers. In this setting, the hyper-objective may be non-differentiable because the upper-level criterion must choose among multiple lower-level solutions. Under a local Polyak–Łojasiewicz (PŁ) condition, we show that differentiability does not require the lower-level solution set to be a singleton: uniqueness of the optimistic selection is sufficient. This yields an explicit pseudoinverse-based hyper-gradient formula extending the classical singleton-minimizer result. We further characterize the regularity of the hyper-objective: non-degeneracy of the selected minimizer along the solution manifold yields local smoothness, while failure of uniqueness can create many non-differentiable points and failure of non-degeneracy can destroy all positive Hölder regularity of the hyper-gradient. Motivated by this theory, we propose HG-MS, a select-then-differentiate method combining explicit optimistic selection with efficient pseudoinverse-based hyper-gradient computation. Despite the nonconvex nature of optimistic selection over the lower-level solution manifold, we show that HG-MS converges to a stationary point of the optimistic objective with complexity governed by the intrinsic dimension of the solution manifold rather than its ambient dimension. Empirically, we test a practical variant of HG-MS for matched-budget LLM source reweighting. This variant preserves the select-then-differentiate principle and obtains the best GSM8K/MATH scores across the tested backbones, along with competitive or best MT-Bench instruction-following results.



1
Introduction

Many modern machine learning tasks involve an upper objective that evaluates the outcome of an inner training procedure. Such tasks can be formulated as bilevel optimization. Canonical examples include hyperparameter optimization and meta-learning
(
22
;
42
;
5
;
23
)
, differentiable neural architecture search
(
41
)
, bilevel formulations in reinforcement learning
(
31
)
, and more recently, bilevel formulations for LLM data reweighting, safe fine-tuning, and preference alignment
(
56
;
64
;
34
)
.
The progress in aforementioned applications has been driven by the ability to compute

hyper-gradients
, which allows us to directly optimize the hyper-objective.

A standard assumption underlying much of the bilevel optimization literature is that the lower-level problem has a
unique
minimizer. In this singleton regime, the lower-level solution map is single-valued, and implicit differentiation yields a tractable hyper-gradient formula
(
57
;
24
;
45
;
33
;
39
;
36
)
. However, this assumption is often violated in the nonconvex and overparameterized settings that dominate modern applications. Neural-network training objectives admit many global minimizers, which often are not isolated points but rather come from connected sets with manifold structure
(
21
;
26
;
53
)
. In such regimes, the lower-level solution map is set-valued, and the hyper-objective critically depends on lower-level
minima selection
.
For instance, in recent LLM data reweighting formulations
(
56
;
64
)
, the lower-level objective is typically the training loss, while the upper-level objective is evaluated on a validation set. Distinct lower-level minimizers can therefore attain nearly identical training loss but result in very different validation performance. This makes
optimistic bilevel optimization
a natural formulation: among all lower-level minimizers, select the one that is best for the upper level.

In this paper, we focus on the optimistic bilevel optimization problem

\min_{\theta\in\Theta}\ F(\theta)\qquad\text{where}\qquad F(\theta):=\min_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)}f(\theta,x),\qquad\mathcal{S}(\theta):=\arg\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}g(\theta,x),

(1)

with compact convex
\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
. We refer to the inner optimization over
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
as
minima selection
and define
{\texttt{O}}(\theta):=\arg\min_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)}f(\theta,x)
.
In particular, when
{\texttt{O}}(\theta)
is a singleton, we denote the unique optimistic selection as
x^{\star}(\theta)
.
In general, without additional structure,
F
need not be continuous, let alone differentiable
(
20
;
3
)
.

Why existing theory is insufficient.
Most existing hyper-gradient analyses establish differentiability of the hyper-objective only when the lower-level solution set is a
singleton
,
\mathcal{S}(\theta)=\{x^{\star}(\theta)\}
(so minima selection is trivial), and when the lower-level loss
g(\theta,\cdot)
satisfies locally quadratic growth around
x^{\star}(\theta)

(
57
;
24
;
45
;
33
;
39
;
36
)
(see Appendix
B.1
for details). Recent extensions go beyond the singleton-minimizer setting
(
72
;
63
)
, but require
g
to be
convex
in the lower-level variable
x
, a strong and often impractical assumption. In overparameterized models,
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
is typically neither singleton nor convex; consequently, existing results leave open whether the optimistic hyper-objective
F
is differentiable, and hence whether its hyper-gradient is well defined. Due to space constraints, a more detailed comparison with related work is deferred to Appendix
B
.

Our starting point: manifold-structured lower-level minimizers.

Motivated by the loss landscapes of overparameterized deep networks
(
67
;
40
)
, we assume the lower-level loss
g
satisfies the
PŁ
∘
condition of
(
28
)
with respect to the lower-level variable
x
. This assumption implies a structured non-singleton regime: the lower-level solution set
S(\theta)
is an
embedded submanifold
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and existing results further imply that the optimistic hyper-objective
F
is Lipschitz continuous
(
48
)
. But Lipschitzness alone does not justify hyper-gradient methods
(
9
)
.
This leads to the main technical question of the paper:
When lower-level minimizers form a manifold, what additional conditions make the optimistic hyper-objective differentiable or smooth, and hence a good candidate for hyper-gradient optimization?

Main insight: uniqueness of the optimistic selection suffices.

The main source of nonsmoothness is the instability of the minima selection. When
{\texttt{O}}(\theta)
contains multiple points, small perturbations of
\theta
can change which minimizer is selected, making
F
non-differentiable even if the map
\theta\mapsto\mathcal{S}(\theta)
varies smoothly. Thus, uniqueness of the optimistic selection is the key regularity condition.
Under the local
PŁ
∘
condition, we show that if
{\texttt{O}}(\theta)=\{x^{\star}(\theta)\}
, then
F
is continuously differentiable, even when
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
is non-singleton and non-convex. The resulting hyper-gradient admits an explicit pseudoinverse-based formula that strictly generalizes the classical singleton-minimizer formula.

Theorem

(Regularity; Informal)
.

Suppose that
g
satisfies the local
PŁ
∘
condition (see
1
).

\bullet

If the optimistic minimizer is unique, i.e.,
{\texttt{O}}(\theta)=\{x^{\star}(\theta)\}
,
F
is continuously differentiable, and

\displaystyle\nabla F(\theta)=\nabla_{\theta}f(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))-\nabla^{2}_{\theta x}g(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))\big[\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))\big]^{\dagger}\nabla_{x}f(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta)),

(2)

where

\big[\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))\big]^{\dagger}=\mathbf{N}_{\theta}\big[\mathbf{N}_{\theta}^{\top}\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))\mathbf{N}_{\theta}\big]^{-1}\mathbf{N}_{\theta}^{\top},

and
\mathbf{N}_{\theta}
is an orthonormal basis for the normal space of
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
at
x^{\star}(\theta)
; see
Section

3
.

\bullet

If, in addition,
x^{\star}(\theta)
is non-degenerate (see
Section

3
), then
\nabla F
is Lipschitz continuous.

Our uniqueness condition is
strictly weaker
than the standard singleton lower-level solution assumption. We further show that our conditions are nearly necessary: if
{\texttt{O}}(\theta)
is not a singleton, then
F
can be non-differentiable at
infinitely many
points in an
arbitrarily small
neighborhood; even under unique selection, if
x^{\star}
is degenerate, then
\nabla F
can fail to be
\alpha
-Hölder continuous for
any

\alpha>0
. Together, these results give a sharp regularity picture for optimistic bilevel optimization in the manifold regime.

Algorithmic consequence: select, then differentiate.

Our regularity theory suggests a simple, practical algorithmic principle for bilevel optimization in the regime when the lower-level solution set exhibits manifold structure and the minima selection yields a unique minimizer: one should first
select
the optimistic minimizer, and only then
differentiate
.
To this end, we propose
Hyper-Gradient with Minima-Selection
(
HG-MS
); see
Algorithm

1
.
The method proceeds iteratively in two stages.
First, it approximates the minima selection using a
Best-of-N
(
BoN
) strategy: it draws
N
samples from the Gibbs measure
\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}x)\propto\exp(-g(\theta,x)/\lambda)\,\mathrm{d}x,
and outputs
\hat{x}_{N}^{\lambda}(\theta)
, the one achieving the smallest upper-level value
f(\theta,\cdot)
, as a proxy for
x^{\star}(\theta)
.
Note that when
\lambda\to 0
,
\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}
concentrates around
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
, allowing the procedure to explore the lower-level solution manifold.
Second, the method computes the hyper-gradient
\nabla F
by plugging
\hat{x}_{N}^{\lambda}(\theta)
in
Equation

2
, which is then used to update
\theta
.

For convergence analysis, we interpret
HG-MS
as an inexact projected gradient method applied to the hyper-objective
F
. The hyper-gradient error decomposes into three terms: (a) sampling error from
\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}
, (b)
BoN
selection error with finite
N
, and (c) approximation error of the pseudo-inverse in
eq.

2
. In particular, we show that the
BoN
selector satisfies
\forall\theta\in\Theta
,
\mathbb{E}[\|x^{\star}(\theta)-\hat{x}_{N}^{\lambda}(\theta)\|^{2}]=\mathcal{O}(N^{-\frac{1}{\texttt{k}}}+\lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}).

Here,
k
denotes the common dimension of the lower-level solution manifold
\{\mathcal{S}(\theta)\}_{\theta\in\Theta}
, rather than the ambient dimension
d
. There is an accuracy-efficiency tradeoff: smaller
\lambda
and larger
N
make the
BoN
selection more accurate, yet increase the computational cost due to slower sampling from the Gibbs measure and the need for more samples from the Gibbs measure. Balancing the tradeoff yields our complexity guarantee: we show that
HG-MS
finds an
\epsilon
-stationary point of the hyper-objective
F
using
\mathcal{O}(\text{poly}(\epsilon^{-\texttt{k}}))
queries to the oracle for
\partial_{x}g(\theta,\cdot)
. Note that the
\epsilon^{-\texttt{k}}
dependence is necessary in the worst case due to the nonconvex nature of the problem
(
51
)
.

Empirical evidence.

HG-MS arises naturally from our hypergradient formula, which also underlies the canonical update analyzed in our theory. At LLM scale, however, fully computing this hypergradient is costly. We therefore evaluate a scalable select-and-differentiate instantiation of HG-MS, which preserves the core mechanism while making large-scale experiments feasible. This allows us to assess the practical impact of the HG-MS selection mechanism in crucial real-world applications.
We evaluate HG-MS primarily on LLM source-reweighting tasks and two MNIST bilevel benchmarks. The LLM experiments are the main practical testbed: under matched LoRA fine-tuning budgets, HG-MS attains the best GSM8K/MATH scores across the Llama-3-8B, Qwen-2-7B, Gemma-2-9B, and Qwen2.5-32B math settings, and is competitive or best on MT-Bench instruction following. In two-source GPT-2 data-composition experiments it improves final test loss over all baselines; MNIST hyper-cleaning
(
61
)
and imbalanced-loss tuning provide multi-seed evidence that explicit selection improves upper-level optimization and test performance.

Contributions.
In summary, we make three contributions:

\bullet

Differentiability of the hyper-objective under manifold-structured lower-level minimizers.

We characterize when the optimistic hyper-objective is differentiable (and smooth) in the regime where the lower-level problem has a manifold of minimizers, showing that unique optimistic selection (and non-degeneracy) is the key condition.

\bullet

A hyper-gradient method with
BoN
minima selection and oracle complexity guarantee.

We propose
HG-MS
, that combines
BoN
minima selection with approximate hyper-gradient computation, achieving first-order stationarity guarantees with oracle complexity governed by the intrinsic dimension of the lower-level solution manifold.

\bullet

Empirical support for the select-then-differentiate principle.

We instantiate HG-MS in LLM source-reweighting tasks, where explicit minima selection changes the learned source mixtures and improves matched-budget downstream performance in several mathematical-reasoning and instruction-following settings. Complementary MNIST hyper-cleaning and imbalanced-loss tuning experiments provide multi-seed evidence that the same select-then-differentiate principle improves optimization stability and test performance relative to standard hyper-gradient and penalty/alternating baselines.

Notations

For any nonempty closed convex set
C\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
, we write
\mathrm{Proj}_{C}(u):=\arg\min_{v\in C}\|v-u\|
.
For any embedded
\mathcal{C}^{2}
submanifold
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
x\in\mathcal{M}
, we write

\mathcal{T}_{x}\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}_{x}\mathcal{M}
for the tangent and normal spaces, and

P_{\mathcal{T}_{x}\mathcal{M}}
,
P_{\mathcal{N}_{x}\mathcal{M}}
for the corresponding orthogonal projectors.
When
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{S}(\theta)
, we abbreviate
\mathcal{T}_{x}^{\theta}:=\mathcal{T}_{x}\mathcal{S}(\theta)
and
\mathcal{N}_{x}^{\theta}:=\mathcal{N}_{x}\mathcal{S}(\theta)
.
For probability measures
\nu
and
\mu
, we write
R_{2}(\nu\|\mu)
for the order-
2
Rényi divergence.
Additional notations are collected in
Section

A.1
.

2
Local PL Condition and the Manifold Solution Set

We now describe the
PŁ
∘
condition introduced by
28
, which is our central structural assumption.
Besides the standard local Polyak–Łojasiewicz (PŁ) inequality around the local minimizers, the extra requirement in the
PŁ
∘
condition ensures that all local minima are connected (hence are all global minima).
This supports efficient sampling-based approximations of the global optimal set
1
1

1

If
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
has at least two connected components, then the mixing time of Langevin sampling of
\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}
scales exponentially in
\lambda^{-1}
; see, e.g.,
49
.
.

Definition 2.1

(Local PŁ)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be the collection of all local minima of
g\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
.
We say that
g
is
locally PŁ
if there exists
\mu>0
such that for any connected component

\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{M}
, there exists an open neighborhood
\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime})\supset\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
satisfying

g(x)-\min_{x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime})}g(x^{\prime})\;\leq\;(2\mu)^{-1}\,\|\nabla g(x)\|^{2},\,\forall x\in\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime}).

Definition 2.2

(
PŁ
∘
condition)
.

A function
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies the
PŁ
∘
condition if: (1)
g\in\mathcal{C}^{2}
is locally PŁ, and
g\in\mathcal{C}^{4}
on every neighborhood
\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime})
; (2) Let
\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{M}):=\cup_{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}}\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{M}^{\prime})
. For any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\mathcal{N}(\mathcal{M})
, if
\nabla g(x)=0
, then
\nabla^{2}g(x)\prec 0
; (3) The collection of all local minima
\mathcal{M}
is contained in a compact set
\mathcal{V}
.

We impose
PŁ
∘
uniformly over the parameter
\theta
in the lower-level problem.

Assumption 1

(Lower-level
PŁ
∘
)
.

There exists a constant
\mu>0
and a compact domain
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, both independent of
\theta
, such that for every
\theta\in\Theta
, the function
g(\theta,\cdot)
satisfies the
PŁ
∘
condition.

1
has both geometrical (manifold structure) and analytical (normal nondegeneracy) implications on the lower-level solution set
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
, summarized as follows.

Proposition 2.3

(Characterizations of
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
)
.

Under
1
, the following hold for every
\theta\in\Theta
:
(i)
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
is a connected compact
\mathcal{C}^{2}
embedded submanifold of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
without boundary

(
28
, Prop. 3, Cor. 1)
. In particular, all local minima of
g(\theta,\cdot)
are global minima.
(ii) For any
x\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)
, the Hessian
H(\theta,x):=\nabla_{xx}^{2}g(\theta,x)
satisfies
\ker H(\theta,x)\;=\;\mathcal{T}_{x}^{\theta}
and
\langle v,H(\theta,x)v\rangle\;\geq\;c\,\|v\|^{2}
for all
v\in\mathcal{N}_{x}^{\theta}

for some constant
c>0
that is uniform over
\theta\in\Theta
and
x\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)

(
60
, Cor. 2.17)
.
Moreover, assuming
g\in\mathcal{C}^{3}
, there exists an integer
k
such that

\dim(\mathcal{S}(\theta))=\texttt{k},\forall\theta\in\Theta
,
(
48
, Lem. 3.5)
.

Remark 2.4
.

Under the lower-level local
PŁ
∘
condition and mild regularity on
f
and
g
, the hyper-objective function is Lipschitz continuous; see

(
48
, Lem. 3.2)
.
While this implies that
F
is differentiable almost everywhere, it remains insufficient for our purpose.

3
Local Differentiability of the Hyper-objective

Under the
PŁ
∘
condition, we identify the
worst-case
necessary conditions for the hyper-objective
F
to be continuously differentiable and smooth, i.e. Lipschitz gradient.
To preserve as much generality as possible, all assumptions are made only locally around a fixed point
\theta_{0}
; Consequently, all conclusions below are local, holding only in a neighborhood of
\theta_{0}
.
All formal proofs for this section are given in Appendix
C
.
This section is structured as follows:

\bullet

Through the counterexample in
Section

3
, we show that a tie in minima-selection, i.e., a non-singleton set
{\texttt{O}}(\theta)
, can make
F
non-differentiable, even if both
f
and
g
are
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
.

\bullet

We show in
Theorem

3.2
that the uniqueness of optimistic minimizer is sufficient to obtain
F\in\mathcal{C}^{1}
, together with the explicit hyper-gradient formula (
2
).

\bullet

Through the counterexample in
Section

3
, we show that the unique optimistic minimizer condition alone does not guarantee
\alpha
-Hölder continuity of
\nabla F
for any
\alpha>0
(including Lipschitz continuity, which corresponds to
\alpha=1
).

\bullet

We show that when enhancing the unique optimistic minimizer condition with the non-degeneracy condition (
Section

3
), the selected minimizer admits a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
local branch and
\nabla F
is locally Lipschitz (see
Theorem

3.6
).

Non-unique minima-selection can create kinks in the hyper-objective.

Even when
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
is a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
connected manifold and varies smoothly with
\theta
, minimizing
f(\theta,x)
over
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
can introduce kinks in
F
whenever the optimistic minimizer is not unique.
The example below shows that, in an
arbitrarily small
neighborhood,
F
may have
infinitely
many non-differentiable points despite
f
,
g
, and
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
being
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
; the singularities arise solely from switching among non-unique minimizers.

x_{1}
x_{2}
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
x^{+}(\theta)
x^{-}(\theta)
a(\theta)<0
selects
x^{+}
a(\theta)>0
selects
x^{-}

(a)

Two competing optimistic minimizers.

0.1
0.15
0.2
0.25
0.3
0.35
-0.0032
-0.0016
0
0.0016
0.0032
\theta
value
F(\theta)
\;\;r(\theta)a(\theta)
-r(\theta)a(\theta)

(b)

Two smooth branches and the resulting hyper-objective (kinks at ties
\theta_{k}=10/(k\pi)
, a countably infinite set accumulating at
0
, marked in black).

Figure 1
:
Illustration of
Section

3
.

Left: selection switches between two endpoints of
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
according to the sign of
a(\theta)
.
Right:
F(\theta)=-r(\theta)|a(\theta)|
is obtained by taking the smaller value between the two branches
\pm r(\theta)a(\theta)
, creating kinks at ties
a(\theta)=0
(black dots).

Example 3.1
.

Let
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
and
x=[x_{1},x_{2}]\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and write
r(\theta)=\sqrt{1+\theta^{2}}
.
Define
a(\theta):=e^{-1/\theta^{2}}\!\sin(10/\theta)
for
\theta\neq 0
and
a(0)=0
.
Consider the functions
f(\theta,x):=a(\theta)\,x_{1}+x_{2}^{2}
and
g(\theta,x):=\big(\|x\|^{2}-(r(\theta))^{2}\big)^{2}
, both of which belong to
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
The lower-level solution set is the circle

\mathcal{S}(\theta)=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\ \|x\|=r(\theta)\}.

On
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
,

f(\theta,x)=a(\theta)x_{1}+r(\theta)^{2}-x_{1}^{2}
, which is a concave quadratic in
x_{1}
. Therefore, for
a(\theta)\neq 0
, the minimizer is unique and given by

x^{\star}(\theta)=[-\mathrm{sign}(a(\theta))r(\theta),0];

at any
\theta
with
a(\theta)=0
, we have

\arg\min_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)}f(\theta,x)=\{[-r(\theta),0],[r(\theta),0]\},
i.e., the selected optimistic minimizer is
not unique
. Consequently,
F(\theta)=\min_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)}f(\theta,x)=-r(\theta)\,|a(\theta)|
. Since
a(\theta)=0
at
\theta_{k}:=10/(k\pi)
for each
k\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}
,
F
is not differentiable at every
\theta_{k}
(a countably infinite set accumulating at
0
); see
Figure

1
.

Uniqueness in minima-selection implies differentiability.

The preceding example suggests that non-uniqueness of the optimistic minimizer is the main obstruction to differentiability. Our first positive result shows that, under the
PŁ
∘
geometry summarized in
Section

2
, uniqueness is sufficient to recover
\mathcal{C}^{1}
regularity of
F
and an explicit hyper-gradient formula.

Theorem 3.2

(
\mathcal{C}^{1}
regularity of the hyper-objective under uniqueness)
.

Let
1
hold and assume
f\in\mathcal{C}^{1}
and
g\in\mathcal{C}^{3}
.
Fix
\theta_{0}\in\Theta
and assume there exist an open set
\mathcal{U}
containing
\theta_{0}
such that, for all
\theta\in\mathcal{U}
,
\arg\min_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)}f(\theta,x)
is a singleton.
Then there exists an open neighborhood
\mathcal{U}_{0}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
of
\theta_{0}
such that
F\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathcal{U}_{0})
.
Moreover, for all
\theta\in\mathcal{U}_{0}
, the hyper-gradient is given by
eq.

2
.

Proof sketch.

We sketch the proof in three steps; see Appendix
C.1
for the full argument.

Step 1: parameterize
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
locally by solving the normal equations.

Let
x_{0}:=x^{\star}(\theta_{0})
, and abbreviate

\mathcal{T}_{0}:=\mathcal{T}_{x_{0}}^{\theta_{0}}
and
\mathcal{N}_{0}:=\mathcal{N}_{x_{0}}^{\theta_{0}}
.
Choose orthonormal bases
U_{\mathcal{T}_{0}}
and
U_{\mathcal{N}_{0}}
spanning
\mathcal{T}_{0}
and
\mathcal{N}_{0}
. Write

x=x_{0}+U_{\mathcal{T}_{0}}u+U_{\mathcal{N}_{0}}v

and consider

\Phi(\theta,u,v):=U_{\mathcal{N}_{0}}^{\top}\nabla_{x}g(\theta,x_{0}+U_{\mathcal{T}_{0}}u+U_{\mathcal{N}_{0}}v).

By the normal nondegeneracy implied by
1
(see
Section

2
),

D_{v}\Phi(\theta_{0},0,0)=U_{\mathcal{N}_{0}}^{\top}\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{0},x_{0})U_{\mathcal{N}_{0}}

is invertible. The implicit function theorem gives a
\mathcal{C}^{2}
map
v=\varphi(\theta,u)
solving

\Phi(\theta,u,\varphi(\theta,u))=0
near
(\theta_{0},0)
. Defining

\psi(\theta,u):=x_{0}+U_{\mathcal{T}_{0}}u+U_{\mathcal{N}_{0}}\varphi(\theta,u),

we obtain a local chart for the normal critical set. The
PŁ
∘
geometry, the fixed local dimension of

\mathcal{S}(\theta)
, and local Hausdorff continuity then identify this chart with the nearby branch of the true minimizer
manifold
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
.

Step 2: reduce to a fixed-domain minimization.

By uniqueness of the optimistic minimizer and compactness of the lower-level solution set,
x^{\star}(\theta)
remains in
this chart patch for all
\theta
close to
\theta_{0}
. After possibly shrinking the parameter neighborhood, choose a
compact coordinate set
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{\texttt{k}}
, contained in the chart domain, that contains the coordinate

u^{\star}(\theta)
of the selected minimizer for all such
\theta
. The optimistic problem becomes the fixed-domain
minimization

F(\theta)=\min_{u\in U}\tilde{f}(\theta,u),\qquad\tilde{f}(\theta,u):=f(\theta,\psi(\theta,u)).

Step 3: apply an envelope argument and compute the hyper-gradient.

We apply the Danskin-type envelope lemma in Appendix
C.1
, namely

Section

C.1
. For a value function
F(\theta)=\min_{u\in U}\phi(\theta,u)
over a fixed compact set,
uniqueness of the minimizer allows one to differentiate
F
by differentiating only
\phi
with respect to

\theta
at the minimizing point; no derivative of the argmin map is needed. Applying this lemma pointwise, and then
using continuity of the unique minimizer on the fixed compact domain, yields the local
\mathcal{C}^{1}
regularity of

F
. Finally, differentiating the lower-level stationarity identity

\nabla_{x}g(\theta,\psi(\theta,u))=0
gives the pseudoinverse hyper-gradient formula (
2
).
∎

Remark 3.3
.

For compactness, denote
\mathbf{H}:=\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))
, and let
\mathbf{N}_{\theta}
and
\mathbf{T}_{\theta}
be orthogonal bases that span
\mathcal{N}_{x^{\star}(\theta)}^{\theta}
and
\mathcal{T}_{x^{\star}(\theta)}^{\theta}
, respectively.
Under the
PŁ
∘
condition,
Section

2
implies
\mathbf{N}_{\theta}^{\top}H\mathbf{N}_{\theta}\succ 0
;
Besides, we have
\mathbf{T}_{\theta}^{\top}\mathbf{H}\mathbf{T}_{\theta}=0
.
Consequently,
\mathbf{N}_{\theta}\left(\mathbf{N}_{\theta}^{\top}\mathbf{H}\mathbf{N}_{\theta}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{N}_{\theta}^{\top}
coincides with the Moore–Penrose pseudoinverse of
\mathbf{H}
.

Uniqueness in minima-selection alone is insufficient for Hölder continuity of
\nabla F
.

From an optimization perspective, however,
\mathcal{C}^{1}
regularity is not enough.
For a non-asymptotic analysis, we typically need
\nabla F
to be
\alpha
-Hölder continuous for some
\alpha>0
.
However,
Section

3
shows that uniqueness alone is
insufficient
to guarantee this property.
This occurs when the upper-level objective restricted to the minimizer manifold has zero intrinsic second-order
curvature at the selected point
2
2

2

One might instead try to require positivity of the ambient Hessian
\nabla^{2}_{xx}f
on tangent vectors, but this is not
an intrinsic condition, as
Section

3
demonstrates.
. Therefore, the restricted objective

f(\theta,\cdot)|_{\mathcal{S}(\theta)}
must be non-degenerate, for example through its Riemannian Hessian.

Definition 3.4

(Degenerate and non-degenerate optimistic minimizers)
.

Consider the setting of
Theorem

3.2
.
Let
\bar{f}_{\theta}:=f(\theta,\cdot)|_{\mathcal{S}(\theta)}
. We say that
x^{*}(\theta)
is a

non-degenerate optimistic minimizer
for
\theta\in\mathcal{U}_{0}
if the Riemannian Hessian
3
3

3

See

Equation

8
in Appendix
A.3
for the convention used here.
of the restricted
objective is positive definite at
x^{*}(\theta)
, i.e.,
\big\langle v,\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{S}(\theta)}\bar{f}_{\theta}(x^{*}(\theta))[v]\big\rangle>0
for all
v\in\mathcal{T}_{x^{*}(\theta)}^{\theta}\setminus\{0\}
.
Equivalently,
f(\theta,\cdot)
has strictly positive intrinsic second-order derivative along every tangent direction
of the manifold
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
at
x^{*}(\theta)
.
If this condition fails, we call
x^{*}(\theta)
a
degenerate optimistic minimizer
.

The next example shows that this intrinsic condition is not cosmetic: uniqueness can hold for every
\theta
while

\nabla F
has no positive Hölder modulus, and a condition based only on the ambient tangent Hessian would miss the
obstruction.

Example 3.5

(Uniqueness does not imply local
\alpha
-Hölder continuity of
\nabla F
)
.

Consider the following smooth construction. For
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
and
x=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, let

a(\theta):=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{4}\exp({-1/\theta^{2}})\sin(10/\theta),&\theta\neq 0,\\
0,&\theta=0,\end{cases}\qquad\eta(t):=\begin{cases}\operatorname{sign}(t)\exp({-1/t^{2}}),&t\neq 0,\\
0,&t=0,\end{cases}

and define
\phi(t):=\int_{0}^{t}\eta(s)\,\mathrm{d}s
. Then
\eta,\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,
\eta=\phi^{\prime}
is strictly increasing, and
\phi^{\prime\prime}(0)=\eta^{\prime}(0)=0
. Set

g(\theta,x):=\big(\|x\|^{2}-(1+\theta^{2})\big)^{2},\qquad f(\theta,x):=\phi(x_{2})+a(\theta)x_{2}+\rho(-x_{1})+b\big(\|x\|^{2}-(1+\theta^{2})\big),

where
b>0
is arbitrary,
\rho(s)=\exp({-1/s})
for
s>0
, and
\rho(s)=0
for
s\leq 0
. Thus
\mathcal{S}(\theta)=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\|x\|=r(\theta)\}
with
r(\theta):=\sqrt{1+\theta^{2}}
.

The added
b
-term is identically zero on
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
, so it does not change the constrained minimization problem.
As in
Section

3
, the term
\rho(-x_{1})
forces the optimistic minimizer to lie on the right semicircle, so minimizing
f(\theta,\cdot)
over
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
reduces to

\min_{|t|\leq r(\theta)}\bigl(\phi(t)+a(\theta)t\bigr).

Since
\eta
is strictly increasing, this scalar problem has a unique critical point determined by
\eta(t)+a(\theta)=0
, namely

t^{\star}(\theta)=\begin{cases}-\operatorname{sign}(a(\theta))\bigl(\log(1/|a(\theta)|)\bigr)^{-1/2},&a(\theta)\neq 0,\\
0,&a(\theta)=0.\end{cases}

Moreover,
|t^{\star}(\theta)|\leq(\log 4)^{-1/2}<1\leq r(\theta)
, so this critical point is feasible and therefore is the unique minimizer. Hence the optimistic minimizer is unique for every
\theta
and is

x^{\star}(\theta)=\bigl(\sqrt{r(\theta)^{2}-(t^{\star}(\theta))^{2}},\,t^{\star}(\theta)\bigr).

By
Theorem

3.2
, the hyper-objective
F
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
. Since the
b
-term vanishes on
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
, the scalar envelope formula gives
F^{\prime}(\theta)=a^{\prime}(\theta)t^{\star}(\theta)
. Let
\theta_{k}:=10/(k\pi)
, so
a(\theta_{k})=0
and
a^{\prime}(\theta_{k})\neq 0
. Then
F^{\prime}(\theta_{k})=0
. Since
a^{\prime}
is continuous and nonzero at
\theta_{k}
, and since
\theta_{k}
is a simple zero of
a
, we have
|a^{\prime}(\theta)|\asymp 1
and
|a(\theta)|\asymp|\theta-\theta_{k}|
near
\theta_{k}
. Therefore

|F^{\prime}(\theta)-F^{\prime}(\theta_{k})|=|F^{\prime}(\theta)|\asymp\frac{1}{\sqrt{\log(1/|\theta-\theta_{k}|)}}\qquad\text{as }\theta\to\theta_{k}.

Consequently, for every
\alpha>0
,

\frac{|F^{\prime}(\theta)-F^{\prime}(\theta_{k})|}{|\theta-\theta_{k}|^{\alpha}}\to\infty\qquad\text{as }\theta\to\theta_{k},

because
|\theta-\theta_{k}|^{\alpha}\sqrt{\log(1/|\theta-\theta_{k}|)}\to 0
. Thus
\nabla F
is not locally
\alpha
-Hölder continuous near
\theta_{k}
for any
\alpha>0
, even though the optimistic minimizer is unique for every
\theta
.

This example also shows why non-degeneracy must be intrinsic to
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
. At any zero
\theta_{k}
, we have
x^{\star}(\theta_{k})=(r(\theta_{k}),0)
, and the unit tangent direction is
v=(0,1)
. The ambient tangent Hessian satisfies

\big\langle v,\nabla^{2}_{xx}f(\theta_{k},x^{\star}(\theta_{k}))v\big\rangle=2b>0,

because the vanishing term
b(\|x\|^{2}-r(\theta_{k})^{2})
contributes
2bI
to the ambient Hessian. However, this term is zero on
\mathcal{S}(\theta_{k})
, so it contributes nothing to the restricted objective. The Riemannian Hessian of
f(\theta_{k},\cdot)|_{\mathcal{S}(\theta_{k})}
in direction
v
is therefore
0
, since the restricted scalar objective has second derivative
\phi^{\prime\prime}(0)=0
. A condition based only on the ambient tangent Hessian would incorrectly classify this example as non-degenerate, even though the intrinsic restricted problem is degenerate and
\nabla F
has no positive Hölder modulus.

Non-degeneracy of the unique optimistic minimizer implies local smoothness of
F
.

To establish the stronger stability needed for the subsequent complexity analysis, we rule out this pathological case by assuming that the unique optimistic minimizer
x^{\star}(\theta)
is non-degenerate.
To prove the local smoothness of
F
, the main missing ingredient is to establish a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
local branch for
x^{\star}(\theta)
.
Once this is available, we can write
F(\theta)=f(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))
locally and then control
\nabla F
using the regularity of
f,g
together with the pseudoinverse hyper-gradient formula.
The next theorem proves this branch regularity by first deriving a parameter-dependent local chart for
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
from
Section

2
, and then applying the implicit function theorem to the restricted problem on this chart.

Theorem 3.6

(Smoothness of the hyper-objective under unique non-degenerate optimistic minimizer)
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{2}
,
g\in\mathcal{C}^{3}
and satisfy
1
.
Fix
\theta_{0}\in\Theta
and set
x_{0}:=x^{\star}(\theta_{0})
. Suppose that
x_{0}
is the unique optimistic minimizer and is non-degenerate in the sense of
Section

3
.
Then there exists a neighborhood
\mathcal{U}
of
\theta_{0}
and a (unique)
\mathcal{C}^{1}
selection

x^{\star}:\mathcal{U}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
x^{\star}(\theta)\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)
such that
F(\theta)=f(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))
for all
\theta\in\mathcal{U}
.
Consequently,
\nabla F
is locally Lipschitz.

Proof sketch.

Let
x_{0}:=x^{\star}(\theta_{0})
, and abbreviate

\mathcal{T}_{0}:=\mathcal{T}_{x_{0}}^{\theta_{0}}
and
\mathcal{N}_{0}:=\mathcal{N}_{x_{0}}^{\theta_{0}}
.
Let
\dim(\mathcal{T}_{0})=\texttt{k}
, and choose orthonormal bases

U_{\mathcal{T}_{0}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times\texttt{k}}
and

U_{\mathcal{N}_{0}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times(d-\texttt{k})}
spanning
\mathcal{T}_{0}
and
\mathcal{N}_{0}
.

Step 1: parameterize
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
locally by solving the normal equations.

This is the same normal-chart construction as in the proof sketch of
Theorem

3.2
. The implicit function
theorem yields a
\mathcal{C}^{2}
map
v=\varphi(\theta,u)
, and hence the local chart

\psi(\theta,u):=x_{0}+U_{\mathcal{T}_{0}}u+U_{\mathcal{N}_{0}}\varphi(\theta,u).

On a sufficiently small neighborhood,
u\mapsto\psi(\theta,u)
parameterizes the local branch of
\mathcal{S}(\theta)

near
x_{0}
.

Step 2: reduce the optimistic minimization to intrinsic coordinates.

Define the pullback objective
\tilde{f}(\theta,u):=f(\theta,\psi(\theta,u))
. A point

x=\psi(\theta,u)
is a constrained critical point of
f(\theta,\cdot)
on
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
if and only if

\nabla_{u}\tilde{f}(\theta,u)=0
. At
(\theta_{0},0)
, this stationarity holds. The non-degeneracy assumption in

Section

3
says precisely that the second-order variation of

\tilde{f}(\theta_{0},\cdot)
at
u=0
is positive definite, so

D_{u}(\nabla_{u}\tilde{f})(\theta_{0},0)
is invertible. Applying the implicit function theorem again gives a

\mathcal{C}^{1}
map
u^{\star}(\theta)
, unique among nearby solutions, satisfying

\nabla_{u}\tilde{f}(\theta,u^{\star}(\theta))=0.

Set
x_{\mathrm{loc}}(\theta):=\psi(\theta,u^{\star}(\theta))
. This gives the unique local optimistic branch. The full
proof then uses the strict value gap from the uniqueness of
x_{0}
on the compact set
\mathcal{S}(\theta_{0})
to rule out
competitors outside the chart patch, yielding the local identity

x^{\star}(\theta)=x_{\mathrm{loc}}(\theta),\qquad F(\theta)=f(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta)).

Step 3: obtain the hyper-gradient by the chain rule.

Since
F(\theta)=f(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))
, the hyper-gradient follows from the chain rule. At a constrained minimizer,
the tangential component of
\nabla_{x}f(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))
vanishes, so only the normal component of

D_{\theta}x^{\star}(\theta)
is needed. Differentiating the lower-level stationarity condition

\nabla_{x}g(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))=0
and applying the pseudoinverse of

\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))
gives this normal sensitivity. Substitution gives
(
2
). The same formula, together with constant rank and the normal spectral gap from

Section

2
, implies local Lipschitz continuity of
\nabla F
.
∎

4
HG-MS
: a Select-then-Differentiate Algorithm

The previous section gives a local regularity result. For algorithm design and convergence analysis on
\Theta
, we impose uniqueness and non-degeneracy globally.

Assumption 2

(Unique and non-degenerate optimistic minimizer;
Global
)
.

For every
\theta\in\Theta
, the selected optimistic minimizer
x^{\star}(\theta)
is unique and non-degenerate in the sense of
Section

3
.

This yields global smoothness of the hyper-objective.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{2}
,
g\in\mathcal{C}^{3}
, and suppose
1
and
2
hold. There exists
L_{F}<\infty
such that
\|\nabla F(\theta)-\nabla F(\theta^{\prime})\|\leq L_{F}\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|
, for all
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta
. In particular,
F
is
L_{F}
-smooth on
\Theta
.

Thus, the natural upper-level update is projected gradient descent,
\theta_{t+1}:=\mathrm{Proj}_{\Theta}\big(\theta_{t}-\alpha_{t}\nabla F(\theta_{t})\big),
with
\nabla F
given by
eq.

2
. Implementing this update requires two primitives:
A
an approximate optimistic selector
x^{\star}(\theta)
;

B
an approximate pseudoinverse action

\big[\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))\big]^{\dagger}\nabla_{x}f(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))
.
Both tasks are challenging because
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
is an implicit non-convex manifold, and because the selected approximate lower-level solution may lie slightly off
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
, resulting in unstable Hessian pseudoinverse computations.

4.1
Algorithm Design

We propose an algorithm, Hyper-Gradient with Minima-Selection (
HG-MS
), that addresses the aforementioned two challenges by:
Best-of-N
(
BoN
) selection step from a Gibbs measure concentrated near
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
, and a stabilized implicit step which computes the pseudoinverse action through a ridge-regularized linear solver that uses Hessian–vector products.
Algorithm
1
is the ULA/Gibbs instantiation used for the formal convergence analysis. In practice, the sampler may be replaced by a finite-budget branch generator that preserves the same select-then-differentiate structure.

A

BoN
selection near
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
.

Since
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
is implicit, we approximate it using the Gibbs measure
\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}x)\propto\exp\{-g(\theta,x)/\lambda\}\,\mathrm{d}x
.
Under the manifold regularity induced by
PŁ
∘
,
\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}
concentrates in a thin tube around
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
as
\lambda\to 0
(see
Section

E.3.2
). Thus, samples from
\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}
serve as proxies for points on
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
.
Given samples
X_{1},\ldots,X_{N}\sim\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}
, we approximate optimistic selection by choosing
i^{\star}\in\arg\min_{1\leq i\leq N}f(\theta,X_{i})
and setting
\hat{x}_{N}^{\lambda}(\theta):=X_{i^{\star}}
.
Equivalently,
BoN
acts as a lower-tail operation at level
\delta=1/N
, selecting from the best
\delta
fraction of candidates under
f(\theta,\cdot)
. The resulting selection error and its geometric dependence on
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
are analyzed in Appendix
E.3
. We sample from
\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}
using ULA primitive
4
4

4

The oracle-complexity guarantee uses
\lambda
-scaled Gaussian initializations for the ULA chains.
(see Appendix
A.2
)
(
10
)
, whose convergence is controlled by the Poincaré inequality of
(
28
)
; this yields a trade-off between selection accuracy and sampling cost.

Remark 4.2
.

Approximating
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
via
\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}
also appears in
(
48
)
, but their goal is to approximate only the
value
of
F
. Here we must approximate the selected
solution
itself in order to compute a hyper-gradient, which requires a different analysis.

B

Hyper-gradient evaluation at the selected point.

After selecting
\hat{x}_{N}^{\lambda}(\theta_{t})
, we estimate the hyper-gradient by substituting it for
x^{\star}(\theta_{t})
in
eq.

2
. Let
H_{t}:=\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},\hat{x}_{N}^{\lambda}(\theta_{t}))
and
b_{t}:=\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},\hat{x}_{N}^{\lambda}(\theta_{t}))
.
We approximate
H_{t}^{\dagger}b_{t}
by solving the ridge-regularized system
(H_{t}+\gamma I)\tilde{v}_{t}=b_{t}
with conjugate gradients, using only Hessian–vector products, until
\|b_{t}-(H_{t}+\gamma I)\tilde{v}_{t}\|\leq\eta_{t}
.
The ridge parameter
\gamma\geq 0
stabilizes the solve when
\hat{x}_{N}^{\lambda}(\theta_{t})
is close to, but not exactly in
\mathcal{S}(\theta_{t})
: tangential zero eigenvalues of the exact Hessian can perturb near-zero eigenvalues off the manifold.
For the theoretical guarantee, we also allow clipping,
v_{t}:=\mathrm{Proj}_{\mathbb{B}_{d}(0;R_{v})}(\tilde{v}_{t})
.
When this optional safeguard is omitted in practice, we set
v_{t}=\tilde{v}_{t}
. The hyper-gradient estimate is then

\displaystyle\widehat{h}_{t}:=\nabla_{\theta}f(\theta_{t},\hat{x}_{N}^{\lambda}(\theta_{t}))-\nabla^{2}_{\theta x}g(\theta_{t},\hat{x}_{N}^{\lambda}(\theta_{t}))v_{t},

(3)

followed by a projected step on
\Theta
.
Algorithm

1
summarizes our approach.

Algorithm 1

HG-MS
: Hyper-gradient with minima selection

1:

initial
\theta_{0}\in\Theta
; stepsizes
\{\alpha_{t}\}
; temperature
\lambda
; number of chains
N
; ULA steps
K
and stepsize
h
; chain starts
\{X^{(0)}_{t,i}\}
; CG tolerances
\{\eta_{t}\}
; ridge
\gamma\geq 0
; clip radius
R_{v}>0

2:

for

t=0,1,2,\ldots

do

3:

Sample in parallel
X_{t,i}\leftarrow\textsc{ULA}(\theta_{t},g,X^{(0)}_{t,i},\lambda,K,h)

4:

i_{t}^{\star}\leftarrow\arg\min_{1\leq i\leq N}f(\theta_{t},X_{t,i})
;
\hat{x}_{N}^{\lambda}(\theta_{t})\leftarrow X_{t,i_{t}^{\star}}

5:

Solve

\big(\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},\hat{x}_{N}^{\lambda}(\theta_{t}))+\gamma I\big)\tilde{v}_{t}=\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},\hat{x}_{N}^{\lambda}(\theta_{t}))

by CG up to residual
\eta_{t}

6:

(Optional safeguard)

v_{t}\leftarrow\mathrm{Proj}_{\mathbb{B}_{d}(0;R_{v})}(\tilde{v}_{t})

7:

\widehat{h}_{t}\leftarrow\nabla_{\theta}f(\theta_{t},\hat{x}_{N}^{\lambda}(\theta_{t}))-\nabla^{2}_{\theta x}g(\theta_{t},\hat{x}_{N}^{\lambda}(\theta_{t}))v_{t}
;
\quad\theta_{t+1}\leftarrow\mathrm{Proj}_{\Theta}\big(\theta_{t}-\alpha_{t}\widehat{h}_{t}\big)

5
Convergence Analysis of HG-MS

Proof roadmap.

This section analyzes the ULA/Gibbs instantiation of
HG-MS
in
Algorithm

1
.
The proof has three layers. First,
Section

5
treats
HG-MS
as an inexact projected-gradient
method for the optimistic hyper-objective
F
; all algorithmic approximation effects enter through the hyper-gradient
error
e_{t}:=\widehat{h}_{t}-\nabla F(\theta_{t})
. Second,
Section

5
is a stability estimate: it bounds

\mathbb{E}\|e_{t}\|^{2}
by the selected-point error
\|\tilde{x}_{t}-x^{\star}(\theta_{t})\|
, the CG residual, the ridge bias,
and a clipped off-tube event. Third,
Section

5
controls the selected-point error for the
Best-of-
N
ULA/Gibbs candidate generator. Substituting these two estimates gives
Theorem

5.4
, and one
conservative parameter choice yields the oracle-complexity consequence in
Theorem

5.5
.

Additional regularity assumptions.

The convergence proof uses several assumptions beyond the local differentiability theory in
Section

3
; they
are collected formally in Appendix
A.3
. Their roles are the following.

3
gives uniform third-derivative control of
g(\theta,\cdot)
near
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
; this gives a
quantitative tube radius
r(\gamma)
on which the ridge-regularized Hessian is invertible and also controls the
curvature and volume terms used in the selection analysis.
4
collects global smoothness,
polynomial-growth, and coercive growth conditions; these make the Gibbs measures well-defined, give tube-tail and
off-tube moment bounds for ULA/Gibbs candidates, and keep the hyper-gradient stability constants finite.
Finally, the uniform intrinsic non-degeneracy result in
Section

A.3
is used in the selection proof to
convert a small validation-value gap on
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
into small distance to
x^{\star}(\theta)
.

Outer-loop guarantee.

This section analyzes
HG-MS
as an inexact projected-gradient method for the hyper-objective
F
.
Recall the hyper-gradient estimate
\widehat{h}_{t}
in (
3
), and define the
hyper-gradient error and the projected gradient mapping as
e_{t}:=\widehat{h}_{t}-\nabla F(\theta_{t})
and
\mathcal{G}_{\Theta}(\theta,\nabla F(\theta);\alpha):=\alpha^{-1}\big(\theta-\mathrm{Proj}_{\Theta}(\theta-\alpha\nabla F(\theta))\big)
, respectively.
Under
1
and
2
,
Section

4
provides the required global smoothness of
F
on
\Theta
.
The following inexact projected-gradient bound is standard when
F
is uniformly smooth; we state it for completeness

(
27
;
52
, e.g.,)
.

Proposition 5.1

(Convergence to near-stationarity with inexact hyper-gradients)
.

Suppose that
F
is
L_{F}
-smooth on
\Theta
.
Let
F_{\star}:=\inf_{\theta\in\Theta}F(\theta)>-\infty
.
Initialize
\theta_{0}\in\Theta
and run the projected update

\theta_{t+1}=\mathrm{Proj}_{\Theta}(\theta_{t}-\alpha\widehat{h}_{t})
with a constant
stepsize
\alpha\leq 1/L_{F}
(last line of
Algorithm

1
), then

\displaystyle\forall\ T\geq 1,\quad\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\big[\|\mathcal{G}_{\Theta}(\theta_{t},\nabla F(\theta_{t});\alpha)\|^{2}\big]\leq\frac{8\,(F(\theta_{0})-F_{\star})}{\alpha T}+\frac{10}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}\big[\|e_{t}\|^{2}\big].

(4)

Hyper-gradient error bound.

We now bound the error term
\mathbb{E}\|e_{t}\|^{2}
.
Write
\tilde{x}_{t}:=\hat{x}_{N}^{\lambda}(\theta_{t})
for the hard-selected lower-level point returned by

Algorithm

1
. The first ingredient is a stability bound for the stabilized hyper-gradient computation. It
does not yet use the fact that
\tilde{x}_{t}
came from Best-of-
N
selection; it only says that if the selected point is
close to
\mathcal{S}(\theta_{t})
, the ridge-regularized and CG-approximated hyper-gradient is close to the exact
pseudoinverse hyper-gradient.

Lemma 5.2

(Second-moment hyper-gradient error bound)
.

Let
1
,
3
,
2
and
4
hold.
Fix
\gamma>0
, choose a tube radius
r(\gamma)=\mathcal{O}(\gamma)
as in
Section

E.2
, and define

\mathsf{D}_{t}:=\mathrm{dist}(\tilde{x}_{t},\mathcal{S}(\theta_{t}))
and

\mathcal{E}_{t}:=\{\mathsf{D}_{t}\leq r(\gamma)\}
.
Choose the clipping radius in
Algorithm

1
so that

R_{v}=\mathcal{O}((L_{f,1}+\eta_{t})/\gamma)
as in
Section

E.2
.
Then for all
t
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|e_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 3C_{x}^{2}\Big(1+\tfrac{2}{\gamma}+\tfrac{2}{\gamma(c+\gamma)}\Big)^{\!2}\mathbb{E}\big[\|\tilde{x}_{t}-x^{\star}(\theta_{t})\|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t}}\big]

(5)

\displaystyle+\ \tfrac{12C_{\mathrm{lin}}^{2}}{\gamma^{2}}\,\eta_{t}^{2}\ +\ 3C_{\mathrm{reg}}^{2}\,\gamma^{2}+\ C_{\mathrm{off}}(1+R_{v}^{2})\,\mathbb{E}\big[(1+\mathsf{D}_{t}^{2\mathsf{n}_{\theta x}})\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t}^{c}}\big],

where
C_{x},C_{\mathrm{lin}},C_{\mathrm{reg}}<\infty
are stability constants depending on the global smoothness bounds
and the normal spectral gap of
\nabla^{2}_{xx}g
on
\Theta
,
c>0
is the uniform normal spectral gap from

Section

2
, and
C_{\mathrm{off}}<\infty
depends on

C_{\theta x},D_{\theta x},\mathsf{n}_{\theta x}
from
4
.

The last term in (
5
) is the off-tube contribution: it is active only when the selected candidate lies
farther than
r(\gamma)
from
\mathcal{S}(\theta_{t})
. For ULA/Gibbs candidates, the tube-concentration estimates in
Appendix
E.3
show that this term is exponentially small in
r(\gamma)^{2}/\lambda
.

It remains to control the selected-point error

\mathbb{E}\|\tilde{x}_{t}-x^{\star}(\theta_{t})\|^{2}
in the first term of (
5
). This is the role of the
next lemma: small
\lambda
keeps ULA/Gibbs candidates close to
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
, Best-of-
N
selection chooses the
candidate with the smallest upper-level value, and the Rényi tolerance
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}
measures the error
from using finite-step ULA rather than exact Gibbs samples.

Lemma 5.3

(Expected squared selection error for parallel ULA candidates)
.

Fix
\theta\in\Theta
and let
x^{\star}(\theta)
denote the unique minimizer of
f(\theta,\cdot)
over

\mathcal{S}(\theta)
. Assume
1
,
3
,
4
and
2
hold,
\texttt{k}\geq 1
, and

0<\lambda\leq\lambda_{0}
. Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{N}
be the outputs of
N
independent finite-step ULA chains targeting

\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}
, let
\nu_{i}
be the marginal law of
X_{i}
, and write

\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}:=\max_{1\leq i\leq N}R_{2}(\nu_{i}\|\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda})
. Assume

\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}\leq 1
, and let
\tilde{x}\in\arg\min_{1\leq i\leq N}f(\theta,X_{i})
be the hard-selection output,
with a measurable tie-breaking rule.
5
5

5

For example, one may choose the smallest index among all candidates attaining
the minimum value of
f(\theta,X_{i})
, and set
\tilde{x}
to that candidate.
Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|\tilde{x}-x^{\star}(\theta)\|^{2}\ \leq\

\displaystyle 2e^{1/2}C_{\mathrm{tube},2}\,\lambda\log(1+N)

\displaystyle\quad+\ 2\Bigg(\frac{1}{c_{\mathrm{hg}}}+\frac{4\mathsf{D}^{2}}{\Delta_{r_{0}}}\Bigg)\Bigg(2L_{f,1}\,C_{\mathrm{tube}}\,\sqrt{\lambda\log(1+N)}+\ C_{1}\,N^{-1/\texttt{k}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\ 4\sqrt{e-1}\,L_{f,1}\sqrt{N\,C_{\mathrm{PI}}}\,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}\Bigg).

(6)

The constants
L_{f,1}
and
\mathsf{D}
are from
4
;

C_{\mathrm{PI}}
is the low-temperature Poincaré constant from the discussion in Appendix
A.2
;

C_{\mathrm{tube}}
,
C_{\mathrm{tube},2}
, and
\lambda_{0}
are from

Sections

E.3.2
and
E.3.2
;
C_{1}
is from
Section

E.3.3
;

c_{\mathrm{hg}}
and
r_{0}
are from
Section

E.3.4
; and
\Delta_{r_{0}}>0
is from

Section

E.3.4
.

The proofs of
Sections

5
and
5
are given in
Appendix
E.2
and Appendix
E.3
, respectively. Combining them gives the
following parameter-level bound.

Theorem 5.4

(Hyper-gradient error bound)
.

Let
1
,
3
,
4
and
2
hold. Suppose
\texttt{k}\geq 1
,
0<\lambda\leq\lambda_{0}
,
\lambda\log(1+N)\leq 1
,
0<\gamma\leq 1
, and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}\leq 1
, where

\lambda_{0}
is the low-temperature threshold from
Section

5
.
Choose the clipping radius
R_{v}
so that
R_{v}\geq(2/\gamma)(L_{f,1}+\eta_{t})
. We have, for all
t
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|e_{t}\|^{2}=\mathcal{O}\!\Bigg(

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{\lambda\log(1+N)}+N^{-1/\texttt{k}}+\sqrt{N\,C_{\mathrm{PI}}}\,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}+\eta_{t}^{2}}{\gamma^{2}}\ +\ \gamma^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\ +\ (1+R_{v}^{2})N\big(1+r(\gamma)^{2\mathsf{n}_{\theta x}}+\lambda^{\mathsf{n}_{\theta x}}\big)\exp\!\Big(-\tfrac{c_{\mathrm{tube}}}{4}\tfrac{r(\gamma)^{2}}{\lambda}\Big)\Bigg).

(7)

Here
N
is the number of parallel ULA candidates in
Algorithm

1
, and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}
denotes a uniform square-root Rényi tolerance:

\max_{i}R_{2}(\nu_{t,i}\|\mu_{\theta_{t}}^{\lambda})\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}
, where
\nu_{t,i}
is the conditional law of the
i
-th candidate.
The hidden constant in the full bound depends only on problem-data constants, not on

(N,\lambda,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}},\gamma,\eta_{t},R_{v})
or
t
. The proof appears in Appendix
E.4
.

Each term in
Theorem

5.4
has a distinct origin. The term
N^{-1/\texttt{k}}
is the finite-candidate
optimistic-selection error along the
k
-dimensional lower-level minimizer manifold. The terms

\sqrt{\lambda\log(1+N)}
and
\lambda\log(1+N)
come from low-temperature Gibbs concentration near

\mathcal{S}(\theta)
. The factor
\sqrt{N\,C_{\mathrm{PI}}}\,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}
is the price of using finite-step ULA
candidates instead of exact Gibbs samples, measured by the square-root Rényi tolerance
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}
.
The
\eta_{t}^{2}
term is the CG residual, while
\gamma^{2}
is the bias from replacing the pseudoinverse by a
ridge-regularized inverse. The final exponential term comes from bounding the last term in (
5
): the
event that the selected candidate lies outside the
r(\gamma)
-tube where the ridge stability argument applies.

Finally, we translate this error bound into one explicit oracle-complexity consequence. This is a worst-case
scaling for the analyzed ULA/Gibbs instantiation. We count evaluations of
\nabla_{x}g
used by ULA, evaluations of
f
used for hard selection,
and the Hessian–vector products used by CG; the number of ULA iterations is chosen so that the square-root Rényi
tolerance
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}
is sufficiently small.

Theorem 5.5

(Total oracle complexity (informal))
.

Let
1
,
3
,
4
and
2
hold and run
HG-MS
with
\alpha=1/L_{F}
.
Suppose
\texttt{k}\geq 1
.
Initialize every ULA chain from
\lambda
-scaled Gaussians (see
Section

E.3.5
).
For all sufficiently small
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, with the same parameter choices as in Appendix
E.4
ensuring
\sup_{t}\mathbb{E}\|e_{t}\|^{2}=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})
,

Theorems

5.4
and
5
yield an
\varepsilon
-stationarity guarantee after

T=\mathcal{O}\big(L_{F}(F(\theta_{0})-F_{\star})/\varepsilon^{2}\big)
outer iterations.
Moreover, under the explicit scaling
N=\lceil\varepsilon^{-4\texttt{k}}\rceil
,
\lambda=\varepsilon^{8}/\log(1+N)
,
the square-root Rényi tolerance
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}=\varepsilon^{4+2\texttt{k}}
,
\gamma=\varepsilon
,
\eta_{t}=\varepsilon^{2}
,
R_{v}=\Theta(\varepsilon^{-1})
, and

K=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\lambda^{-4}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}^{-2})
,
each outer iteration uses
NK
evaluations of
\nabla_{x}g
(ULA),
N
evaluations of
f
(hard selection), and
\#\mathrm{HVP}
Hessian–vector products for CG.
Hence the total oracle complexity for achieving an
\varepsilon
-stationary point is

T(NK+N+\#\mathrm{HVP})=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\Big(\varepsilon^{-42-8\texttt{k}}\Big).

Remark 5.6

(On the
k
-dependence)
.

The intrinsic dimension enters through the minima-selection subproblem, most visibly via the
N^{-1/\texttt{k}}
approximation term in
Theorem

5.4
.
This matches the worst-case intuition that optimizing over a
k
-dimensional nonconvex minimizer manifold can reduce to
k
-dimensional global optimization and exhibit
\Omega(\varepsilon^{-\texttt{k}})
-type hardness
(
48
, Rem. 1.1)
.

6
Empirical Evaluations

Overview.

We study minima selection for hyper-gradients across LLM source-reweighting and MNIST
(
37
)
benchmarks. The main text reports both controlled two-source GPT-2 reweighting and real-world LLM
data-composition benchmarks; the two MNIST benchmarks are reported in Appendix
F.2
(data hyper-cleaning)
and Appendix
F.3
(imbalanced loss tuning). The two LLM subsections below state the corresponding
source-reweighting bilevel objectives explicitly.
In this section,
HG-MS
refers to practical finite-budget implementations of the same algorithmic template as the analyzed method.
While the convergence analysis is based on the ULA/Gibbs instantiation of
HG-MS
, the LLM experiments use a small number of finite-budget lower-level optimizer branches, implemented with SGD or AdamW, instead of ULA samples.
The main LLM comparisons use a common implementation and matched downstream fine-tuning budgets. We compare against the vanilla hyper-gradient baseline,
HG-baseline
,
ScaleBiO

(
56
)
,
LESS

(
71
)
, and
RHO-LOSS

(
50
)
.

6.1
Controlled LLM source reweighting

We first test the source-reweighting mechanism in the controlled two-source GPT-2 tasks adapted from

56
.

Bilevel formulation.

Let
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{tr}}_{1},\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{tr}}_{2}
be the two training sources and let

\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{val}}
be the target validation set. The outer variable is a two-dimensional source-logit vector

\theta
, with source weights
\omega=\operatorname{softmax}(\theta)
, and the lower variable
y
is the full trainable GPT-2
(124M) parameter state. With token-level next-token loss
\ell(y;\xi)
, the controlled source-reweighting objective is

g_{\mathrm{ctrl}}(\theta,y):=\sum_{s=1}^{2}\omega_{s}\,|\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{tr}}_{s}|^{-1}\sum_{\xi\in\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{tr}}_{s}}\ell(y;\xi),\qquad f_{\mathrm{ctrl}}(y):=|\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{val}}|^{-1}\sum_{\xi\in\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{val}}}\ell(y;\xi).

Thus
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{ctrl}}(\theta)=\arg\min_{y}g_{\mathrm{ctrl}}(\theta,y)
is the set of lower-level GPT-2 states
obtained under the current source mixture, and the optimistic upper objective is

F_{\mathrm{ctrl}}(\theta)=\min_{y\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{ctrl}}(\theta)}f_{\mathrm{ctrl}}(y)
. In practice, the algorithms
approximate
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{ctrl}}(\theta)
with finite lower-level optimizer runs;
HG-MS
selects the candidate
state with the smallest validation loss before forming the outer update. Unlike the real-world LLM experiments below, this
controlled code path trains the full GPT-2 trainable parameter state rather than LoRA adapters;
Appendix
F.5
gives the full protocol and hyperparameters.

Table

1
reports two tasks. In
denoising
, the sources are clean Alpaca examples and synthetically
corrupted Alpaca examples, so the target source mixture is
\omega^{\star}=(1,0)
. In
multilingual training
, the
sources are Alpaca-GPT4-ZH and Alpaca-GPT4-EN, with validation/test composition

\omega^{\star}=(0.6,0.4)
. We report learned source weights, source-weight recovery error, validation loss, and test
loss. On denoising, all reweighting methods improve over
Uniform
;
HG-MS
gives the best test loss and
the best clean/noisy recovery. On multilingual training,
HG-MS
gives the best validation and test losses, while

ScaleBiO
is closest to the nominal source ratio. Since the bilevel objective optimizes held-out loss rather than
source-ratio recovery, the multilingual result shows that minima selection can favor branches with better validation
behavior even when the learned mixture is not closest to
\omega^{\star}
.

and

6.2
Real-world LLM applications

We test the source-reweighting template on the LLM data-composition benchmarks of
56
: mathematical reasoning and instruction following. These experiments use multi-source LoRA fine-tuning portfolios; Appendix
F.6

gives the task definitions, source inventories, budgets, inference protocol, and judging details. Here we summarize the
matched-budget outcomes.

Bilevel formulation.

Both real-world benchmarks use the multi-source version of the controlled formulation above. Let
p
be the number of
training sources, let
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
be source logits, let
\omega=\operatorname{softmax}(\theta)\in\Delta^{p-1}
, and let
y

denote the LoRA adapter parameters for a fixed base model. With source datasets

\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{tr}}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{tr}}_{p}
and held-out validation set

\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{val}}
, the reweighting stage solves the bilevel problem induced by

g_{\mathrm{rw}}(\theta,y):=\sum_{s=1}^{p}\omega_{s}\,|\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{tr}}_{s}|^{-1}\sum_{\xi\in\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{tr}}_{s}}\ell(y;\xi),\qquad f_{\mathrm{rw}}(y):=|\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{val}}|^{-1}\sum_{\xi\in\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{val}}}\ell_{\mathrm{val}}(y;\xi),

namely
F_{\mathrm{rw}}(\theta)=\min_{y\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{rw}}(\theta)}f_{\mathrm{rw}}(y)
with

\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{rw}}(\theta)=\arg\min_{y}g_{\mathrm{rw}}(\theta,y)
. Here
\ell
is the token-level next-token
cross-entropy on source examples, and
\ell_{\mathrm{val}}
is the same loss on validation examples used only during
source reweighting. After reweighting, the learned
\omega
is converted into source counts for a final one-epoch
supervised fine-tuning run, and the resulting checkpoint is evaluated on the task metrics reported below (GSM8K/MATH
accuracy or MT-Bench score).

Mathematical reasoning benchmark.

Following
(
56
, Section 4.2.2)
, this benchmark uses a ten-source portfolio and evaluates the final fine-tuned checkpoint on GSM8K and MATH. All methods share the source portfolio, final one-epoch fine-tuning stage, and seed.

7
Limitations and Future Directions

The main theoretical contribution of
Section

3
is an exact-selection regularity result for optimistic bilevel
optimization with manifold lower-level solution sets. Under local
PŁ
∘
, the lower-level minimizers form a structured
manifold and the Hessian of
g
has a normal spectral gap. We show that this non-singleton regime still admits a
differentiable optimistic hyper-objective when the optimistic selection is unique, and that intrinsic non-degeneracy of the
selected point yields local smoothness and the pseudoinverse hyper-gradient formula. These assumptions mark the clean
smooth regime for exact optimistic selection: uniqueness prevents branch switching, non-degeneracy stabilizes the
restricted minimizer on
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
, and local
PŁ
∘
implies the geometry needed to differentiate through the
moving solution set. A natural theoretical direction is to develop analogous exact-selection results under other
geometric descriptions of the lower-level landscape, while preserving the same principle that regularity is governed by
the stability of the selected lower-level solution.

Sections

4
and
5
turn this exact formula into a select-then-differentiate algorithm. The analyzed version of

HG-MS
uses ULA/Gibbs candidates because this gives a mathematically controlled candidate distribution near

\mathcal{S}(\theta)
. The resulting oracle-complexity bound is therefore best viewed as a worst-case existence guarantee for
this analyzed instantiation, not as a practical runtime model. Its exponent is conservative because the proof separately
uses low-temperature sampling, Rényi accuracy of finite-step ULA, finite-candidate selection, and the stabilized
pseudoinverse solve. The dependence on the intrinsic dimension
k
in the selection term is unavoidable in the worst
case, since selecting the best point on a
k
-dimensional nonconvex minimizer manifold can be reduced to a

k
-dimensional global optimization problem. The larger dimension-independent exponent comes from combining several
uniform worst-case controls in a single end-to-end guarantee. Improving this part of the rate would require new
techniques that preserve the present selection-error and hyper-gradient-stability mechanisms while coupling the sampling,
selection, and linear-solve accuracies more tightly.

The experiments use the same selection principle with practical finite-budget lower-level branches. In the LLM
source-reweighting tasks, each branch performs only one lower-level SGD update because the lower-level model is large and
source reweighting must be run under a fixed wall-clock budget; in the MNIST experiments, the branches use longer
AdamW/SGD lower-level runs. This practical version should not be understood as trying to sample the full Gibbs measure.
Rather, the branch distribution is induced by initialization, minibatch randomness, optimizer state, perturbations, and the
finite training budget. A promising next step is a candidate-generator-centered theory: if
\nu_{\theta,B}
denotes the
law of a
B
-step optimizer branch, one can study when
\nu_{\theta,B}
places enough mass on lower-level candidates
with strong upper-level validation performance. Then
BoN
selection amplifies this validation-good mass across branches.
Such a theory would connect the exact optimistic hyper-gradient picture developed here with the finite-time optimizer
dynamics used in large-scale bilevel applications.

Appendix

Appendix A
Technical Preliminaries and Regularity Assumptions

A.1
Appendix notation guide

This subsection supplements the notation paragraph in the introduction. It records mathematical notation used repeatedly
in the appendix proofs.

Distance, balls, and spheres.

For a nonempty set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we write

\mathrm{dist}(x,A):=\inf_{y\in A}\|x-y\|.

We denote the closure and interior of
A
by
\overline{A}
and
\mathrm{int}(A)
, respectively.
For
a\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
r\geq 0
,
\mathbb{B}_{d}(a;r):=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|x-a\|\leq r\}
denotes the closed Euclidean ball.
For
q\geq 1
,
\mathbb{S}^{q-1}:=\{u\in\mathbb{R}^{q}:\|u\|=1\}
denotes the unit sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{q}
.

Manifold volume and geodesic distance.

If
\mathcal{M}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
is an
r
-dimensional embedded submanifold, then

\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}_{\mathcal{M}}(y)
denotes the Riemannian volume element induced by the ambient Euclidean metric, and

\mathrm{Vol}_{r}(\mathcal{M}):=\int_{\mathcal{M}}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}_{\mathcal{M}}(y)

denotes its total
r
-dimensional volume. We write
d_{\mathcal{M}}(x,y)
for the geodesic distance between

x,y\in\mathcal{M}
.

Riemannian second-order notation.

For a smooth scalar function
h
on
\mathcal{M}
,
\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{M}}h(x)
denotes the Riemannian Hessian
of
h
at
x
. When
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{S}(\theta)
,
\mathrm{II}_{x}^{\theta}
denotes the second fundamental form of

\mathcal{S}(\theta)
at
x
.

Lower-level objective shorthand.

We write

g_{\star}(\theta):=\min_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}g(\theta,z).

The common intrinsic dimension of
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
is denoted by
k
. In normal-coordinate arguments, we write

q:=d-\texttt{k}
for the normal dimension.

Linear algebra.

For a matrix
A
,
\ker(A)
denotes its null space and
A^{\dagger}
denotes its Moore–Penrose pseudoinverse. If

A
is symmetric,
\lambda_{\min}(A)
denotes its smallest eigenvalue. We write
\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}
for the
operator norm.

Asymptotic notation.

We use
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
for upper bounds up to problem-dependent constants that are independent of the displayed
scaling parameters. We use
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\cdot)
to additionally suppress logarithmic factors.

Probability measures.

For
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\delta_{x}
denotes the Dirac measure at
x
. Given candidates
X_{1},\ldots,X_{M}
, their empirical
measure is

\widehat{\nu}_{M}:=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\delta_{X_{i}}.

The Gibbs measure is

\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}x)\propto\exp\{-g(\theta,x)/\lambda\}\,\mathrm{d}x.

The marginal laws of approximate ULA candidates are denoted by
\nu_{i}
.

Lower-tail notation.

For a real random variable
Z
, its lower
u
-quantile is

q_{u}(Z):=\inf\{r\in\mathbb{R}:\mathbb{P}(Z\leq r)\geq u\}.

The lower-tail superquantile
\mathrm{SQ}^{\mathrm{low}}_{\delta}(Z)
is defined in
Section

E.3
.

Selected candidates and tube events.

For candidates
X_{1},\ldots,X_{M}
, the hard-selected point is

\tilde{x}\in\arg\min_{1\leq i\leq M}f(\theta,X_{i}).

We write
\bar{x}\in\arg\min_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)}\|x-\tilde{x}\|
for a Euclidean projection of
\tilde{x}
onto

\mathcal{S}(\theta)
. When each candidate is projected separately,
\Pi(X_{i})
denotes a measurable choice from

\arg\min_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)}\|x-X_{i}\|
. At outer iteration
t
,

\tilde{x}_{t}:=\hat{x}_{N}^{\lambda}(\theta_{t}),\qquad\mathsf{D}_{t}:=\mathrm{dist}(\tilde{x}_{t},\mathcal{S}(\theta_{t})),\qquad\mathcal{E}_{t}:=\{\mathsf{D}_{t}\leq r(\gamma)\}.

We write
\mathbf{1}_{A}
for the indicator of an event or set
A
.

A.2
ULA sampling and Poincaré preliminaries

For completeness, we record the standard unadjusted Langevin algorithm (ULA) used as the candidate generator in the
ULA/Gibbs instantiation of
HG-MS
; see, e.g.,
10
.

Algorithm 2

ULA
(\theta,g,x^{(0)},\lambda,K,h)

1:

\theta
; lower objective
g(\theta,\cdot)
; initial state
x^{(0)}
;
temperature
\lambda
; steps
K
; stepsize
h
.

2:

x\leftarrow x^{(0)}
.

3:

for

s=0
to
K-1

do

4:

Draw
\xi^{(s)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
.

5:

x\leftarrow x-h\nabla_{x}g(\theta,x)+\sqrt{2\lambda h}\,\xi^{(s)}
.

6:

return

x
.

Poincaré constant.

A probability measure
\mu
over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is said to satisfy the Poincaré inequality with constant
C_{\mathrm{PI}}
if, for every test function
\varphi\in H^{1}(\mu)
,

\int\Bigl(\varphi-\int\varphi\,\mathrm{d}\mu\Bigr)^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu\;\leq\;C_{\mathrm{PI}}\int\|\nabla\varphi\|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\mu,

where
H^{1}(\mu)
denotes the Sobolev space weighted by
\mu
. Importantly, for Gibbs measures of the form
\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}x)\propto\exp(-g(\theta,x)/\lambda)\,\mathrm{d}x
,
28
show that under
PŁ
∘
the corresponding Poincaré constant admits a bound independent of
\lambda
for all sufficiently small
\lambda\leq\lambda_{0}
. Accordingly, when
C_{\mathrm{PI}}
appears later in our sampling and complexity bounds, it refers to this low-temperature uniform bound inherited from
PŁ
∘
.

A.3
Regularity assumptions for sampling and selection

The assumptions in this subsection are used for the finite-time convergence and complexity analysis in

Section

5
; they are not needed for the local differentiability statements in
Section

3
.
Their roles are as follows.

3
gives uniform third-derivative control of
g(\theta,\cdot)
in a fixed tube around

\mathcal{S}(\theta)
. In
3
,
\rho
denotes the radius of this fixed tube and
L_{g,3}
denotes the
uniform third-derivative bound in the tube. This is used in two places: first, to obtain the explicit tube radius

r(\gamma)=\min\left\{\rho,\frac{\gamma}{2\max\{L_{g,3},1\}}\right\}

for ridge invertibility in
Section

E.2
; and second, through the second-fundamental-form bound in

Section

E.3.1
, to justify the curvature/volume control used in the nearest-neighbor part of the
selection analysis.

4
collects the global analytic controls needed because the ULA and Gibbs candidates are not
compactly supported: smoothness and Lipschitz bounds make the hyper-gradient stability constants finite; polynomial
growth of the derivative quantities
\partial_{\theta}g
and
\nabla^{2}_{\theta x}g
gives finite moments and controls
the off-tube term in
Section

E.2
; and the coercive quadratic-growth condition gives well-defined
low-temperature Gibbs measures and Gaussian-type tube tails in
Section

E.3.2
. The global Lipschitz
condition on
f
already implies the linear growth of
f
needed for tail estimates, so no separate polynomial-growth
assumption on
f
is imposed. Finally, the global intrinsic non-degeneracy in

2
yields the uniform restricted-curvature constant
m_{\mathrm{R}}
in

Section

A.3
. This constant is used only in the selection-error proof:

Sections

E.3.4
,
E.3.4
and
E.3.4
convert a small validation-value gap on the manifold into a
small distance to
x^{\star}(\theta)
.

Our sharp selection-rate analysis also uses volume comparison bounds for small geodesic balls on the compact manifolds

\mathcal{S}(\theta)
, which require the curvature control supplied by
3
.

Assumption 3

(regularity of
g
)
.

There exist constants
\rho>0
and
L_{g,3}<\infty
such that for all
\theta\in\Theta
,

g(\theta,\cdot)
is
\mathcal{C}^{3}
on the tube
\{x:\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{S}(\theta))\leq\rho\}
and

\sup_{\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{S}(\theta))\leq\rho}\ \big\|\nabla^{3}_{xxx}g(\theta,x)\big\|_{\mathrm{op}}\ \leq\ L_{g,3}.

Assumption 4
.

The following statements hold for all
\theta\in\Theta
.

\bullet

Regularity of
f
.

f
is
\mathcal{C}^{2}
with respect to
(\theta,x)
. Moreover,
f
is
L_{f,1}
-Lipschitz and
L_{f,2}
-smooth with respect to
(\theta,x)
.
Since
\Theta
is compact, this global Lipschitz condition implies the derived linear-growth bound

|f(\theta,x)|\leq C(1+\|x\|)
, uniformly over
\theta\in\Theta
.

\bullet

Regularity of
g
.

g
is
\mathcal{C}^{3}
with respect to
(\theta,x)
.
For every
\theta
, the map
x\mapsto g(\theta,x)
is
\mathcal{C}^{2}
and
L_{g,2}
-smooth (i.e.,
\nabla_{x}g(\theta,\cdot)
is
L_{g,2}
-Lipschitz).
Moreover, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\|\partial_{\theta}g(\theta,x)\|\;\leq\;C_{g}\|x\|^{\mathsf{n}_{g}}+D_{g},

for constants
C_{g},D_{g}\geq 0
and an integer
\mathsf{n}_{g}\geq 1
.
We also assume polynomial growth of the mixed derivative used in the hyper-gradient:

\|\nabla^{2}_{\theta x}g(\theta,x)\|\;\leq\;C_{\theta x}\|x\|^{\mathsf{n}_{\theta x}}+D_{\theta x},

for constants
C_{\theta x},D_{\theta x}\geq 0
and an integer
\mathsf{n}_{\theta x}\geq 0
.
The Hessian
\partial_{x}^{2}g(\theta,x)
is continuous with respect to
(\theta,x)
.
Moreover, there exist constants
\mathsf{D}>0
and
\mu_{\mathsf{qg}}>0
such that for all
\|x\|\geq\mathsf{D}
,

\frac{\mu_{\mathsf{qg}}}{2}\,\mathrm{dist}^{2}\!\bigl(x,\mathcal{S}(\theta)\bigr)\;\leq\;g(\theta,x)-\min_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}g(\theta,z).

W.l.o.g. we take
\mathsf{D}
large enough so that
\mathcal{S}(\theta)\subseteq\mathbb{B}_{d}(0;\mathsf{D})
for all
\theta\in\Theta
.

Proposition A.1

(Uniform restricted curvature from global non-degeneracy)
.

Let
f\in\mathcal{C}^{2}
,
g\in\mathcal{C}^{3}
, and suppose
1
and
2
hold.
For every
\theta\in\Theta
, let

\bar{f}_{\theta}:\mathcal{S}(\theta)\to\mathbb{R},\qquad\bar{f}_{\theta}(x):=f(\theta,x),

denote the restriction of the upper-level objective to the lower-level solution manifold.
There exists a constant
m_{\mathrm{R}}>0
, independent of
\theta
, such that

\big\langle v,\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{S}(\theta)}\bar{f}_{\theta}(x^{\star}(\theta))[v]\big\rangle\ \geq\ m_{\mathrm{R}}\|v\|^{2},\qquad\forall\theta\in\Theta,\ \forall v\in\mathcal{T}_{x^{\star}(\theta)}^{\theta}.

Proof.

If
\texttt{k}=0
, the tangent spaces are trivial and the claim is vacuous; take any
m_{\mathrm{R}}>0
.
Assume
\texttt{k}\geq 1
. For each
\bar{\theta}\in\Theta
,
Theorem

3.6
and
2
give a
neighborhood
U_{\bar{\theta}}
, a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
selected branch
x^{\star}(\theta)
, and a
\mathcal{C}^{2}
local chart

\psi_{\bar{\theta}}(\theta,u)
for
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
near
x^{\star}(\theta)
. Let
u^{\star}(\theta)
be the local
coordinate satisfying
x^{\star}(\theta)=\psi_{\bar{\theta}}(\theta,u^{\star}(\theta))
, and write

\tilde{f}_{\bar{\theta}}(\theta,u):=f(\theta,\psi_{\bar{\theta}}(\theta,u)),\qquad A_{\bar{\theta}}(\theta):=D_{u}\psi_{\bar{\theta}}(\theta,u^{\star}(\theta)).

For a tangent vector
v=A_{\bar{\theta}}(\theta)w
, the second variation of the pullback equals the Riemannian Hessian
quadratic form, while
\|v\|^{2}=w^{\top}A_{\bar{\theta}}(\theta)^{\top}A_{\bar{\theta}}(\theta)w
. Hence the local restricted
curvature can be written as the smallest generalized eigenvalue

\mu_{\bar{\theta}}(\theta):=\min_{w\neq 0}\frac{w^{\top}\nabla^{2}_{uu}\tilde{f}_{\bar{\theta}}(\theta,u^{\star}(\theta))w}{w^{\top}A_{\bar{\theta}}(\theta)^{\top}A_{\bar{\theta}}(\theta)w}.

This quantity is continuous on
U_{\bar{\theta}}
and is positive at every point by the intrinsic non-degeneracy in

2
. Shrinking
U_{\bar{\theta}}
if necessary, there is a smaller neighborhood

V_{\bar{\theta}}
with
\overline{V_{\bar{\theta}}}\subset U_{\bar{\theta}}
on which

\inf_{\theta\in V_{\bar{\theta}}}\mu_{\bar{\theta}}(\theta)>0
.
Since
\Theta
is compact, choose a finite subcover
V_{\bar{\theta}_{1}},\ldots,V_{\bar{\theta}_{J}}
and set

m_{\mathrm{R}}:=\min_{1\leq j\leq J}\inf_{\theta\in V_{\bar{\theta}_{j}}}\mu_{\bar{\theta}_{j}}(\theta)>0.

This gives the stated uniform lower bound.
∎

Section

A.3
is the uniform version of the local intrinsic non-degeneracy condition in

Section

3
. The Hessian of the restricted objective includes the curvature of the constraint
manifold, as shown by the identity below.
With the convention for the second fundamental form used in
Section

E.3.1
, for
v\in\mathcal{T}_{x^{\star}(\theta)}^{\theta}
,

\big\langle v,\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{S}(\theta)}\bar{f}_{\theta}(x^{\star}(\theta))[v]\big\rangle=\langle v,\nabla^{2}_{xx}f(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))v\rangle+\langle\nabla_{x}f(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta)),\mathrm{II}_{x^{\star}(\theta)}^{\theta}(v,v)\rangle.

(8)

Here
\mathrm{II}_{x^{\star}(\theta)}^{\theta}
denotes the second fundamental form of the embedded manifold
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
at
x^{\star}(\theta)
.
Thus the conclusion of
Section

A.3
holds, for instance, if the ambient tangent Hessian has a uniform
positive margin and the second-fundamental-form correction is not too negative.
Concretely, it is sufficient that for some
m_{\mathrm{amb}}>0
and
\kappa<m_{\mathrm{amb}}
,

\langle v,\nabla^{2}_{xx}f(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))v\rangle\geq m_{\mathrm{amb}}\|v\|^{2},\qquad\langle\nabla_{x}f(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta)),\mathrm{II}_{x^{\star}(\theta)}^{\theta}(v,v)\rangle\geq-\kappa\|v\|^{2}

uniformly over
\theta\in\Theta
and
v\in\mathcal{T}_{x^{\star}(\theta)}^{\theta}
.
Using
Section

E.3.1
, a further sufficient condition is

m_{\mathrm{amb}}>\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\|\nabla_{x}f(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))\|\frac{L_{g,3}}{c},

where
c
is the uniform normal spectral gap in
Section

2
.

We close this subsection by comparing these assumptions with common assumptions in related analyses.
The smoothness and Lipschitz-type parts of
4
are standard in hyper-gradient analyses of bilevel
optimization: they play the same role as the uniform derivative bounds used to control implicit-gradient stability and
inexact lower-level solves
[
36
,
9
]
. Many bilevel convergence results additionally avoid
tail issues by imposing a bounded lower-level domain, bounded gradients, or bounded iterates. Our sampling-based method
does not have compactly supported candidates, because the Gibbs and ULA laws live on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We therefore replace those
bounded-domain requirements with derivative polynomial-growth bounds, the linear-growth consequence of Lipschitzness of

f
, and coercive quadratic growth away from
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
. This is the standard type of tail-control condition
used in low-temperature Gibbs/Langevin analyses around minimizer sets

[
29
,
10
]
. It is weaker than assuming all relevant quantities are bounded on

\mathbb{R}^{d}
, but strong enough to make the Gibbs normalizing constants finite, to prove tube concentration, and to control
the off-tube contribution in the hyper-gradient error.

The manifold-specific assumptions have a different purpose.
3
is a quantitative version of local
regularity near the minimizer manifold; it is analogous to the Hessian-Lipschitz or third-order smoothness assumptions
often used when one needs stable local quadratic models, but here it is imposed only in a tube around
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
.
Together with the normal spectral gap from
PŁ
∘
, it provides the uniform curvature bounds required for the
manifold-volume and nearest-neighbor estimates used in the selection analysis; related manifold Gibbs estimates appear in

48
. Finally,
Section

A.3
is the manifold analogue of a uniform
second-order sufficient condition for the upper-level restricted problem
f(\theta,\cdot)|_{\mathcal{S}(\theta)}
. This
conclusion is vacuous in the classical singleton-minimizer regime, but in the non-singleton manifold regime it is what
converts validation-value suboptimality into distance to the unique optimistic selection. Thus the additional assumptions
in this subsection are not substitutes for the local differentiability theory in
Section

3
; they are uniform,
quantitative conditions needed for the finite-sample
BoN
selection rate and the resulting oracle-complexity bound.

Appendix B
Related Work

Bilevel optimization has a long history in mathematical programming; see, e.g.,

68
,
19
,
12
,
47
,
18
.
Classical optimistic and pessimistic formulations already recognize that nonunique lower-level solutions create a genuine

selection
issue
[
16
,
76
,
73
]
. Standard single-level reformulations, whether via
value functions or KKT/MPEC systems
[
75
,
74
,
17
,
77
]
, provide an
important optimization viewpoint. Our focus here is a different question: when does the induced hyper-objective admit a
usable hyper-gradient?

Classical hyper-gradient methods.

Most classical hyper-gradient methods study the regime in which the lower-level minimizer is a singleton, so the
lower-level response can be treated as a single-valued and differentiable map of
\theta
, typically because their
assumptions (such as strong convexity) guarantee that the relevant lower-level Hessian is invertible. In this regime, the
hyper-objective
F(\theta)=f(\theta,x(\theta))
is differentiated exactly by implicit differentiation, while truncated
backpropagation is used to approximate its hyper-gradient by differentiating through finitely many lower-level iterations

[
57
,
24
,
45
,
62
]
. Closely
related methodological work studies how to differentiate through parameterized optimization problems more generally.

1
analyze gradients of functions defined as minima and compare analytic implicit gradients with
automatic differentiation through iterative solvers.
2
develop amortized inexact implicit
differentiation for stochastic bilevel optimization with strongly convex inner problems.
6
