develop a general framework for implicit differentiation of parameterized optimization problems: given an optimality system defining a single-valued solution map, they differentiate the solution by applying the implicit function theorem to that system. Their focus is modular implicit-differentiation machinery rather than minima selection from a non-singleton lower-level solution set.
These works are closely related in technique, but they still largely assume a well-defined single-valued solution map, and therefore do not address the minima-selection issue that arises when the lower-level minimizer set is non-singleton.

Degenerate implicit differentiation and pseudoinverse formulas.

A separate line studies bilevel differentiation when the lower-level Hessian is singular. Most relevant for us,

3
study bilevel games in which the lower-level problem may have multiple critical points and a
selection map chooses the particular critical point reached by a prescribed lower-level process (e.g., gradient flow).
They show that, under a parametric Morse–Bott condition, this algorithmic critical-point selection map remains
differentiable near local minima even when the lower Hessian is singular, and its derivative is characterized by a
pseudoinverse-based implicit-differentiation formula.
In a different overparameterized setting,
69
,
70
consider lower-level objectives
with nonunique minimizers and study the minimum-norm lower-level solution selected by the optimization dynamics. Because
this selected solution is defined through a singular linear system, its response Jacobian is characterized by the
minimum-norm solution of that system, which is expressed using the Moore–Penrose pseudoinverse.

The pseudoinverse formulas in these works are technically related to ours, but the object being differentiated is
different.
3
differentiate a prescribed algorithmic critical-point selection map, and the
overparameterized line
[
69
,
70
]
differentiates a minimum-norm or algorithm-induced
lower-level response. In contrast, we differentiate the exact optimistic value

F(\theta)=\min_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)}f(\theta,x),

where
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
is the manifold of global lower-level minimizers and the selected point is determined by the
upper-level objective itself. Thus our contribution is to show that, under local
PŁ
∘
and local uniqueness of the
optimistic minimizer, this hard optimistic value function is differentiable and its exact hyper-gradient admits a
pseudoinverse formula.

Alternative formulations, selection-based models, and nonsmooth bilevel objectives.

Several recent works address nonconvex or set-valued lower levels by modifying either the object being optimized or the
solution concept.
43
propose a trajectory-based approximation framework for nonconvex followers,
introducing an initialization auxiliary and a pessimistic trajectory truncation rule so that the upper-level method
optimizes through finite lower-level dynamics rather than through a globally solved lower-level problem.
3

introduce bilevel games with critical-point selection maps, where the lower-level object is a selected critical point
reached by a prescribed optimization process, and they study how finite-budget unrolled methods relate to equilibria of
these selection-based games. Under a Morse-type parametric qualification,
7
show that the graphs of
lower-level critical points and local minima decompose into finitely many smooth branches and use this structure to define
branch-wise hyper-objectives and gradient methods along individual branches.
35
argue that,
for general nonconvex lower levels, the classical hyper-objective may be unsettled if global minimizers are unattainable,
and instead propose a correspondence-driven hyper-objective tied to the output of a prescribed lower-level algorithm,
together with Gaussian smoothing and projected stochastic-gradient guarantees.
8
take a
different nonsmooth route: rather than recovering classical differentiability, they introduce a set-smoothness condition
that yields weak convexity/concavity of the induced hyper-objective and enables computation of approximate Clarke
hyper-stationary points.

Another recent line develops Moreau-envelope single-level reformulations as an alternative to classical value-function and
KKT-based reductions.
4
derive directional necessary optimality conditions in this framework,

25
propose a difference-of-weakly-convex reformulation with a sequentially convergent algorithm, and

46
develop a two-sided smoothed primal–dual method for general nonconvex bilevel problems with KKT-type
convergence guarantees. Our focus is different from all of these directions: we retain the classical optimistic
hyper-objective
F(\theta)=\min_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)}f(\theta,x)
itself and ask when, despite the lower-level solution set
being manifold-valued, that object is differentiable and even locally smooth.

A recent sampling-based relaxation
[
48
]
replaces hard minima selection by a
superquantile–Gibbs surrogate and studies a zeroth-order method that optimizes the resulting nonsmooth Lipschitz
objective through sampling-based function-value information rather than exact hyper-gradients. That approach is designed
to circumvent the nondifferentiability of hard minima selection by changing the objective being optimized. The present
paper takes a different route: we keep the hard optimistic objective, prove classical
\mathcal{C}^{1}
regularity and
local smoothness under uniqueness and nondegeneracy of the optimistic minimizer, derive the corresponding exact
pseudoinverse hyper-gradient, and then build a practical select-then-differentiate algorithm with an end-to-end
stationarity guarantee. In this sense, the present paper can be viewed as a differentiable counterpart to that
relaxation-based, zeroth-order line.

Relaxing strong convexity through global regularity or convexity assumptions.

Recent works on bilevel optimization study nonconvex lower-level problems under
global
PŁ or closely related
uniform error-bound assumptions

[
9
,
36
,
72
,
33
,
63
,
39
]
. One attraction
of this line is that such global regularity helps guarantee a smooth and analytically tractable hyper-objective

[
36
,
9
]
. From the viewpoint of the lower-level solution set, however, these assumptions are
quite restrictive. For a
\mathcal{C}^{2}
lower-level objective satisfying a global PŁ condition, the minimizer set

\mathcal{S}(\theta)
can only be either a singleton or an unbounded subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}

[
13
,
48
]
. Therefore, once one adds the boundedness or coercivity assumptions
typically used in this literature, the lower-level problem collapses back to the singleton-minimizer regime, and bounded
non-singleton minimizer manifolds are excluded.

This restriction is also visible in the specific assumptions used by these papers.
33
assume a
nondegenerate lower Hessian at global minimizers, which effectively enforces local uniqueness;
39

explicitly assume that
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
is a singleton; and
36
impose a proximal error bound for
smooth
g
together with coercivity, which again yields a singleton solution

[
9
, cf. Prop. C.1]
. Other extensions obtain differentiability or algorithmic guarantees under
convexity of the lower-level objective in
x

[
72
,
63
]
; this allows non-strongly
convex followers, but it still restricts
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
to a convex geometry rather than the nonconvex compact
manifold-valued setting that motivates our work. A related direction is
8
, who study approximate Clarke hyper-stationarity via a new
set-smoothness property of the lower-level solution map. Their framework assumes, in particular, that for every

\theta
, the lower-level solution set
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
is nonempty, closed, and convex, together with a uniform
error-bound condition (equivalently, under smoothness, a global PŁ condition). Under these assumptions, they show that
the hyper-objective is weakly convex/concave and develop an algorithm for approximate Clarke hyper-stationarity. From the
perspective of lower-level geometry, this is still quite different from our setting: their analysis is tailored to
convex solution sets, whereas we study compact nonconvex manifold-valued minimizer sets under the local

PŁ
∘
condition.

By contrast, our analysis is based on the local
PŁ
∘
condition of
28
, which is specifically
designed to allow a connected compact set of global lower-level minimizers with
\mathcal{C}^{2}
manifold structure. This
regime remains rich enough to cover overparameterized deep-learning landscapes where connected minima have been observed

[
21
,
26
,
53
]
, and it keeps minima selection genuinely relevant rather
than collapsing the problem back to the singleton-minimizer case.

B.1
Comparison with penalty-based differentiability analyses

We compare our standing assumptions to penalty-based differentiability analyses such as
36
and
9
, which study the perturbed lower-level objective

h_{\sigma}(\theta,x)\;:=\;g(\theta,x)+\sigma f(\theta,x),\qquad\sigma>0.

What is assumed in penalty-based differentiability works.

A representative sufficient condition in this line of work is a
\sigma
-uniform
error-bound / PŁ-type regularity of
h_{\sigma}(\theta,\cdot)
in the lower-level variable
x
for all
\sigma\in[0,\sigma_{0}]
, with constants that do not degenerate as
\sigma\downarrow 0
.
For example,
36
assume a (small-error) proximal error-bound condition for
h_{\sigma}(\theta,\cdot)
that holds uniformly for all
\sigma\in[0,\sigma_{0}]
, together with coercivity and smoothness assumptions.
In the smooth unconstrained setting, such a proximal error bound is equivalent to a PŁ inequality
[
9
, Prop. C.1]
.
In particular, since
\sigma=0
is included, these assumptions enforce a PŁ / error-bound condition for
g(\theta,\cdot)
itself.

Implication: singleton lower-level minimizers.

For
\mathcal{C}^{2}
objectives, a global PŁ condition implies that the minimizer set is either a singleton or unbounded
[
13
]
.
Therefore, under standard assumptions that rule out unbounded minimizers (e.g., coercivity, or compactness of
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
), the lower-level minimizer must be unique
[
48
]
.
In this regime, the optimistic bilevel objective reduces to the classical singleton-minimizer setting, where minima-selection is redundant.
Several recent analyses in nonconvex bilevel optimization fall into this category (global PŁ / error bounds together with coercivity/compactness) and hence effectively assume a singleton lower-level minimizer; see, e.g.,
33
,
36
.
Other works enforce uniqueness more directly (without explicitly invoking global PŁ), e.g.,
39
.

Why our assumptions are weaker than
\sigma
-uniform local PŁ for
g+\sigma f
.

The key obstruction is already visible by testing a putative PŁ inequality on points of the minimizer manifold.
On
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
, the lower objective
g
is constant and
\nabla_{x}g=0
. Thus the value gap of

h_{\sigma}=g+\sigma f
between two manifold points is scaled by
\sigma
, while the gradient contribution from

f
is also scaled by
\sigma
. The following lemma shows that any PŁ constant on a neighborhood containing both
the optimistic point and a higher-value manifold point must therefore be
\mathcal{O}(\sigma)
.

Lemma B.1

(No
\sigma
-uniform local PŁ constant on neighborhoods containing a higher-value manifold point)
.

Fix
\theta
and let
x^{\star}\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)
be the unique optimistic minimizer.
Assume there exists
\bar{x}\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)
such that
f(\theta,\bar{x})>f(\theta,x^{\star})
(i.e.,
f(\theta,\cdot)
is not constant on
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
).
Let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be any neighborhood containing both
x^{\star}
and
\bar{x}
.
If
h_{\sigma}(\theta,\cdot)
satisfies a (local) PŁ inequality on
U
with constant
\mu_{\sigma}>0
, i.e.,

h_{\sigma}(\theta,x)-\inf_{y\in U}h_{\sigma}(\theta,y)\ \leq\ (2\mu_{\sigma})^{-1}\,\|\nabla_{x}h_{\sigma}(\theta,x)\|^{2}\qquad\forall x\in U,

then

\mu_{\sigma}\ \leq\ \frac{\sigma}{2}\cdot\frac{\|\nabla_{x}f(\theta,\bar{x})\|^{2}}{f(\theta,\bar{x})-f(\theta,x^{\star})}.

(9)

In particular,
\mu_{\sigma}=\mathcal{O}(\sigma)
as
\sigma\downarrow 0
, so no constant bounded away from
0
can hold
uniformly over
\sigma\in(0,\sigma_{0}]
on any such neighborhood.

Proof.

Fix such a neighborhood
U
and point
\bar{x}\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)\cap U
.
Since
x^{\star}\in U
, we have
\inf_{y\in U}h_{\sigma}(\theta,y)\leq h_{\sigma}(\theta,x^{\star})
, hence

h_{\sigma}(\theta,\bar{x})-h_{\sigma}(\theta,x^{\star})\ \leq\ h_{\sigma}(\theta,\bar{x})-\inf_{y\in U}h_{\sigma}(\theta,y).

Applying the PŁ inequality at
x=\bar{x}
and using that
\bar{x}\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)
(so
\nabla_{x}g(\theta,\bar{x})=0
and
g(\theta,\bar{x})=g(\theta,x^{\star})
) gives

\sigma\big(f(\theta,\bar{x})-f(\theta,x^{\star})\big)\ \leq\ (2\mu_{\sigma})^{-1}\,\|\sigma\nabla_{x}f(\theta,\bar{x})\|^{2},

which rearranges to (
9
).
∎

When
\texttt{k}>0
and
x^{\star}
is non-degenerate in the sense of

Section

3
, such higher-value points occur arbitrarily close to
x^{\star}
: along any nonzero
tangent direction, the restricted objective
f(\theta,\cdot)|_{\mathcal{S}(\theta)}
increases quadratically for sufficiently
small geodesic displacement. Thus the obstruction above is genuinely local in the non-singleton manifold regime.

Appendix C
Proof of
Section

3

This appendix provides the proofs for
Section

3
.

C.1
Proof of
Theorem

3.2

Example
3
shows that even when the optimistic minimizer is unique,
the selection map
\theta\mapsto x^{\star}(\theta)
can fail to be differentiable at degenerate points.
Nevertheless, uniqueness is still enough to ensure that the
value function

F(\theta)=\min_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)}f(\theta,x)
is differentiable, and when uniqueness holds in a neighborhood it is in fact
\mathcal{C}^{1}
.

The following is a standard envelope/Danskin lemma; see, e.g.,
15
and

55
. We provide the proof for completeness.

Lemma C.1

(A basic envelope/Danskin lemma)
.

Let
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
be an open neighborhood of
\theta_{0}
, let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
be compact, and let

\phi:V\times U\to\mathbb{R}
be continuous. Suppose that
\phi(\cdot,u)
is differentiable in
\theta
for all

u\in U
, and that
(\theta,u)\mapsto\nabla_{\theta}\phi(\theta,u)
is continuous on a neighborhood of

(\theta_{0},u_{0})
.
Define
F(\theta):=\min_{u\in U}\phi(\theta,u)
for
\theta\in V
.
If
\arg\min_{u\in U}\phi(\theta_{0},u)=\{u_{0}\}
, then
F
is differentiable at
\theta_{0}
and

\nabla F(\theta_{0})=\nabla_{\theta}\phi(\theta_{0},u_{0}).

Proof.

We first note that every local choice of minimizers converges to the unique minimizer at
\theta_{0}
.
Let
\theta_{n}\to\theta_{0}
and pick any minimizer
u_{n}\in\arg\min_{u\in U}\phi(\theta_{n},u)
.
By compactness of
U
, along a subsequence (not relabeled) we have
u_{n}\to\bar{u}\in U
.
For any fixed
u\in U
, optimality of
u_{n}
gives
\phi(\theta_{n},u_{n})\leq\phi(\theta_{n},u)
.
Taking
n\to\infty
and using continuity of
\phi
yields
\phi(\theta_{0},\bar{u})\leq\phi(\theta_{0},u)
for all
u\in U
,
so
\bar{u}\in\arg\min_{u\in U}\phi(\theta_{0},u)=\{u_{0}\}
.
Thus every convergent subsequence of
u_{n}
converges to
u_{0}
, hence
u_{n}\to u_{0}
.

We now prove Fréchet differentiability. Let
h\to 0
in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
with
\theta_{0}+h\in V
, and let

u_{h}\in\arg\min_{u\in U}\phi(\theta_{0}+h,u)
. By the first part,
u_{h}\to u_{0}
as
h\to 0
.
Set
a:=\nabla_{\theta}\phi(\theta_{0},u_{0})
. Optimality of
u_{h}
and
u_{0}
gives

\phi(\theta_{0}+h,u_{h})-\phi(\theta_{0},u_{h})\ \leq\ F(\theta_{0}+h)-F(\theta_{0})\ \leq\ \phi(\theta_{0}+h,u_{0})-\phi(\theta_{0},u_{0}).

It remains to show that both the lower and upper bounds have first-order expansion
\langle a,h\rangle+o(\|h\|)
.
The upper bound satisfies this immediately from differentiability of
\phi(\cdot,u_{0})
at
\theta_{0}
:

\phi(\theta_{0}+h,u_{0})-\phi(\theta_{0},u_{0})-\langle a,h\rangle=o(\|h\|).

For the lower bound, take
\|h\|
small enough so that

\{(\theta_{0}+sh,u_{h}):s\in[0,1]\}
lies in the neighborhood where
\nabla_{\theta}\phi
is continuous, and write

\phi(\theta_{0}+h,u_{h})-\phi(\theta_{0},u_{h})-\langle a,h\rangle=\int_{0}^{1}\big\langle\nabla_{\theta}\phi(\theta_{0}+sh,u_{h})-a,\ h\big\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s.

We claim that the integrand is uniformly
o(\|h\|)
. Indeed, since
u_{h}\to u_{0}
and

\theta_{0}+sh\to\theta_{0}
uniformly in
s\in[0,1]
, continuity of
\nabla_{\theta}\phi
at

(\theta_{0},u_{0})
gives

\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\big\|\nabla_{\theta}\phi(\theta_{0}+sh,u_{h})-a\big\|\to 0.

Consequently,

\left|\phi(\theta_{0}+h,u_{h})-\phi(\theta_{0},u_{h})-\langle a,h\rangle\right|\leq\|h\|\sup_{s\in[0,1]}\big\|\nabla_{\theta}\phi(\theta_{0}+sh,u_{h})-a\big\|=o(\|h\|).

Combining the upper and lower bounds yields

F(\theta_{0}+h)-F(\theta_{0})-\langle a,h\rangle=o(\|h\|),

which proves differentiability of
F
at
\theta_{0}
with gradient
a=\nabla_{\theta}\phi(\theta_{0},u_{0})
.
∎

Lemma
C.1
is the bridge from minimizing over the moving manifold
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
to
minimizing over fixed local coordinates: near
x_{0}
, points in
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
can be written as

\psi(\theta,u)
with
u
in a fixed neighborhood of
0\in\mathbb{R}^{\texttt{k}}
.

Lemma C.2

(Local parameter-dependent chart for the minimizer manifold)
.

Let
g\in\mathcal{C}^{3}
and let
1
hold. Fix

\theta_{0}\in\Theta
and
x_{0}\in\mathcal{S}(\theta_{0})
. Then, after shrinking the
neighborhood if needed, there exist neighborhoods
\mathcal{U}_{\theta}
of
\theta_{0}
,

\mathcal{U}_{u}
of
0\in\mathbb{R}^{\texttt{k}}
, and
\mathcal{W}
of
x_{0}
, together with a

\mathcal{C}^{2}
map

\psi:\mathcal{U}_{\theta}\times\mathcal{U}_{u}\to\mathcal{W},\qquad\psi(\theta_{0},0)=x_{0},

such that
u\mapsto\psi(\theta,u)
is an embedded chart and

\mathcal{S}(\theta)\cap\mathcal{W}=\{\psi(\theta,u):u\in\mathcal{U}_{u}\},\qquad\theta\in\mathcal{U}_{\theta}.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{0}:=\mathcal{T}_{x_{0}}^{\theta_{0}}
,
\mathcal{N}_{0}:=\mathcal{N}_{x_{0}}^{\theta_{0}}
, and choose orthonormal matrices

U_{\mathcal{T}_{0}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times\texttt{k}}
and
U_{\mathcal{N}_{0}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times(d-\texttt{k})}
spanning these spaces. For

(u,v)
near
(0,0)
, write
x(u,v):=x_{0}+U_{\mathcal{T}_{0}}u+U_{\mathcal{N}_{0}}v
and define

\Phi(\theta,u,v):=U_{\mathcal{N}_{0}}^{\top}\nabla_{x}g(\theta,x(u,v)).

By
Section

2
,
U_{\mathcal{N}_{0}}^{\top}\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{0},x_{0})U_{\mathcal{N}_{0}}
is positive definite.
The implicit function theorem therefore gives a
\mathcal{C}^{2}
map

h(\theta,u)
, defined for
(\theta,u)
near
(\theta_{0},0)
, such that

\Phi(\theta,u,h(\theta,u))=0
. Set

\psi(\theta,u):=x_{0}+U_{\mathcal{T}_{0}}u+U_{\mathcal{N}_{0}}h(\theta,u).

For each fixed
\theta
, this parametrizes the
k
-dimensional manifold

\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}(\theta):=\{x:\ U_{\mathcal{N}_{0}}^{\top}\nabla_{x}g(\theta,x)=0\}

near
x_{0}
. This last statement is local in the following precise sense. The IFT gives open neighborhoods

\mathcal{U}_{\theta}^{0}
of
\theta_{0}
,
\mathcal{U}_{u}^{0}
of
0\in\mathbb{R}^{\texttt{k}}
, and

\mathcal{U}_{v}^{0}
of
0\in\mathbb{R}^{d-\texttt{k}}
such that every solution of

U_{\mathcal{N}_{0}}^{\top}\nabla_{x}g(\theta,x_{0}+U_{\mathcal{T}_{0}}u+U_{\mathcal{N}_{0}}v)=0
with

(\theta,u,v)\in\mathcal{U}_{\theta}^{0}\times\mathcal{U}_{u}^{0}\times\mathcal{U}_{v}^{0}
is uniquely represented as

v=h(\theta,u)
. We now choose a connected open set
\mathcal{U}_{u}
containing
0
whose closure is contained in

\mathcal{U}_{u}^{0}
, shrink the parameter neighborhood to

\mathcal{U}_{\theta}\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{\theta}^{0}
, and choose an ambient neighborhood
\mathcal{W}
of
x_{0}

small enough so that every
x\in\mathcal{W}
can be written uniquely as

x=x_{0}+U_{\mathcal{T}_{0}}u+U_{\mathcal{N}_{0}}v
with
u\in\mathcal{U}_{u}^{0}
and
v\in\mathcal{U}_{v}^{0}
, and such that
the points of the graph over
\mathcal{U}_{u}
lie in
\mathcal{W}
. With these choices, the local piece of

\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}(\theta)
inside
\mathcal{W}
is exactly the graph:
for every
\theta\in\mathcal{U}_{\theta}
,

\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}(\theta)\cap\mathcal{W}=\{\psi(\theta,u):u\in\mathcal{U}_{u}\}.

Finally, since every point of
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
is a lower-level minimizer and hence satisfies the full first-order
condition
\nabla_{x}g(\theta,x)=0
, it satisfies the projected condition defining

\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}(\theta)
and therefore

\mathcal{S}(\theta)\cap\mathcal{W}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}(\theta)\cap\mathcal{W}
.

It remains to show that this inclusion is equality on a smaller neighborhood. By the fixed-dimension conclusion in

Section

2
and the local Hausdorff continuity of the solution manifolds

[
48
, Lem. 3.2]
, after shrinking
\mathcal{U}_{\theta}
and
\mathcal{W}
, for every

\theta\in\mathcal{U}_{\theta}
,

\dim\mathcal{S}(\theta)=\texttt{k}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{S}(\theta)\cap\mathcal{W}\neq\emptyset.

For such a
\theta
, define

Z_{\theta}:=\{u\in\mathcal{U}_{u}:\psi(\theta,u)\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)\}.

The set
Z_{\theta}
records which points of the graph for
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}(\theta)
are genuine lower-level
minimizers. It is nonempty by the local Hausdorff-continuity step above: since

\mathcal{S}(\theta)\cap\mathcal{W}\neq\emptyset
and

\mathcal{S}(\theta)\cap\mathcal{W}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}(\theta)\cap\mathcal{W}
, at least one point of

\mathcal{S}(\theta)\cap\mathcal{W}
has the form
\psi(\theta,u)
. It is closed relative to
\mathcal{U}_{u}
because

Z_{\theta}=\{u\in\mathcal{U}_{u}:\psi(\theta,u)\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)\}=\psi_{\theta}^{-1}(\mathcal{S}(\theta))\cap\mathcal{U}_{u},\qquad\psi_{\theta}(u):=\psi(\theta,u),

where
\psi_{\theta}
is continuous and
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
is compact, hence closed, by
Section

2
.

We next show that
Z_{\theta}
is open. Take
u_{1}\in Z_{\theta}
and set
x_{1}:=\psi(\theta,u_{1})
. Near
x_{1}
,
both
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}(\theta)
are embedded
k
-dimensional submanifolds, and we already
know that
\mathcal{S}(\theta)\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}(\theta)
. Consider the inclusion map

i:\mathcal{S}(\theta)\hookrightarrow\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}(\theta),\qquad i(x)=x.

This map is smooth because both manifolds are embedded submanifolds of the same ambient space. Its derivative at

x_{1}
is the identity map on tangent vectors:

Di(x_{1}):\mathcal{T}_{x_{1}}\mathcal{S}(\theta)\to\mathcal{T}_{x_{1}}\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}(\theta),\qquad v\mapsto v.

Since the two tangent spaces both have dimension
k
, this derivative is an isomorphism. The inverse function
theorem for manifolds therefore gives a neighborhood
\mathcal{O}_{S}
of
x_{1}
in
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
such that

i(\mathcal{O}_{S})
is an open neighborhood of
x_{1}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}(\theta)
. But
i
is just inclusion,
so
i(\mathcal{O}_{S})=\mathcal{O}_{S}\subseteq\mathcal{S}(\theta)
. Thus some neighborhood of
x_{1}
inside

\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}(\theta)
is contained in
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
. Pulling this neighborhood back through the chart

u\mapsto\psi(\theta,u)
, we obtain a neighborhood of
u_{1}
contained in
Z_{\theta}
. Since this argument works
for every
u_{1}\in Z_{\theta}
, each point of
Z_{\theta}
has a relative neighborhood in
\mathcal{U}_{u}
contained in

Z_{\theta}
. Thus
Z_{\theta}
is open in
\mathcal{U}_{u}
.

Now
Z_{\theta}
is nonempty and is both closed and open relative to
\mathcal{U}_{u}
. If

Z_{\theta}\neq\mathcal{U}_{u}
, then
Z_{\theta}
and
\mathcal{U}_{u}\setminus Z_{\theta}
would form a separation of

\mathcal{U}_{u}
: two nonempty disjoint relatively open sets whose union is
\mathcal{U}_{u}
. This contradicts the
connectedness of
\mathcal{U}_{u}
. Hence
Z_{\theta}=\mathcal{U}_{u}
. Therefore every point of the local graph

\{\psi(\theta,u):u\in\mathcal{U}_{u}\}
belongs to
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
. Together with the earlier inclusion

\mathcal{S}(\theta)\cap\mathcal{W}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}(\theta)\cap\mathcal{W}
, this gives

\mathcal{S}(\theta)\cap\mathcal{W}=\{\psi(\theta,u):u\in\mathcal{U}_{u}\},

after shrinking
\mathcal{W}
within the chart neighborhood if needed.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

3.2
.

Let
x^{\star}(\theta)
denote the unique optimistic minimizer for
\theta\in\mathcal{U}
.
Set
x_{0}:=x^{\star}(\theta_{0})
.
We prove that
F
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
locally by (i) building a smooth chart for
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
near
x_{0}
,
(ii) reducing the bilevel problem to a fixed-domain minimization and applying an envelope/Danskin lemma (which yields both differentiability and continuity of
\nabla F
),
and (iii) computing the resulting gradient in the pseudoinverse form.

Step 1: A
\theta
-dependent chart for
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
near
x_{0}
.

By
Section

C.1
, after shrinking neighborhoods if needed there are an open neighborhood

\mathcal{U}_{\theta}\subseteq\mathcal{U}
, a bounded open ball
\mathcal{U}_{u}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\texttt{k}}
around
0
, an open
neighborhood
\mathcal{W}
of
x_{0}
, and a
\mathcal{C}^{2}
map

\psi:\mathcal{U}_{\theta}\times\mathcal{U}_{u}\to\mathcal{W}

such that
\psi(\theta_{0},0)=x_{0}
, each
u\mapsto\psi(\theta,u)
is an embedded chart, and

\mathcal{S}(\theta)\cap\mathcal{W}=\{\psi(\theta,u):u\in\mathcal{U}_{u}\},\qquad\theta\in\mathcal{U}_{\theta}.

Step 2: Reduce to a fixed-domain minimization and show
F\in\mathcal{C}^{1}
.

Step 1 only gives a
\mathcal{C}^{2}
chart near
x_{0}
. Since
u\mapsto\psi(\theta,u)
parametrizes only the part of
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
near
x_{0}
,
we first show that the minimizer stays in this chart patch for
\theta
close to
\theta_{0}
.

Step 2a:
x^{\star}(\theta)
stays near
x_{0}
.

Let
\theta_{n}\to\theta_{0}
with
\theta_{n}\in\mathcal{U}_{\theta}
and set
x_{n}:=x^{\star}(\theta_{n})\in\mathcal{S}(\theta_{n})\subseteq\mathcal{V}
.
By compactness of
\mathcal{V}
, along a subsequence (not relabeled) we have
x_{n}\to\bar{x}\in\mathcal{V}
.
For any fixed
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, since
x_{n}\in\arg\min_{x}g(\theta_{n},x)
we have
g(\theta_{n},x_{n})\leq g(\theta_{n},y)
.
Taking
n\to\infty
and using continuity of
g
yields
g(\theta_{0},\bar{x})\leq g(\theta_{0},y)
for all
y
, hence
\bar{x}\in\mathcal{S}(\theta_{0})
.

Moreover,
\psi(\theta_{n},0)\in\mathcal{S}(\theta_{n})
is a feasible competitor for the optimistic problem at
\theta_{n}
, so

f(\theta_{n},x_{n})=F(\theta_{n})\ \leq\ f(\theta_{n},\psi(\theta_{n},0)).

Sending
n\to\infty
and using continuity of
f
and
\psi
gives
f(\theta_{0},\bar{x})\leq f(\theta_{0},x_{0})=F(\theta_{0})
.
By uniqueness of
x_{0}\in\arg\min_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\theta_{0})}f(\theta_{0},x)
and
\bar{x}\in\mathcal{S}(\theta_{0})
, we conclude
\bar{x}=x_{0}
.
Thus every convergent subsequence of
x_{n}
converges to
x_{0}
, hence
x_{n}\to x_{0}
.
After possibly shrinking
\mathcal{U}_{\theta}
, we may assume
x^{\star}(\theta)
lies in the chart patch for all
\theta\in\mathcal{U}_{\theta}
.

Step 2b: fixed-domain reduction.

Write
x^{\star}(\theta)=\psi(\theta,u^{\star}(\theta))
for some
u^{\star}(\theta)\in\mathcal{U}_{u}
.
The convergence
x^{\star}(\theta)\to x_{0}
from Step 2a and the fact that
u\mapsto\psi(\theta,u)
is a local
chart imply
u^{\star}(\theta)\to 0
as
\theta\to\theta_{0}
. Hence, after shrinking

\mathcal{U}_{\theta}
once more, we may choose a compact neighborhood
U\subset\mathcal{U}_{u}
of
0
such that

u^{\star}(\theta)\in U
for all
\theta\in\mathcal{U}_{\theta}
.
Define
\tilde{f}(\theta,u):=f(\theta,\psi(\theta,u))
.
Then, for all
\theta\in\mathcal{U}_{\theta}
,

F(\theta)=\min_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)}f(\theta,x)=\min_{u\in U}\tilde{f}(\theta,u).

Step 2c: differentiability via an envelope lemma.

Since
f
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
and
\psi
is
\mathcal{C}^{2}
,
\tilde{f}
is continuous and
\mathcal{C}^{1}
in
\theta
with continuous
\nabla_{\theta}\tilde{f}
.
We claim that for every
\theta\in\mathcal{U}_{\theta}
, the minimizer of
u\mapsto\tilde{f}(\theta,u)
over
U
is unique and equals
u^{\star}(\theta)
.
Indeed, by Step 2b,

F(\theta)=\min_{u\in U}\tilde{f}(\theta,u),\qquad\tilde{f}(\theta,u^{\star}(\theta))=f(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))=F(\theta),

so
u^{\star}(\theta)
is a minimizer.
Conversely, if
\bar{u}\in U
also minimizes
\tilde{f}(\theta,\cdot)
, then

f(\theta,\psi(\theta,\bar{u}))=\tilde{f}(\theta,\bar{u})=F(\theta),

so
\psi(\theta,\bar{u})\in\arg\min_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)}f(\theta,x)
.
By the uniqueness assumption in
Theorem

3.2
, this implies

\psi(\theta,\bar{u})=x^{\star}(\theta)=\psi(\theta,u^{\star}(\theta))
.
Since
u\mapsto\psi(\theta,u)
is a chart parametrization on the patch, it is injective, hence
\bar{u}=u^{\star}(\theta)
.
Applying
Section

C.1
at each
\theta\in\mathcal{U}_{\theta}
yields that
F
is differentiable on
\mathcal{U}_{\theta}
and

\nabla F(\theta)=\nabla_{\theta}\tilde{f}(\theta,u^{\star}(\theta))\qquad\text{for all }\theta\in\mathcal{U}_{\theta}.

(10)

Importantly, (
10
) does
not
require differentiability of
\theta\mapsto u^{\star}(\theta)
.

Step 2d: continuity of
\nabla F
(hence
F\in\mathcal{C}^{1}
).

We show that
\theta\mapsto u^{\star}(\theta)
is continuous on
\mathcal{U}_{\theta}
.
Fix
\bar{\theta}\in\mathcal{U}_{\theta}
and let
\theta_{n}\to\bar{\theta}
with
\theta_{n}\in\mathcal{U}_{\theta}
.
By compactness of
U
, along a subsequence (not relabeled) we have
u^{\star}(\theta_{n})\to\bar{u}\in U
.
Optimality of
u^{\star}(\theta_{n})
gives
\tilde{f}(\theta_{n},u^{\star}(\theta_{n}))\leq\tilde{f}(\theta_{n},u)
for all
u\in U
.
Sending
n\to\infty
and using continuity of
\tilde{f}
yields
\tilde{f}(\bar{\theta},\bar{u})\leq\tilde{f}(\bar{\theta},u)
for all
u\in U
.
Thus
\bar{u}\in\arg\min_{u\in U}\tilde{f}(\bar{\theta},u)=\{u^{\star}(\bar{\theta})\}
, hence
\bar{u}=u^{\star}(\bar{\theta})
.
Therefore
u^{\star}(\theta_{n})\to u^{\star}(\bar{\theta})
, proving continuity.
Since
\nabla_{\theta}\tilde{f}
is continuous, (
10
) implies that
\nabla F
is continuous on
\mathcal{U}_{\theta}
.
Setting
\mathcal{U}_{0}:=\mathcal{U}_{\theta}
, we conclude that
F
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
on
\mathcal{U}_{0}
.

Step 3: Compute
\nabla F(\theta)
and conclude (
2
).

Fix any
\theta\in\mathcal{U}_{0}
and write
u^{\star}:=u^{\star}(\theta)
and
x^{\star}:=x^{\star}(\theta)=\psi(\theta,u^{\star})\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)
.
By (
10
) and the chain rule for
\tilde{f}(\theta,u)=f(\theta,\psi(\theta,u))
(with
u
held fixed),

\nabla F(\theta)=\nabla_{\theta}f(\theta,x^{\star})+\big(\nabla_{\theta}\psi(\theta,u^{\star})\big)^{\top}\nabla_{x}f(\theta,x^{\star}).

Since
x^{\star}
minimizes
f(\theta,\cdot)
over the manifold
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
(without boundary),
its Riemannian gradient vanishes, i.e.,

P_{\mathcal{T}_{x^{\star}}^{\theta}}\nabla_{x}f(\theta,x^{\star})=0
, hence
\nabla_{x}f(\theta,x^{\star})\in\mathcal{N}_{x^{\star}}^{\theta}
.

On the other hand, for all
(\theta,u)\in\mathcal{U}_{0}\times\mathcal{U}_{u}
we have
\psi(\theta,u)\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)
, so

\nabla_{x}g(\theta,\psi(\theta,u))=0
.
Differentiating this identity with respect to
\theta
(with
u
held fixed) gives

H(\theta,\psi(\theta,u))\,\nabla_{\theta}\psi(\theta,u)+\nabla_{x\theta}^{2}g(\theta,\psi(\theta,u))=0.

Evaluating at
u=u^{\star}
and multiplying by
H(\theta,x^{\star})^{\dagger}
yields

P_{\mathcal{N}_{x^{\star}}^{\theta}}\nabla_{\theta}\psi(\theta,u^{\star})=-H(\theta,x^{\star})^{\dagger}\nabla_{x\theta}^{2}g(\theta,x^{\star}),

where we used that
H(\theta,x^{\star})^{\dagger}H(\theta,x^{\star})=P_{\mathcal{N}_{x^{\star}}^{\theta}}
by
Section

2
(ii).
Since
\nabla_{x}f(\theta,x^{\star})\in\mathcal{N}_{x^{\star}}^{\theta}
, we can insert the projector and obtain

\displaystyle\big(\nabla_{\theta}\psi(\theta,u^{\star})\big)^{\top}\nabla_{x}f(\theta,x^{\star})

\displaystyle=-\big(\nabla_{x\theta}^{2}g(\theta,x^{\star})\big)^{\top}H(\theta,x^{\star})^{\dagger}\nabla_{x}f(\theta,x^{\star})

\displaystyle=-\nabla_{\theta x}^{2}g(\theta,x^{\star})H(\theta,x^{\star})^{\dagger}\nabla_{x}f(\theta,x^{\star}),

where we used
\nabla^{2}_{\theta x}g:=(\nabla^{2}_{x\theta}g)^{\top}
and the symmetry of
H(\theta,x^{\star})^{\dagger}
.
Substituting into the expression for
\nabla F(\theta)
gives (
2
).
∎

Remark C.3

(A convenient sufficient condition for
F\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\Theta)
)
.

Assume
\Theta
is compact and
\arg\min_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)}f(\theta,x)
is a singleton for every
\theta\in\Theta
.
If, in addition, the lower-level solution manifolds are uniformly bounded in the sense that there exists a compact set
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\mathcal{S}(\theta)\subseteq\mathcal{V}
for all
\theta\in\Theta

(e.g.,
\mathcal{V}=\mathbb{B}_{d}(0;\mathsf{D})
under
4
),
then
F
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
on
\Theta
and (
2
) holds for all
\theta\in\Theta
.
Indeed,
Theorem

3.2
applies at each
\theta_{0}\in\Theta
; compactness of
\Theta
allows us to extract a finite subcover of the resulting local
\mathcal{C}^{1}
neighborhoods.

C.2
Proof of
Theorem

3.6

Proof of
Theorem

3.6
.

Let
x_{0}:=x^{\star}(\theta_{0})
. We prove the result in four steps. First, we write the
moving manifold
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
in fixed local coordinates. Second, we use intrinsic non-degeneracy and the implicit
function theorem to obtain a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
local selected branch. Third, we use uniqueness at
\theta_{0}
to show that
this local branch is the global optimistic branch for nearby parameters. Finally, we differentiate through lower-level
stationarity to obtain the pseudoinverse formula and local Lipschitzness of
\nabla F
.

Step 1: Reduce the moving-manifold problem to fixed local coordinates.

By
Section

C.1
, after shrinking neighborhoods if necessary, there are an open neighborhood

\mathcal{U}_{\theta}
of
\theta_{0}
, a bounded convex open ball
\mathcal{U}_{u}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\texttt{k}}
around
0
whose closure
lies in a slightly larger coordinate domain, an open neighborhood
\mathcal{W}
of
x_{0}
, and a
\mathcal{C}^{2}
map

\psi:\mathcal{U}_{\theta}\times\mathcal{U}_{u}\to\mathcal{W},\qquad\psi(\theta_{0},0)=x_{0},

such that, for every
\theta\in\mathcal{U}_{\theta}
,

\mathcal{S}(\theta)\cap\mathcal{W}=\{\psi(\theta,u):u\in\mathcal{U}_{u}\}.

We keep the same symbol
\psi
for its extension to this slightly larger coordinate domain and shrink

\mathcal{U}_{u}
, if needed, so that
u\mapsto\psi(\theta,u)
is injective on a neighborhood of

\overline{\mathcal{U}_{u}}
for all
\theta\in\mathcal{U}_{\theta}
. In these coordinates, the local restriction of the
upper-level objective to
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
is

\tilde{f}(\theta,u):=f(\theta,\psi(\theta,u)).

Since
f\in\mathcal{C}^{2}
and
\psi\in\mathcal{C}^{2}
, we have
\tilde{f}\in\mathcal{C}^{2}
.

Step 2: Non-degeneracy gives a smooth local minimizer branch.

At
\theta=\theta_{0}
, the point
u=0
corresponds to
x_{0}
. Since
x_{0}
minimizes
f(\theta_{0},\cdot)
over

\mathcal{S}(\theta_{0})
,
u=0
is a local minimizer of
\tilde{f}(\theta_{0},\cdot)
, and hence

\nabla_{u}\tilde{f}(\theta_{0},0)=0.

Let
A:=D_{u}\psi(\theta_{0},0)
. Then
A
has full column rank and

\mathrm{range}(A)=\mathcal{T}_{x_{0}}^{\theta_{0}}
, because
u\mapsto\psi(\theta_{0},u)
is an embedded chart for

\mathcal{S}(\theta_{0})
around
x_{0}
. Because
u=0
is a critical point of the restricted objective, the
second-derivative terms of the chart do not contribute to the Hessian quadratic form at
u=0
. Therefore, for every

w\neq 0
, with
v:=Aw
,

w^{\top}\nabla^{2}_{uu}\tilde{f}(\theta_{0},0)w=\big\langle v,\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{S}(\theta_{0})}\bar{f}_{\theta_{0}}(x_{0})[v]\big\rangle.

The non-degeneracy assumption makes the right-hand side positive for all
w\neq 0
, so

\nabla^{2}_{uu}\tilde{f}(\theta_{0},0)\succ 0
. Applying the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
implicit function theorem to

\nabla_{u}\tilde{f}(\theta,u)=0

gives, after shrinking
\mathcal{U}_{\theta}
, a unique
\mathcal{C}^{1}
map

u^{\star}:\mathcal{U}_{\theta}\to\mathcal{U}_{u},\qquad u^{\star}(\theta_{0})=0,

such that
\nabla_{u}\tilde{f}(\theta,u^{\star}(\theta))=0
. By continuity of
\nabla^{2}_{uu}\tilde{f}
, we can further
shrink
\mathcal{U}_{\theta}
and choose a closed ball
\mathcal{B}_{u}\subset\mathcal{U}_{u}
, with

u^{\star}(\theta)\in\mathrm{int}(\mathcal{B}_{u})
, such that

\nabla^{2}_{uu}\tilde{f}(\theta,u)\succ 0,\qquad\theta\in\mathcal{U}_{\theta},\ u\in\mathcal{B}_{u}.

Since
\mathcal{B}_{u}
is convex,
\tilde{f}(\theta,\cdot)
is strictly convex on
\mathcal{B}_{u}
. Thus

u^{\star}(\theta)
is the unique minimizer of
\tilde{f}(\theta,\cdot)
on
\mathcal{B}_{u}
. Define

x_{\mathrm{loc}}(\theta):=\psi(\theta,u^{\star}(\theta)).

Then
x_{\mathrm{loc}}
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
, belongs to
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
, and uniquely minimizes
f(\theta,\cdot)

over the local branch
\psi(\theta,\mathcal{B}_{u})
.

Step 3: The local branch is the global optimistic branch.

The previous step only controls the part of
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
near
x_{0}
. We now rule out competitors on the rest of

\mathcal{S}(\theta)
. Choose a closed ball
\mathcal{B}_{u}^{0}
around
0
such that

\mathcal{B}_{u}^{0}\subset\mathrm{int}(\mathcal{B}_{u})
, and then choose an open Euclidean ball

\mathcal{W}_{0}:=\{x:\|x-x_{0}\|<r\}
such that
\overline{\mathcal{W}_{0}}\subset\mathcal{W}
and

\mathcal{S}(\theta_{0})\cap\overline{\mathcal{W}_{0}}\subset\psi(\theta_{0},\mathrm{int}(\mathcal{B}_{u}^{0})).

After shrinking
\mathcal{U}_{\theta}
, we may assume

\mathcal{S}(\theta)\cap\overline{\mathcal{W}_{0}}\subseteq\psi(\theta,\mathcal{B}_{u}),\qquad\theta\in\mathcal{U}_{\theta}.

Indeed, otherwise there would be
\theta_{n}\to\theta_{0}
and points

z_{n}=\psi(\theta_{n},u_{n})\in\mathcal{S}(\theta_{n})\cap\overline{\mathcal{W}_{0}}
with
u_{n}\notin\mathcal{B}_{u}
. Passing to a
subsequence,
u_{n}\to\bar{u}\in\overline{\mathcal{U}_{u}}\setminus\mathrm{int}(\mathcal{B}_{u})
, and

z_{n}=\psi(\theta_{n},u_{n})\to\psi(\theta_{0},\bar{u})\in\mathcal{S}(\theta_{0})\cap\overline{\mathcal{W}_{0}}.

This contradicts

\mathcal{S}(\theta_{0})\cap\overline{\mathcal{W}_{0}}\subset\psi(\theta_{0},\mathrm{int}(\mathcal{B}_{u}^{0}))
, the inclusion

\mathcal{B}_{u}^{0}\subset\mathrm{int}(\mathcal{B}_{u})
, and injectivity of the chart at
\theta_{0}
.

Since
x_{0}
is the unique minimizer of
f(\theta_{0},\cdot)
over the compact set
\mathcal{S}(\theta_{0})
, there is a
positive gap away from
\mathcal{W}_{0}
:

\Delta_{0}:=\min_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\theta_{0})\setminus\mathcal{W}_{0}}\big(f(\theta_{0},x)-f(\theta_{0},x_{0})\big)>0,

with the convention that the minimum over an empty set is
+\infty
.

We claim that, after shrinking
\mathcal{U}_{\theta}
once more, no optimistic minimizer at parameter

\theta\in\mathcal{U}_{\theta}
lies outside
\mathcal{W}_{0}
. Suppose otherwise. Then there are
\theta_{n}\to\theta_{0}

and
y_{n}\in\mathcal{S}(\theta_{n})\setminus\mathcal{W}_{0}
such that

f(\theta_{n},y_{n})\leq f(\theta_{n},x_{\mathrm{loc}}(\theta_{n})).

By
1
, all lower-level minimizers lie in the fixed compact set
\mathcal{V}
. Passing to a subsequence,

y_{n}\to\bar{y}\in\mathcal{V}
. To see that
\bar{y}\in\mathcal{S}(\theta_{0})
, fix any
z\in\mathcal{S}(\theta_{0})
. Since

y_{n}
minimizes
g(\theta_{n},\cdot)
,
g(\theta_{n},y_{n})\leq g(\theta_{n},z)
. Taking
n\to\infty
gives

g(\theta_{0},\bar{y})\leq g(\theta_{0},z)=\min_{x}g(\theta_{0},x),

so
\bar{y}\in\mathcal{S}(\theta_{0})
. Also
\bar{y}\notin\mathcal{W}_{0}
, because
\mathcal{W}_{0}
is open and

y_{n}\notin\mathcal{W}_{0}
. Taking limits in the displayed inequality and using

x_{\mathrm{loc}}(\theta_{n})\to x_{0}
, we get

f(\theta_{0},\bar{y})\leq f(\theta_{0},x_{0}),

which contradicts the positive gap
\Delta_{0}
. Therefore every global optimistic minimizer for
\theta
close to

\theta_{0}
lies in
\mathcal{W}_{0}
, hence in the charted branch
\psi(\theta,\mathcal{B}_{u})
. Since

u^{\star}(\theta)
is the unique minimizer on this branch, the global optimistic minimizer is unique and equals

x^{\star}(\theta):=x_{\mathrm{loc}}(\theta).

Thus
x^{\star}
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
near
\theta_{0}
, and

F(\theta)=f(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta)).

Step 4: Differentiate the selected branch.

Because
x^{\star}(\theta)\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)
, lower-level stationarity gives

\nabla_{x}g(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))=0.

Differentiating this identity with respect to
\theta
yields

\nabla_{x\theta}^{2}g(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))+\nabla_{xx}^{2}g(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))\,\nabla_{\theta}x^{\star}(\theta)=0.

Let
H(\theta):=\nabla_{xx}^{2}g(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))
. By
Section

2
,

\ker H(\theta)=\mathcal{T}_{x^{\star}(\theta)}^{\theta},\qquad H(\theta)^{\dagger}H(\theta)=P_{\mathcal{N}_{x^{\star}(\theta)}^{\theta}}.

Multiplying the differentiated stationarity identity by
H(\theta)^{\dagger}
gives the normal component of the branch
sensitivity:

P_{\mathcal{N}_{x^{\star}(\theta)}^{\theta}}\nabla_{\theta}x^{\star}(\theta)=-H(\theta)^{\dagger}\nabla_{x\theta}^{2}g(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta)).

Since
x^{\star}(\theta)
minimizes
f(\theta,\cdot)
over
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
, the Riemannian first-order condition gives

\nabla_{x}f(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))\in\mathcal{N}_{x^{\star}(\theta)}^{\theta}
. Hence the tangent component of

\nabla_{\theta}x^{\star}(\theta)
does not contribute to the chain rule:

\displaystyle\nabla F(\theta)

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\theta}f(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))+\big(\nabla_{\theta}x^{\star}(\theta)\big)^{\top}\nabla_{x}f(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\theta}f(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))-\nabla_{\theta x}^{2}g(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))\,H(\theta)^{\dagger}\nabla_{x}f(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta)).

This is the pseudoinverse hyper-gradient formula. Finally,
H(\theta)
has constant rank along the branch and its
nonzero eigenvalues are bounded away from zero by
Section

2
. Since
g\in\mathcal{C}^{3}
and

x^{\star}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}
, the map
\theta\mapsto H(\theta)
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
, and the Moore–Penrose
pseudoinverse is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
on the constant-rank stratum. Therefore
\theta\mapsto H(\theta)^{\dagger}
is

\mathcal{C}^{1}
after shrinking the neighborhood if needed. The displayed formula, together with
f\in\mathcal{C}^{2}

and
x^{\star}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}
, implies
\nabla F\in\mathcal{C}^{1}
. On a compact smaller neighborhood of
\theta_{0}
,

D(\nabla F)
is bounded, so
\nabla F
is locally Lipschitz.
∎

Appendix D
Proofs for Section
4

This appendix contains the proof of the global smoothness result used in the algorithmic development of
Section

4
.

D.1
Proof of
Section

4

Proof of
Section

4
.

For each
\bar{\theta}\in\Theta
,
2
and
Theorem

3.6
give an ambient open neighborhood

W_{\bar{\theta}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
on which the local selected branch exists and
F
is smooth.
Choose a smaller open neighborhood
\mathcal{U}_{\bar{\theta}}
of
\bar{\theta}
such that

\overline{\mathcal{U}_{\bar{\theta}}}\cap\Theta\subset W_{\bar{\theta}}
, and set

V_{\bar{\theta}}:=\mathcal{U}_{\bar{\theta}}\cap\Theta
.
On this compact patch there is a finite constant
L_{\bar{\theta}}
such that

\|\nabla F(\theta)-\nabla F(\theta^{\prime})\|\leq L_{\bar{\theta}}\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|,\qquad\forall\,\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in V_{\bar{\theta}}.

(11)

The local constant above can be obtained in the standard hyper-gradient way from the closed-form formula.
On such a local patch, let
x^{\star}_{\bar{\theta}}
denote the selected
\mathcal{C}^{1}
branch and define

z(\theta):=(\theta,x^{\star}_{\bar{\theta}}(\theta))
. Write, for compactness,

a(z):=\nabla_{\theta}f(z),\qquad A(z):=\nabla^{2}_{\theta x}g(z),\qquad P(z):=\big[\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(z)\big]^{\dagger},\qquad b(z):=\nabla_{x}f(z).

After shrinking the local patch if necessary, choose finite constants
\kappa_{\bar{\theta}}
,

B_{A,\bar{\theta}},B_{P,\bar{\theta}},B_{b,\bar{\theta}}
and

L_{a,\bar{\theta}},L_{A,\bar{\theta}},L_{P,\bar{\theta}},L_{b,\bar{\theta}}
such that

\|z(\theta)-z(\theta^{\prime})\|\leq\kappa_{\bar{\theta}}\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|,

and, on the local graph
\{z(\theta):\theta\in V_{\bar{\theta}}\}
, the functions

a,A,P,b
have Lipschitz constants
L_{a,\bar{\theta}},L_{A,\bar{\theta}},L_{P,\bar{\theta}},L_{b,\bar{\theta}}
,
while
A,P,b
are bounded by
B_{A,\bar{\theta}},B_{P,\bar{\theta}},B_{b,\bar{\theta}}
.
The pseudoinverse constants are finite because, along this local graph, the Hessian

\nabla^{2}_{xx}g
has constant rank and its nonzero eigenvalues are bounded away from zero by the normal spectral gap in

Section

2
. We use the hyper-gradient formula

\nabla F(\theta)=a(z(\theta))-A(z(\theta))P(z(\theta))b(z(\theta))

as follows. Fix
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in V_{\bar{\theta}}
and abbreviate

z:=z(\theta)
and
z^{\prime}:=z(\theta^{\prime})
. Then

\displaystyle\nabla F(\theta)-\nabla F(\theta^{\prime})

\displaystyle=a(z)-a(z^{\prime})-\big(A(z)P(z)b(z)-A(z^{\prime})P(z^{\prime})b(z^{\prime})\big),

and the product difference is decomposed by adding and subtracting

A(z^{\prime})P(z)b(z)
and
A(z^{\prime})P(z^{\prime})b(z)
:

\displaystyle A(z)P(z)b(z)-A(z^{\prime})P(z^{\prime})b(z^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\big(A(z)-A(z^{\prime})\big)P(z)b(z)

\displaystyle\quad+A(z^{\prime})\big(P(z)-P(z^{\prime})\big)b(z)

\displaystyle\quad+A(z^{\prime})P(z^{\prime})\big(b(z)-b(z^{\prime})\big).

Therefore, using the local Lipschitz and boundedness constants above,

\displaystyle\|\nabla F(\theta)-\nabla F(\theta^{\prime})\|

\displaystyle\leq\|a(z)-a(z^{\prime})\|+\|A(z)-A(z^{\prime})\|\,\|P(z)\|\,\|b(z)\|

\displaystyle\quad+\|A(z^{\prime})\|\,\|P(z)-P(z^{\prime})\|\,\|b(z)\|

\displaystyle\quad+\|A(z^{\prime})\|\,\|P(z^{\prime})\|\,\|b(z)-b(z^{\prime})\|

\displaystyle\leq\Big(L_{a,\bar{\theta}}+L_{A,\bar{\theta}}B_{P,\bar{\theta}}B_{b,\bar{\theta}}+B_{A,\bar{\theta}}L_{P,\bar{\theta}}B_{b,\bar{\theta}}+B_{A,\bar{\theta}}B_{P,\bar{\theta}}L_{b,\bar{\theta}}\Big)\|z-z^{\prime}\|.

Combining this with
\|z-z^{\prime}\|\leq\kappa_{\bar{\theta}}\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|
yields the explicit local bound

L_{\bar{\theta}}\;:=\;\kappa_{\bar{\theta}}\Big(L_{a,\bar{\theta}}+L_{A,\bar{\theta}}B_{P,\bar{\theta}}B_{b,\bar{\theta}}+B_{A,\bar{\theta}}L_{P,\bar{\theta}}B_{b,\bar{\theta}}+B_{A,\bar{\theta}}B_{P,\bar{\theta}}L_{b,\bar{\theta}}\Big),

(12)

which is one admissible choice in (
11
).

The sets
\{V_{\bar{\theta}}\}_{\bar{\theta}\in\Theta}
form an open cover of the compact metric space
\Theta
(with the relative topology).
Choose a finite subcover
V_{1},\ldots,V_{J}
, with corresponding local constants
L_{1},\ldots,L_{J}
, and define

L_{F}:=\max_{1\leq j\leq J}L_{j}\quad\left(\text{equivalently, one may take }L_{j}=\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in V_{j}\\
\theta\neq\theta^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\frac{\|\nabla F(\theta)-\nabla F(\theta^{\prime})\|}{\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|}\right).

(13)

This quantity is finite by the local smoothness bound (
11
).

It remains to show that the local constants globalize without any additional Lipschitz assumption on the selected branch.
Let
\delta>0
be a Lebesgue number of the finite cover
\{V_{j}\}_{j=1}^{J}
of
\Theta
: every subset of
\Theta
with diameter
strictly smaller than
\delta
is contained in some
V_{j}
.
Fix
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta
. If
\theta=\theta^{\prime}
, the claim is trivial.
Otherwise, since
\Theta
is convex, the line segment

\theta_{s}:=\theta+s(\theta^{\prime}-\theta),\qquad s\in[0,1],

is contained in
\Theta
. Choose an integer
M
such that
\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|/M<\delta
, and set

\theta_{\ell}:=\theta_{\ell/M}
for
\ell=0,\ldots,M
.
For each
\ell
, the two-point set
\{\theta_{\ell},\theta_{\ell+1}\}
has diameter below
\delta
, so it lies in some

V_{j_{\ell}}
. Applying the local bound on that set gives

\|\nabla F(\theta_{\ell+1})-\nabla F(\theta_{\ell})\|\leq L_{j_{\ell}}\|\theta_{\ell+1}-\theta_{\ell}\|\leq L_{F}\|\theta_{\ell+1}-\theta_{\ell}\|.

Summing over
\ell=0,\ldots,M-1
yields

\|\nabla F(\theta^{\prime})-\nabla F(\theta)\|\leq L_{F}\sum_{\ell=0}^{M-1}\|\theta_{\ell+1}-\theta_{\ell}\|=L_{F}\|\theta^{\prime}-\theta\|,

which proves the global smoothness claim.
∎

Appendix E
Proofs and Technical Details for Section
5

This appendix gives the complete proof pipeline behind the convergence analysis in
Section

5
.
First,
Section

E.1
proves the inexact projected-gradient reduction in
Section

5
: once
F
is smooth, convergence to stationarity is controlled by the average hyper-gradient error
\mathbb{E}\|e_{t}\|^{2}
.
Second,
Section

E.2
proves the hyper-gradient stability bound
Section

5
. This step compares the exact pseudoinverse hyper-gradient at
x^{\star}(\theta_{t})
with the ridge-regularized, CG-approximated hyper-gradient evaluated at the selected candidate
\tilde{x}_{t}
, separating the error into selected-point error, CG residual, ridge bias, and an off-tube moment term.

The remaining subsections bound these terms in algorithmic quantities.

Section

E.3
proves the selection estimate
Section

5
: it combines curvature control for
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
, Gibbs/ULA tube concentration, a hard-selection value-gap argument, and the uniform restricted-curvature bound in
Section

A.3
to bound
\mathbb{E}\|\tilde{x}_{t}-x^{\star}(\theta_{t})\|^{2}
.
Finally,
Section

E.4
substitutes the stability and selection estimates into a single parameter-level bound for
\mathbb{E}\|e_{t}\|^{2}
, chooses
(N,\lambda,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}},\gamma,\eta_{t},R_{v})
so that this error is
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})
, and derives the conservative oracle-complexity bound reported in
Section

5
.

E.1
Outer-loop guarantee

Proof of
Section

5
.

Fix
T\geq 1
and write
g_{t}:=\widehat{h}_{t}=\nabla F(\theta_{t})+e_{t}
.
Define the (used) gradient mapping

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{t}:=\alpha^{-1}\big(\theta_{t}-\mathrm{Proj}_{\Theta}(\theta_{t}-\alpha g_{t})\big)=\alpha^{-1}(\theta_{t}-\theta_{t+1}).

Step 1: a descent inequality in terms of
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{t}
.

Let
\Delta_{t}:=\theta_{t+1}-\theta_{t}=-\alpha\,\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{t}
.
By
L_{F}
-smoothness of
F
on
\Theta
and convexity of
\Theta
, we have

F(\theta_{t+1})\leq F(\theta_{t})+\langle\nabla F(\theta_{t}),\Delta_{t}\rangle+\frac{L_{F}}{2}\|\Delta_{t}\|^{2}.

Since
\theta_{t+1}=\mathrm{Proj}_{\Theta}(\theta_{t}-\alpha g_{t})
and
\theta_{t}\in\Theta
, the projection
optimality condition yields

\langle g_{t},\Delta_{t}\rangle\ \leq\ -\frac{1}{\alpha}\|\Delta_{t}\|^{2}.

Using
\nabla F(\theta_{t})=g_{t}-e_{t}
, Cauchy–Schwarz, and the weighted Young inequality

uv\leq\frac{1}{4\alpha}u^{2}+\alpha v^{2},\qquad u,v\geq 0,

gives

\displaystyle F(\theta_{t+1})

\displaystyle\leq F(\theta_{t})+\langle g_{t},\Delta_{t}\rangle-\langle e_{t},\Delta_{t}\rangle+\frac{L_{F}}{2}\|\Delta_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq F(\theta_{t})-\frac{1}{\alpha}\|\Delta_{t}\|^{2}+\|e_{t}\|\,\|\Delta_{t}\|+\frac{L_{F}}{2}\|\Delta_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq F(\theta_{t})-\frac{1}{\alpha}\|\Delta_{t}\|^{2}+\frac{1}{4\alpha}\|\Delta_{t}\|^{2}+\alpha\|e_{t}\|^{2}+\frac{L_{F}}{2}\|\Delta_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=F(\theta_{t})-\Big(\frac{3}{4\alpha}-\frac{L_{F}}{2}\Big)\|\Delta_{t}\|^{2}+\alpha\|e_{t}\|^{2}.

Using
\alpha\leq 1/L_{F}
gives
\frac{3}{4\alpha}-\frac{L_{F}}{2}\geq\frac{1}{4\alpha}
, hence

F(\theta_{t+1})\leq F(\theta_{t})-\frac{1}{4\alpha}\|\Delta_{t}\|^{2}+\alpha\|e_{t}\|^{2}=F(\theta_{t})-\frac{\alpha}{4}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{t}\|^{2}+\alpha\|e_{t}\|^{2}.

Summing over
t=0,\dots,T-1
and using
F(\theta_{T})\geq F_{\star}
yields

\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{t}\|^{2}\leq\frac{4\,(F(\theta_{0})-F_{\star})}{\alpha}+4\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\|e_{t}\|^{2}.

(14)

Step 2: relate
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{t}
to the true gradient mapping.

By nonexpansiveness of projection,

\|\mathcal{G}_{\Theta}(\theta_{t},\nabla F(\theta_{t});\alpha)-\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{t}\|=\frac{1}{\alpha}\big\|\mathrm{Proj}_{\Theta}(\theta_{t}-\alpha g_{t})-\mathrm{Proj}_{\Theta}(\theta_{t}-\alpha\nabla F(\theta_{t}))\big\|\leq\|e_{t}\|.

Therefore
\|\mathcal{G}_{\Theta}(\theta_{t},\nabla F(\theta_{t});\alpha)\|^{2}\leq 2\|\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{t}\|^{2}+2\|e_{t}\|^{2}
,
and combining with (
14
) gives

\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\|\mathcal{G}_{\Theta}(\theta_{t},\nabla F(\theta_{t});\alpha)\|^{2}\leq\frac{8\,(F(\theta_{0})-F_{\star})}{\alpha}+10\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\|e_{t}\|^{2}.

Dividing by
T
and taking expectations yields (
4
).
∎

E.2
Hyper-gradient stability and proof of
Section

5

Standing assumptions

1
,
3
,
2
and
4

Tube radius and ridge invertibility

Section

E.2

General stability bound

Section

E.2

On-tube stability

Section

E.2

Off-tube moment control

Section

E.2

Hyper-gradient error

Section

5

Figure 2
:
Hyper-gradient error proof for
Section

5
.

This figure summarizes the proof strategy presented in Appendix
E.2
: tube invertibility, deterministic stability, its on-tube specialization, and off-tube clipping control are combined to prove Lemma
5
.

We prove
Section

5
in the same order as the terms in (
5
).
First,
Section

E.2
gives a quantitative tube radius
r(\gamma)
on which the ridge matrix is uniformly invertible.
Second,
Section

E.2
proves a deterministic stability estimate for a ridge-regularized, inexact linear solve.
Third,
Section

E.2
specializes this estimate to the tube event
\mathcal{E}_{t}
.
Fourth,
Section

E.2
controls the complement
\mathcal{E}_{t}^{c}
using the clipping safeguard and the
polynomial-growth condition on
\nabla^{2}_{\theta x}g
in
4
.
The final proof then splits the second moment over
\mathcal{E}_{t}
and
\mathcal{E}_{t}^{c}
.

Figure

2
summarizes the proof strategy and dependencies for this section.

Lemma E.1

(Tube radius ensuring ridge invertibility)
.

Let
1
and
3
hold.
Recall from
3
that
\rho
is the radius of the tube around
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
on which the
third-derivative bound
L_{g,3}
holds. Let
\bar{L}_{g,3}:=\max\{L_{g,3},1\}
and define

r(\gamma):=\min\left\{\rho,\frac{\gamma}{2\bar{L}_{g,3}}\right\}.

Then for all
\theta\in\Theta
and all

x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfying
\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{S}(\theta))\leq r(\gamma)
, we have

\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta,x)+\gamma I\ \succeq\ \tfrac{\gamma}{2}\,I,\qquad\text{and hence}\qquad\big\|\big(\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta,x)+\gamma I\big)^{-1}\big\|\ \leq\ \tfrac{2}{\gamma}.

Proof.

Let
y\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)
be a nearest projection of
x
, so
\|x-y\|=\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{S}(\theta))\leq r(\gamma)
.
By
Section

2
,
\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta,y)\succeq 0
.
Since
r(\gamma)\leq\rho
, the segment from
y
to
x
stays inside the
\rho
-tube around
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
.
Assumption
3
therefore gives

\|\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta,x)-\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta,y)\|\leq L_{g,3}\|x-y\|\leq\bar{L}_{g,3}r(\gamma)\leq\gamma/2.

Weyl’s inequality yields
\lambda_{\min}(\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta,x))\geq-\gamma/2
.
Thus
\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta,x)+\gamma I\succeq(\gamma/2)I
, and the inverse-norm bound follows.
∎

Before stating the stability lemma, we clarify the notation for the selected branch and the candidate point. By
2
, for every

\theta\in\Theta
there is a unique optimistic minimizer, which we denote by
x^{\star}(\theta)
. For each

\bar{\theta}\in\Theta
,
Theorem

3.6
gives a neighborhood of
\bar{\theta}
on which this same selection
coincides with a local
\mathcal{C}^{1}
branch. On overlaps, the local branches agree by uniqueness. Thus
x^{\star}
is a
globally defined selection on
\Theta
, and it is locally
\mathcal{C}^{1}
around every point of
\Theta
.
In
Section

E.2
,
\tilde{x}_{t}
is a fixed candidate point at which the inexact implicit gradient is evaluated.
The lemma is deterministic and does not use how
\tilde{x}_{t}
was generated; in the ULA/Gibbs application below,

\tilde{x}_{t}=\hat{x}_{N}^{\lambda}(\theta_{t})
, the
BoN
-selected point returned by the ULA candidate generator.

Lemma E.2

(General stability bound (technical))
.

Let
1
,
2
and
4
hold, and let
x^{\star}:\Theta\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
denote the unique optimistic
selection described above.
Fix
\gamma>0
, an iteration
t
, a parameter
\theta_{t}\in\Theta
, and a candidate point
\tilde{x}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Let
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be any compact convex set containing
\{x^{\star}(\theta):\theta\in\Theta\}\cup\{\tilde{x}_{t}\}
.
Assume
\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})+\gamma I
is invertible and satisfies

\big\|\big(\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})+\gamma I\big)^{-1}\big\|\leq\kappa_{\gamma}.

Let
\tilde{v}_{t}
satisfy the residual bound

\big\|\big(\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})+\gamma I\big)\tilde{v}_{t}-\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})\big\|\ \leq\ \eta_{t}.

Define

\widehat{h}_{t}:=\nabla_{\theta}f(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})-\nabla^{2}_{\theta x}g(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})\tilde{v}_{t},\qquad e_{t}:=\widehat{h}_{t}-\nabla F(\theta_{t}).

Then

\|e_{t}\|\ \leq\ C_{x}\Bigl(1+\kappa_{\gamma}+\frac{\kappa_{\gamma}}{c+\gamma}\Bigr)\,\|\tilde{x}_{t}-x^{\star}(\theta_{t})\|\ +\ C_{\mathrm{lin}}\cdot\kappa_{\gamma}\,\eta_{t}\ +\ C_{\mathrm{reg}}\cdot\gamma,

where one admissible choice of constants is

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{lin}}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{(\theta,x)\in\Theta\times\mathcal{V}}\|\nabla^{2}_{\theta x}g(\theta,x)\|,

\displaystyle C_{x}

\displaystyle:=L_{\theta f,x}+L_{\theta x,x}\,B_{f,x}+B_{\theta x}\,L_{f_{x},x}+B_{\theta x}\,L_{g_{xx},x}\,B_{f,x},

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{reg}}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\frac{\|\nabla^{2}_{\theta x}g(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))\|\,\|\nabla_{x}f(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))\|}{c^{2}},

where
c>0
is the normal spectral gap constant from
Section

2
,

\displaystyle B_{\theta x}

\displaystyle:=\sup_{(\theta,x)\in\Theta\times\mathcal{V}}\|\nabla^{2}_{\theta x}g(\theta,x)\|,\qquad B_{f,x}:=\sup_{(\theta,x)\in\Theta\times\mathcal{V}}\|\nabla_{x}f(\theta,x)\|,

and
L_{\theta f,x},L_{\theta x,x},L_{f_{x},x},L_{g_{xx},x}<\infty
are uniform Lipschitz constants on
\mathcal{V}

(uniform over
\theta\in\Theta
) for

x\mapsto\nabla_{\theta}f(\theta,x)
,
x\mapsto\nabla^{2}_{\theta x}g(\theta,x)
,

x\mapsto\nabla_{x}f(\theta,x)
, and
x\mapsto\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta,x)
, respectively.
Such constants exist and are finite because these maps are continuously differentiable and
\Theta\times\mathcal{V}
is compact and convex.

Proof of
Section

E.2
.

Write
x_{t}^{\star}:=x^{\star}(\theta_{t})
.
By
Theorem

3.6
and
2
,

\nabla F(\theta_{t})=\nabla_{\theta}f(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})-\nabla^{2}_{\theta x}g(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\big[\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\big]^{\dagger}\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star}).

Let

v_{t,\gamma}:=\big(\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})+\gamma I\big)^{-1}\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t}).

Add and subtract the ridge-regularized expressions at
\tilde{x}_{t}
and
x_{t}^{\star}
, and write

e_{t}=(I)+(II)+(III),

where

(I):=\nabla^{2}_{\theta x}g(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})\,(v_{t,\gamma}-\tilde{v}_{t}),

\displaystyle(II):={}

\displaystyle\nabla_{\theta}f(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})-\nabla^{2}_{\theta x}g(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})v_{t,\gamma}-\nabla_{\theta}f(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})

\displaystyle+\nabla^{2}_{\theta x}g(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\big(\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})+\gamma I\big)^{-1}\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star}),

and

(III):=\nabla^{2}_{\theta x}g(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\Big(\big[\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\big]^{\dagger}-\big(\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})+\gamma I\big)^{-1}\Big)\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star}).

For term (II), the
\nabla_{\theta}f
difference is bounded by

\|\nabla_{\theta}f(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})-\nabla_{\theta}f(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\|\leq L_{\theta f,x}\|\tilde{x}_{t}-x_{t}^{\star}\|.

For the product difference, add and subtract

\nabla^{2}_{\theta x}g(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\big(\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})+\gamma I\big)^{-1}\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})

and

\nabla^{2}_{\theta x}g(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\big(\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})+\gamma I\big)^{-1}\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star}).

This gives

\displaystyle\Big\|\nabla^{2}_{\theta x}g(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})\big(\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})+\gamma I\big)^{-1}\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})

\displaystyle-\nabla^{2}_{\theta x}g(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\big(\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})+\gamma I\big)^{-1}\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\Big\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\nabla^{2}_{\theta x}g(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})-\nabla^{2}_{\theta x}g(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\|\,\big\|\big(\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})+\gamma I\big)^{-1}\big\|\,\|\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})\|

\displaystyle+\|\nabla^{2}_{\theta x}g(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\|\,\big\|\big(\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})+\gamma I\big)^{-1}\big\|\,\|\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})-\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\|

\displaystyle+\|\nabla^{2}_{\theta x}g(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\|\,\Big\|\Big[\big(\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})+\gamma I\big)^{-1}

\displaystyle-\big(\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})+\gamma I\big)^{-1}\Big]\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\Big\|.

The first two terms are bounded by

L_{\theta x,x}\kappa_{\gamma}B_{f,x}\|\tilde{x}_{t}-x_{t}^{\star}\|\quad\text{and}\quad B_{\theta x}\kappa_{\gamma}L_{f_{x},x}\|\tilde{x}_{t}-x_{t}^{\star}\|.

For the last term, use the resolvent identity to obtain

\displaystyle\Big\|\Big[\big(\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})+\gamma I\big)^{-1}-\big(\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})+\gamma I\big)^{-1}\Big]\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\Big\|

\displaystyle\leq\kappa_{\gamma}\,\|\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})-\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\|\,\big\|\big(\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})+\gamma I\big)^{-1}\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\big\|.

Since
x_{t}^{\star}\in\mathcal{S}(\theta_{t})
and
\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\in\mathcal{N}_{x_{t}^{\star}}^{\theta_{t}}
,
Section

2
implies

\big\|\big(\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})+\gamma I\big)^{-1}\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\big\|\leq\frac{\|\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\|}{c+\gamma}\leq\frac{B_{f,x}}{c+\gamma}.

Moreover,

\|\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})-\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\|\leq L_{g_{xx},x}\|\tilde{x}_{t}-x_{t}^{\star}\|.

Therefore, the last product term is bounded by

B_{\theta x}\kappa_{\gamma}L_{g_{xx},x}B_{f,x}\,\frac{\|\tilde{x}_{t}-x_{t}^{\star}\|}{c+\gamma}.

Combining these bounds yields

\|(II)\|\leq C_{x}\Bigl(1+\kappa_{\gamma}+\frac{\kappa_{\gamma}}{c+\gamma}\Bigr)\,\|\tilde{x}_{t}-x_{t}^{\star}\|.

For term (I), define the residual

r_{t}:=\big(\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})+\gamma I\big)\tilde{v}_{t}-\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t}).

Then

\tilde{v}_{t}-v_{t,\gamma}=\big(\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})+\gamma I\big)^{-1}r_{t},

and hence

\|(I)\|=\|\nabla^{2}_{\theta x}g(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})(\tilde{v}_{t}-v_{t,\gamma})\|\leq\|\nabla^{2}_{\theta x}g(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})\|\,\big\|\big(\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})+\gamma I\big)^{-1}\big\|\,\|r_{t}\|\leq C_{\mathrm{lin}}\,\kappa_{\gamma}\,\eta_{t}.

For term (III), note that
x_{t}^{\star}
is a minimizer of
f(\theta_{t},\cdot)
over the manifold
\mathcal{S}(\theta_{t})
, so
the first-order condition implies
\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\in\mathcal{N}_{x_{t}^{\star}}^{\theta_{t}}
.
By
Section

2
, the restriction of
\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})
to
\mathcal{N}_{x_{t}^{\star}}^{\theta_{t}}
has
eigenvalues in
[c,\infty)
.
Let
\{u_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d-\texttt{k}}
be an orthonormal eigenbasis of
\mathcal{N}_{x_{t}^{\star}}^{\theta_{t}}
with

\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})u_{i}=\lambda_{i}u_{i}
and
\lambda_{i}\geq c
.
Since
\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\in\mathcal{N}_{x_{t}^{\star}}^{\theta_{t}}
, we can write

\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})=\sum_{i=1}^{d-\texttt{k}}\beta_{i}u_{i}
.
On
\mathcal{N}_{x_{t}^{\star}}^{\theta_{t}}
, the pseudoinverse coincides with the inverse, hence

\big[\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\big]^{\dagger}\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})=\sum_{i=1}^{d-\texttt{k}}(\beta_{i}/\lambda_{i})u_{i},

while

\big(\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})+\gamma I\big)^{-1}\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})=\sum_{i=1}^{d-\texttt{k}}\big(\beta_{i}/(\lambda_{i}+\gamma)\big)u_{i}.

Therefore

\displaystyle\big(\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})+\gamma I\big)^{-1}\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})-\big[\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\big]^{\dagger}\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d-\texttt{k}}\beta_{i}\Big(\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}+\gamma}-\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}}\Big)u_{i}.

Hence

\displaystyle\big\|\big(\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})+\gamma I\big)^{-1}\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})-\big[\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\big]^{\dagger}\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\big\|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{i=1,\dots,d-\texttt{k}}\Big|\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}+\gamma}-\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}}\Big|\,\|\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\|.

Using
\big|\frac{1}{\lambda+\gamma}-\frac{1}{\lambda}\big|=\frac{\gamma}{\lambda(\lambda+\gamma)}
and
\lambda_{i}\geq c
gives

\displaystyle\big\|\big(\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})+\gamma I\big)^{-1}\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})-\big[\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\big]^{\dagger}\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\big\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\gamma}{c(c+\gamma)}\,\|\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\|\leq\frac{\gamma}{c^{2}}\,\|\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\|.

Multiplying by
\|\nabla^{2}_{\theta x}g(\theta_{t},x_{t}^{\star})\|
and taking the supremum over
\theta\in\Theta
yields

\|(III)\|\leq C_{\mathrm{reg}}\gamma
.
Combining the three bounds gives the claim.
∎

Section

E.2
follows by taking a fixed compact set containing the
\rho
-tube around all
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
, restricting to

\mathrm{dist}(\tilde{x}_{t},\mathcal{S}(\theta_{t}))\leq r(\gamma)
, and applying
Section

E.2
to bound
\kappa_{\gamma}\leq 2/\gamma
.

Lemma E.3

(Stability of the inexact implicit term on the tube)
.

Assume the hypotheses of Lemma
E.2
hold for the same
\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t},\tilde{v}_{t}
, and fix
\gamma>0
with tube radius
r(\gamma)
from Lemma
E.2
.
Let
\mathcal{V}=\mathbb{B}_{d}(0;\mathsf{D}+\rho)
.
Fix an iteration
t
with
\theta_{t}\in\Theta
and
\mathrm{dist}(\tilde{x}_{t},\mathcal{S}(\theta_{t}))\leq r(\gamma)
, so that
\tilde{x}_{t}\in\mathcal{V}
.
Assume clipping is inactive, i.e., the clipped vector used by the algorithm satisfies
v_{t}=\tilde{v}_{t}
.
Then the algorithmic hyper-gradient error
e_{t}=\widehat{h}_{t}-\nabla F(\theta_{t})
obeys

\|e_{t}\|\ \leq\ A_{\gamma}\,\|\tilde{x}_{t}-x^{\star}(\theta_{t})\|\ +\ \tfrac{2C_{\mathrm{lin}}}{\gamma}\,\eta_{t}\ +\ C_{\mathrm{reg}}\,\gamma,

where
C_{\mathrm{lin}},C_{\mathrm{reg}}
are as in Lemma
E.2
and

A_{\gamma}:=C_{x}\Bigl(1+\tfrac{2}{\gamma}+\tfrac{2}{\gamma(c+\gamma)}\Bigr),

with
c>0
the normal spectral gap constant from
Section

2
and
C_{x}
as in Lemma
E.2
.

Proof.

Because
r(\gamma)\leq\rho
and
\mathcal{S}(\theta_{t})\subseteq\mathbb{B}_{d}(0;\mathsf{D})
, the tube condition implies
\tilde{x}_{t}\in\mathcal{V}
.
Under this same condition, Lemma
E.2
gives
\kappa_{\gamma}\leq 2/\gamma
.
Since clipping is inactive, the algorithmic
\widehat{h}_{t}
, which is formed with
v_{t}
, coincides with the quantity in Lemma
E.2
, which is formed with
\tilde{v}_{t}
.
Applying Lemma
E.2
with
\mathcal{V}=\mathbb{B}_{d}(0;\mathsf{D}+\rho)
and substituting this inverse-norm bound gives the claim.
∎

Lemma E.4

(Bounding the off-tube term under clipping)
.

Fix
\gamma>0
and let
r(\gamma)
be the tube radius from Lemma
E.2
.
Assume Algorithm
1
uses the safeguard
v_{t}=\mathrm{Proj}_{\mathbb{B}_{d}(0;R_{v})}(\tilde{v}_{t})
, and assume the CG output
\tilde{v}_{t}
satisfies
\|b_{t}-(H_{t}+\gamma I)\tilde{v}_{t}\|\leq\eta_{t}
.
Let
\mathsf{D}_{t}:=\mathrm{dist}(\tilde{x}_{t},\mathcal{S}(\theta_{t}))
.
Define the tube event
\mathcal{E}_{t}:=\{\mathsf{D}_{t}\leq r(\gamma)\}
.
Under
4
and the smoothness of
F
on compact
\Theta
, there exists a constant
C_{\mathrm{off}}<\infty
, independent of
t,\gamma,\eta_{t},R_{v}
, such that

\|e_{t}\|^{2}\ \leq\ C_{\mathrm{off}}(1+R_{v}^{2})(1+\mathsf{D}_{t}^{2\mathsf{n}_{\theta x}}).

Consequently,

\mathbb{E}\big[\|e_{t}\|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t}^{c}}\big]\ \leq\ C_{\mathrm{off}}(1+R_{v}^{2})\,\mathbb{E}\big[(1+\mathsf{D}_{t}^{2\mathsf{n}_{\theta x}})\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t}^{c}}\big].

Moreover, on
\mathcal{E}_{t}
Lemma
E.2
implies
\|\tilde{v}_{t}\|\leq(2/\gamma)(L_{f,1}+\eta_{t})
, so choosing

R_{v}\geq(2/\gamma)(L_{f,1}+\eta_{t})
ensures the safeguard is inactive on
\mathcal{E}_{t}
(i.e.,
v_{t}=\tilde{v}_{t}
).

Proof.

Since
\|v_{t}\|\leq R_{v}
by construction,
4
gives

\|\widehat{h}_{t}\|\leq\|\nabla_{\theta}f(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})\|+\|\nabla^{2}_{\theta x}g(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})\|\,\|v_{t}\|\leq L_{f,1}+\big(C_{\theta x}\|\tilde{x}_{t}\|^{\mathsf{n}_{\theta x}}+D_{\theta x}\big)R_{v}.

Since
F
is smooth on compact
\Theta
,
B_{F}:=\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\|\nabla F(\theta)\|<\infty
.
Using
(a+b+c)^{2}\leq 3(a^{2}+b^{2}+c^{2})
, we obtain

\|e_{t}\|^{2}\leq C(1+R_{v}^{2})(1+\|\tilde{x}_{t}\|^{2\mathsf{n}_{\theta x}})

for a finite constant
C
.
Because
\mathcal{S}(\theta_{t})\subseteq\mathbb{B}_{d}(0;\mathsf{D})
, we have
\|\tilde{x}_{t}\|\leq\mathsf{D}+\mathsf{D}_{t}
, and hence

(1+\|\tilde{x}_{t}\|^{2\mathsf{n}_{\theta x}})\leq C^{\prime}(1+\mathsf{D}_{t}^{2\mathsf{n}_{\theta x}})
.
This proves the off-tube moment bound.

For the final claim, on
\mathcal{E}_{t}
, Lemma
E.2
and the residual relation

(\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})+\gamma I)\tilde{v}_{t}=\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})+r_{t}
with
\|r_{t}\|\leq\eta_{t}
give

\|\tilde{v}_{t}\|\leq\frac{2}{\gamma}\big(\|\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})\|+\eta_{t}\big)\leq\frac{2}{\gamma}(L_{f,1}+\eta_{t}),

where the last inequality uses the global Lipschitzness of
f
.
Under the stated choice of
R_{v}
, this gives
\|\tilde{v}_{t}\|\leq R_{v}
, so the Euclidean projection onto
\mathbb{B}_{d}(0;R_{v})
leaves
\tilde{v}_{t}
unchanged.
∎

E.2.1
Proof of
Section

5

Proof.

Fix an iteration
t
.
Split

\mathbb{E}\|e_{t}\|^{2}=\mathbb{E}\big[\|e_{t}\|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t}}\big]\ +\ \mathbb{E}\big[\|e_{t}\|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t}^{c}}\big].

On the tube.

On
\mathcal{E}_{t}
, our choice of clipping radius ensures
v_{t}=\tilde{v}_{t}
(Lemma
E.2
).
Therefore Lemma
E.2
gives

\|e_{t}\|\ \leq\ A_{\gamma}\,\|\tilde{x}_{t}-x^{\star}(\theta_{t})\|\ +\ \tfrac{2C_{\mathrm{lin}}}{\gamma}\,\eta_{t}\ +\ C_{\mathrm{reg}}\,\gamma.

Using
(a+b+c)^{2}\leq 3(a^{2}+b^{2}+c^{2})
and that
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t}}\leq 1
yields

\mathbb{E}\big[\|e_{t}\|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t}}\big]\ \leq\ 3A_{\gamma}^{2}\,\mathbb{E}\big[\|\tilde{x}_{t}-x^{\star}(\theta_{t})\|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t}}\big]\ +\ \tfrac{12C_{\mathrm{lin}}^{2}}{\gamma^{2}}\,\eta_{t}^{2}\ +\ 3C_{\mathrm{reg}}^{2}\,\gamma^{2}.

Recalling
A_{\gamma}=C_{x}(1+\tfrac{2}{\gamma}+\tfrac{2}{\gamma(c+\gamma)})
from Lemma
E.2
gives the first three terms in (
5
).

Off the tube.

Lemma
E.2
yields

\mathbb{E}\big[\|e_{t}\|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t}^{c}}\big]\leq C_{\mathrm{off}}(1+R_{v}^{2})\,\mathbb{E}\big[(1+\mathsf{D}_{t}^{2\mathsf{n}_{\theta x}})\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t}^{c}}\big].

Combining the two bounds gives (
5
).
∎

E.3
Selection Error and Proof of
Section

5

We prove
Section

5
by reducing the selected-point error to two simpler quantities.
Fix
\theta
and let
\tilde{x}
be the hard-selected candidate. Since
\tilde{x}
is generally not on the minimizer
manifold
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
, we let
\bar{x}
be its Euclidean projection onto
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
. The proof then follows the
decomposition

\|\tilde{x}-x^{\star}(\theta)\|^{2}\ \leq\ 2\,\mathrm{dist}(\tilde{x},\mathcal{S}(\theta))^{2}+2\,\|\bar{x}-x^{\star}(\theta)\|^{2}.

The first term is an
off-manifold tube error
: it is controlled by showing that Gibbs/ULA candidates remain close to

\mathcal{S}(\theta)
with high probability. The second term is an
on-manifold selection error
: hard selection first gives
a small value gap
f(\theta,\bar{x})-F(\theta)
, and
Section

A.3
converts this value gap
into squared distance on
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
. More concretely, the proof uses the following sequence:

1.

establish geometric and sampling ingredients: curvature control, global quadratic growth of
g
, tube concentration,
and Poincaré/Rényi transportation control for approximate ULA samples;

2.

prove that hard selection gives a small on-manifold value gap for
\bar{x}
;

3.

convert the on-manifold value gap into
\|\bar{x}-x^{\star}(\theta)\|^{2}
using uniform quadratic growth of

f|_{\mathcal{S}(\theta)}
and a positive away-from-minimizer gap;

4.

combine the tube error and the on-manifold distance bound to obtain (
6
).

Figure

3
summarizes this dependency structure.

,
