Figure 3
:
Four-step selection-error proof for
Section

5
.

Each lemma, proposition, corollary, or definition used in Appendix
E.3
appears in its own box. The larger step boxes group the results according to the proof strategy stated at the beginning of Appendix
E.3
; dashed arrows mark supporting assumptions or intermediate deterministic superquantile steps.

Notation for the selection proof.

We fix notation for the selection argument and record the lower-tail functional used in the deterministic superquantile bound inside
Section

E.3.3
.
Fix
\theta\in\Theta
and recall

\mathcal{S}(\theta)=\arg\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}g(\theta,x),\qquad F(\theta)=\min_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)}f(\theta,x).

Throughout, assume that the constrained problem admits a unique optimistic minimizer

x^{\star}(\theta)\in\arg\min_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)}f(\theta,x),

as in
2
. Given candidates
X_{1},\dots,X_{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, our hard selection rule is

\tilde{x}\in\arg\min_{i\in[1:M]}f(\theta,X_{i}),\qquad\delta:=\frac{1}{M}.

Since
\tilde{x}
need not lie on
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
, we also define its projection

\bar{x}\in\arg\min_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)}\|x-\tilde{x}\|,\qquad\text{so that}\qquad\|\tilde{x}-\bar{x}\|=\mathrm{dist}(\tilde{x},\mathcal{S}(\theta)).

Empirical and Gibbs measures.

Define the empirical measure of the candidates,

\widehat{\nu}_{M}:=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\delta_{X_{i}},

and recall the Gibbs measure used to sample near
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
,

\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}x)\propto\exp\{-g(\theta,x)/\lambda\}\,\mathrm{d}x.

Lower-tail superquantile (best-
\delta
CVaR).

We use the lower-tail superquantile functional
\mathrm{SQ}^{\mathrm{low}}_{\delta}
.

Definition E.5

(Lower
\delta
-superquantile)
.

Let
Z
be a real-valued random variable with (lower) quantile function

q_{u}(Z):=\inf\{t\in\mathbb{R}:\ \mathbb{P}(Z\leq t)\geq u\}
for
u\in(0,1)
.
Define

\mathrm{SQ}^{\mathrm{low}}_{\delta}(Z):=\frac{1}{\delta}\int_{0}^{\delta}q_{u}(Z)\,\mathrm{d}u,\qquad\delta\in(0,1).

If
X\sim\nu
and
Z=f(\theta,X)
, we also write

\mathrm{SQ}^{\mathrm{low}}_{\delta}(f(\theta,X);\,X\sim\nu)
.

E.3.1
Bounded curvature from analytic regularity of
g

This subsection proves the second fundamental form bound used in the volume comparison arguments of

[
48
, e.g., Lem. 4.1]
. The proof combines the manifold and normal-gap consequences of

PŁ
∘
from
Section

2
with the analytic regularity condition in
3
.
Note that
3
is stronger than the
L_{g,2}
-smoothness in
4
:
it also controls how
\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta,\cdot)
varies with
x
(via a uniform bound on
\nabla^{3}_{xxx}g
in a tube around
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
).
We use this bound later in
Section

E.3.3
, where the
M^{-1/\texttt{k}}
nearest-neighbor term follows from
small-ball volume scaling on
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
. Thus
Section

E.3.1
is the step that verifies, from our
assumptions on
g
, the curvature regularity needed to invoke the volume comparison result.

Proposition E.6

(Bounded second fundamental form from analytic regularity)
.

Let
1
and
3
hold, and let
c>0
be the uniform normal spectral gap from

Section

2
. Fix any
\theta\in\Theta
. Then the second fundamental form
\mathrm{II}
of
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
satisfies

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)}\ \|\mathrm{II}_{x}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\ \leq\ \frac{L_{g,3}}{c}.

In particular, since
L_{g,3}
and
c
are uniform over
\theta\in\Theta
, we obtain a uniform curvature bound over the family
\{\mathcal{S}(\theta)\}_{\theta\in\Theta}
.

Proof.

Fix
\theta
and abbreviate
\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{S}(\theta)
.
Let
H(x):=\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta,x)
.
Fix
x\in\mathcal{S}
and unit tangent vectors
u,v\in\mathcal{T}_{x}^{\theta}
.
Choose a
\mathcal{C}^{2}
curve
\gamma:(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\to\mathcal{S}
with
\gamma(0)=x
and
\dot{\gamma}(0)=v
,
and choose a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
tangent vector field
U(\cdot)
along
\gamma
with
U(0)=u
and
U(t)\in\mathcal{T}_{\gamma(t)}^{\theta}
.

By
Section

2
,
\ker H(\gamma(t))=\mathcal{T}_{\gamma(t)}^{\theta}
for all
t
. Since
U(t)
is tangent along

\gamma
, we have
H(\gamma(t))\,U(t)=0
.
Differentiating at
t=0
gives

0=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Big(H(\gamma(t))\,U(t)\Big)\Big|_{t=0}=\big(D_{v}H(x)\big)\,u+H(x)\,\dot{U}(0),

where
D_{v}H(x)
denotes the directional derivative of the Hessian in direction
v
.
Let
P_{\mathcal{N}_{x}^{\theta}}
denote the orthogonal projection onto the normal space
\mathcal{N}_{x}^{\theta}
.
Since
H(x)
is symmetric and
\ker H(x)=\mathcal{T}_{x}^{\theta}
, we have
\mathrm{range}(H(x))=(\mathcal{T}_{x}^{\theta})^{\perp}=\mathcal{N}_{x}^{\theta}
and hence

P_{\mathcal{N}_{x}^{\theta}}H(x)=H(x)P_{\mathcal{N}_{x}^{\theta}}
.
Applying
P_{\mathcal{N}_{x}^{\theta}}
to the display above yields

H(x)\,P_{\mathcal{N}_{x}^{\theta}}\dot{U}(0)\;=\;-\,P_{\mathcal{N}_{x}^{\theta}}\big(D_{v}H(x)\big)\,u.

By the definition of the second fundamental form of an embedded submanifold in Euclidean space,

\mathrm{II}_{x}(v,u)=P_{\mathcal{N}_{x}^{\theta}}\dot{U}(0)
.
Restricting
H(x)
to
\mathcal{N}_{x}^{\theta}
and using the uniform normal spectral gap gives

\|\mathrm{II}_{x}(v,u)\|\leq\big\|\big(H(x)|_{\mathcal{N}_{x}^{\theta}}\big)^{-1}\big\|\;\big\|\big(D_{v}H(x)\big)\,u\big\|\leq\frac{1}{c}\,\big\|\big(D_{v}H(x)\big)\,u\big\|.

Finally, by the bounded third derivative assumption,

\|(D_{v}H(x))\,u\|\leq\|\nabla^{3}_{xxx}g(\theta,x)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|v\|\,\|u\|\leq L_{g,3}.

Therefore
\|\mathrm{II}_{x}(v,u)\|\leq L_{g,3}/c
for all unit
u,v\in\mathcal{T}_{x}^{\theta}
, and taking the supremum over
x\in\mathcal{S}
yields the claim.
∎

E.3.2
Controlling the off-manifold term
\mathrm{dist}(\tilde{x},\mathcal{S}(\theta))

Proof strategy.

The off-manifold term is controlled in two steps. First, since the hard-selected point
\tilde{x}
is one of the
candidates, (
15
) reduces the problem to bounding the largest normal distance among the candidate
points. Second,
Section

E.3.2
turns the lower-level objective gap
g(\theta,x)-g_{\star}(\theta)
into a
uniform quadratic lower bound in
\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{S}(\theta))
. This converts the Gibbs density

\exp(-g(\theta,x)/\lambda)
into a subgaussian tail for the distance to
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
, which

Section

E.3.2
integrates to control

\mathbb{E}[\max_{i}\mathrm{dist}(X_{i},\mathcal{S}(\theta))]
and

\mathbb{E}[\max_{i}\mathrm{dist}^{2}(X_{i},\mathcal{S}(\theta))]
for exact Gibbs candidates. Finally,

Section

E.3.2
transfers the same bounds to candidates with Rényi-controlled marginal laws
\nu_{i}
, which is the
case used for the ULA outputs.

Max-distance reduction.

Because
\tilde{x}
is one of the candidates, it satisfies the deterministic inequality

\mathrm{dist}(\tilde{x},\mathcal{S}(\theta))\ \leq\ \max_{1\leq i\leq M}\mathrm{dist}(X_{i},\mathcal{S}(\theta)).

(15)

We now show that, under our standing assumptions, Gibbs samples concentrate around
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
with a
subgaussian
tail in the
normal distance
R:=\mathrm{dist}(X,\mathcal{S}(\theta))
, which yields the sharper moment bound

\mathbb{E}[\max_{i}R_{i}]\lesssim\sqrt{\lambda\log M}
.

Uniform quadratic growth for
g(\theta,\cdot)
.

Recall from
4
that
g(\theta,\cdot)
satisfies a quadratic growth bound
outside
a compact ball.
The next lemma shows that, under local
PŁ
∘
and normal nondegeneracy on the minimizer manifold, this can be upgraded to a
global

quadratic growth inequality in terms of the distance to
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
, uniformly over
\theta\in\Theta
.

Lemma E.7

(Uniform quadratic growth of
g(\theta,\cdot)
over
\Theta
)
.

Let
1
and
4
hold.
Then there exists a constant
\mu_{\mathrm{QG}}>0
such that for all
\theta\in\Theta
and all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

g(\theta,x)-\min_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}g(\theta,z)\ \geq\ \frac{\mu_{\mathrm{QG}}}{2}\,\mathrm{dist}^{2}\!\bigl(x,\mathcal{S}(\theta)\bigr).

(16)

Proof.

Let
\mathsf{D}
be the compactness radius from
4
, so that

\mathcal{S}(\theta)\subseteq\mathbb{B}_{d}(0;\mathsf{D})
for all
\theta\in\Theta
.
Write
g_{\star}(\theta):=\min_{z}g(\theta,z)
.

Preliminaries on the minimizer family.

Since each minimizer set is contained in
\mathbb{B}_{d}(0;\mathsf{D})
,

g_{\star}(\theta)=\min_{\|z\|\leq\mathsf{D}}g(\theta,z).

Thus
g_{\star}
is continuous on the compact set
\Theta
by Berge’s maximum theorem. Consequently, the solution graph

\mathcal{K}:=\{(\theta,y):\theta\in\Theta,\ y\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)\}=\{(\theta,y)\in\Theta\times\mathbb{B}_{d}(0;\mathsf{D}):g(\theta,y)=g_{\star}(\theta)\}

is compact. The standard local normal-chart argument for the zero set
\{\nabla_{x}g(\theta,x)=0\}
, using the normal
spectral gap in
Section

2
around each
(\theta,y)\in\mathcal{K}
and then patched by compactness, gives local Hausdorff continuity of

\theta\mapsto\mathcal{S}(\theta)
on
\Theta
. In particular, the map

(\theta,x)\mapsto\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{S}(\theta))
is continuous on
\Theta\times\mathbb{B}_{d}(0;\mathsf{D})
, and the
normal spaces
\mathcal{N}_{y}^{\theta}
vary continuously over
\mathcal{K}
.

Step 1: local quadratic growth in a uniform tube.

Let
c>0
be the normal spectral gap from
Section

2
, i.e.,

\langle v,\nabla_{xx}^{2}g(\theta,y)v\rangle\geq c\|v\|^{2}
for all
\theta\in\Theta
,
y\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)
, and

v\in\mathcal{N}_{y}^{\theta}
.
Since
(\theta,x)\mapsto\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta,x)
is continuous (
4
) and the normal bundle over

\mathcal{K}
is compact, there exists
r_{\mathrm{loc}}>0
such that
for all
\theta\in\Theta
, all
y\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)
, and all
x
with
\|x-y\|\leq r_{\mathrm{loc}}
,

\langle v,\nabla_{xx}^{2}g(\theta,x)v\rangle\ \geq\ \tfrac{c}{2}\,\|v\|^{2}\qquad\forall v\in\mathcal{N}_{y}^{\theta}.

(17)

Now fix any
\theta\in\Theta
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
R:=\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{S}(\theta))\leq r_{\mathrm{loc}}
.
Let
y\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)
be a Euclidean projection of
x
onto
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
, so that
\|x-y\|=R
.
By first-order optimality of the projection problem on the manifold,
u:=x-y
lies in the normal space
\mathcal{N}_{y}^{\theta}
.
Using
\nabla_{x}g(\theta,y)=0
(since
y\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)
) and the integral form of Taylor’s theorem,

g(\theta,x)-g(\theta,y)=\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)\,u^{\top}\nabla_{xx}^{2}g(\theta,y+tu)\,u\,\mathrm{d}t.

Because
\|tu\|\leq R\leq r_{\mathrm{loc}}
, (
17
) applies along the segment
y+tu
, giving

u^{\top}\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta,y+tu)u\geq(c/2)\|u\|^{2}
for all
t\in[0,1]
.
Therefore,

g(\theta,x)-g_{\star}(\theta)\geq g(\theta,x)-g(\theta,y)\geq\tfrac{c}{4}\,\|u\|^{2}=\tfrac{c}{4}\,R^{2}.

(18)

Step 2: quadratic growth outside the compact ball.

By
4
, there exist constants
\mu_{\mathsf{qg}}>0
and
\mathsf{D}>0
such that for all
\|x\|\geq\mathsf{D}
,

g(\theta,x)-g_{\star}(\theta)\ \geq\ \tfrac{\mu_{\mathsf{qg}}}{2}\,\mathrm{dist}^{2}\!\bigl(x,\mathcal{S}(\theta)\bigr),\qquad\forall\theta\in\Theta.

(19)

Step 3: the middle region by compactness.

Consider the set

\mathcal{A}:=\Big\{(\theta,x):\theta\in\Theta,\ \|x\|\leq\mathsf{D},\ \mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{S}(\theta))\geq r_{\mathrm{loc}}\Big\}.

By the continuity of the distance map just noted,
\mathcal{A}
is closed in

\Theta\times\mathbb{B}_{d}(0;\mathsf{D})
, hence compact. If
\mathcal{A}=\emptyset
, the middle region is vacuous; in this
case set
\mu_{\mathrm{mid}}:=+\infty
. Otherwise, the map

(\theta,x)\mapsto g(\theta,x)-g_{\star}(\theta)
is continuous on
\Theta\times\mathbb{B}_{d}(0;\mathsf{D})
and is strictly
positive on
\mathcal{A}
(since
\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{S}(\theta))>0
implies
x\notin\mathcal{S}(\theta)
).
In the nonempty case, therefore,

m_{\min}:=\min_{(\theta,x)\in\mathcal{A}}\big(g(\theta,x)-g_{\star}(\theta)\big)>0.

Moreover, if
\|x\|\leq\mathsf{D}
then
\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{S}(\theta))\leq 2\mathsf{D}
, so on the nonempty set

\mathcal{A}
we have

g(\theta,x)-g_{\star}(\theta)\ \geq\ m_{\min}\ \geq\ \frac{m_{\min}}{(2\mathsf{D})^{2}}\,\mathrm{dist}^{2}(x,\mathcal{S}(\theta)).

For this nonempty case, set
\mu_{\mathrm{mid}}:=2m_{\min}/(2\mathsf{D})^{2}
.

Step 4: combine the three regions.

Let

\mu_{\mathrm{QG}}:=\min\Big\{\tfrac{c}{2},\ \mu_{\mathsf{qg}},\ \mu_{\mathrm{mid}}\Big\}.

Then (
18
), (
19
), and the middle-region bound above imply (
16
) for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and all

\theta\in\Theta
.
∎

Subgaussian tube concentration and Rényi transfer.

The uniform quadratic growth bound from
Section

E.3.2
first gives Gaussian-like concentration of the normal
distance to
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
for exact Gibbs samples. We then transfer the same type of control to candidates whose
marginal laws are close to the Gibbs law in order-
2
Rényi divergence.

Lemma E.8

(Tube concentration for Gibbs samples)
.

Assume the setting of
Section

E.3.2
and fix any
\theta\in\Theta
.
Let
X\sim\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}
and define
R:=\mathrm{dist}(X,\mathcal{S}(\theta))
.
Then there exist constants
\lambda_{0},c_{\mathrm{tube}},C_{\mathrm{tube}},C_{\mathrm{tube},2}>0
(independent of
\theta
and
\lambda
) such that, for every
0<\lambda\leq\lambda_{0}
,

\mathbb{P}(R\geq t)\ \leq\ C_{\mathrm{tube}}\exp\!\Big(-c_{\mathrm{tube}}\,\tfrac{t^{2}}{\lambda}\Big),\qquad\forall t\geq 0.

(20)

Consequently,

\mathbb{E}[R]\leq C_{\mathrm{tube}}\sqrt{\lambda},\qquad\mathbb{E}[R^{2}]\leq C_{\mathrm{tube},2}\lambda.

Moreover, if
X_{1},\dots,X_{M}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}
and
\tilde{x}
is any measurable selection of one of the candidates, then

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{dist}(\tilde{x},\mathcal{S}(\theta))\big]\ \leq\ \mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{1\leq i\leq M}\mathrm{dist}(X_{i},\mathcal{S}(\theta))\Big]\ \leq\ C_{\mathrm{tube}}\,\sqrt{\lambda\,\log(1+M)}.

(21)

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{dist}(\tilde{x},\mathcal{S}(\theta))^{2}\big]\ \leq\ \mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{1\leq i\leq M}\mathrm{dist}(X_{i},\mathcal{S}(\theta))^{2}\Big]\ \leq\ C_{\mathrm{tube},2}\,\lambda\,\log(1+M).

(22)

Proof of
Section

E.3.2
.

Fix
\theta
and abbreviate
\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{S}(\theta)
and
g_{\star}:=\min_{z}g(\theta,z)
.
By
Section

E.3.2
,
g(\theta,x)\geq g_{\star}+\frac{\mu_{\mathrm{QG}}}{2}\mathrm{dist}^{2}(x,\mathcal{S})
.
Therefore, for any
t\geq 0
,

\mathbb{P}(R\geq t)=\frac{\int_{\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{S})\geq t}e^{-g(\theta,x)/\lambda}\,\mathrm{d}x}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-g(\theta,x)/\lambda}\,\mathrm{d}x}\leq\frac{\int_{\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{S})\geq t}\exp\!\big(-\tfrac{\mu_{\mathrm{QG}}}{2\lambda}\mathrm{dist}^{2}(x,\mathcal{S})\big)\,\mathrm{d}x}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-(g(\theta,x)-g_{\star})/\lambda}\,\mathrm{d}x}.

Let
q:=d-\texttt{k}
be the normal dimension of
\mathcal{S}
. Since
\mathcal{S}
is compact and embedded in

\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have
q\geq 1
.
We next use a uniform tubular-coordinate estimate around the family
\{\mathcal{S}(\theta)\}_{\theta\in\Theta}
.
By compactness of the solution graph, the uniform normal spectral gap in
Section

2
, and the uniform curvature
control in
Section

E.3.1
, there are constants
r_{\mathrm{tube}}>0
,
0<J_{\min}\leq J_{\max}<\infty
,
and
0<V_{\min}\leq V_{\max}<\infty
, independent of
\theta
, such that the normal map

\Phi_{\theta}(y,z)=y+z,\qquad y\in\mathcal{S},\quad z\in\mathcal{N}_{y}^{\theta},\quad\|z\|<r_{\mathrm{tube}},

is a diffeomorphism onto the
r_{\mathrm{tube}}
-tube around
\mathcal{S}
, its Jacobian lies in

[J_{\min},J_{\max}]
, and the Riemannian volume of
\mathcal{S}
lies in
[V_{\min},V_{\max}]
.

We first lower bound the denominator. Let

Z_{\lambda}(\theta):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-(g(\theta,x)-g_{\star})/\lambda}\,\mathrm{d}x.

The small-temperature normalizing-constant lower bound used in

48
, applied here in the normal coordinates above, gives the manifold-version
scaling

Z_{\lambda}(\theta)\geq c_{\mathrm{den}}\,\lambda^{q/2}

(23)

for all sufficiently small
\lambda
, with
c_{\mathrm{den}}>0
uniform in
\theta
. The exponent is
q/2
, rather
than
d/2
, because the Gibbs mass only concentrates in the
q=d-\texttt{k}
normal directions; integration along

\mathcal{S}
contributes a bounded positive manifold-volume factor.

We now upper bound the numerator. Write
a:=\mu_{\mathrm{QG}}/(2\lambda)
and split the domain into the part inside the
uniform tube and the part outside it.

Inside the tube.

Every point in the tube has a unique representation
x=y+z
, where
y\in\mathcal{S}
and

z\in\mathcal{N}_{y}^{\theta}
. Here
\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}_{\mathcal{S}}(y)
denotes the intrinsic
k
-dimensional volume
measure on
\mathcal{S}
induced by the ambient Euclidean metric; for example, it is arclength when
\texttt{k}=1
and
surface area when
\texttt{k}=2
. Thus

\int_{\mathcal{S}}\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}_{\mathcal{S}}(y)=\mathrm{Vol}_{\texttt{k}}(\mathcal{S}).

In these coordinates,
\mathrm{dist}(y+z,\mathcal{S})=\|z\|
and the Euclidean volume element decomposes as

\mathrm{d}x=J_{\theta}(y,z)\,\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}\mathrm{Vol}_{\mathcal{S}}(y),

where
J_{\theta}(y,z)\leq J_{\max}
. Therefore, after integrating out the
y
-variable using

\mathrm{Vol}_{\texttt{k}}(\mathcal{S})\leq V_{\max}
, and identifying each normal fiber

\mathcal{N}_{y}^{\theta}
with
\mathbb{R}^{q}
through an arbitrary orthonormal basis, the tube contribution is bounded by a

q
-dimensional Gaussian tail:

\displaystyle\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{S})\geq t\\
\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{S})<r_{\mathrm{tube}}\end{subarray}}e^{-a\,\mathrm{dist}^{2}(x,\mathcal{S})}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq J_{\max}V_{\max}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\mathbb{R}^{q}:\ \|z\|\geq t\\
\|z\|<r_{\mathrm{tube}}\end{subarray}}e^{-a\|z\|^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\leq J_{\max}V_{\max}\,|\mathbb{S}^{q-1}|\int_{t}^{\infty}e^{-ar^{2}}r^{q-1}\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\,a^{-q/2}\exp\!\big(-at^{2}/2\big).

(24)

The second inequality is just polar coordinates in the
q
-dimensional normal variable
z
: writing

z=r\omega
, with
\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{q-1}
, gives

\mathrm{d}z=r^{q-1}\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}\sigma(\omega)
, and integrating over
\omega
gives the factor

|\mathbb{S}^{q-1}|
. We then upper bound the radial integral by replacing the upper limit

r_{\mathrm{tube}}
with
\infty
.
For the last inequality, substitute
u=\sqrt{a}\,r
and use

\int_{s}^{\infty}u^{q-1}e^{-u^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}u\leq C_{q}e^{-s^{2}/2}
for all
s\geq 0
.

Outside the tube.

Let
R:=\max\{t,r_{\mathrm{tube}}\}
. We give a direct bound for this part. Since

\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathbb{B}_{d}(0;\mathsf{D})
, every point with
\|x\|>\mathsf{D}+R
satisfies

\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{S})\geq\|x\|-\mathsf{D}.

Hence

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{S})\geq R}e^{-a\,\mathrm{dist}^{2}(x,\mathcal{S})}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{S})\geq R\\
\|x\|\leq\mathsf{D}+R\end{subarray}}e^{-aR^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x\ +\ \int_{\|x\|>\mathsf{D}+R}e^{-a(\|x\|-\mathsf{D})^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq C_{d}(\mathsf{D}+R)^{d}e^{-aR^{2}}\ +\ C_{d}\int_{R}^{\infty}(\mathsf{D}+s)^{d-1}e^{-as^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}s,

where the last line uses polar coordinates with
s=\|x\|-\mathsf{D}
for the radial tail.
Because
R\geq r_{\mathrm{tube}}>0
, the polynomial factors are dominated by the Gaussian decay. More explicitly, using
the standard one-dimensional Gaussian-tail bound

\int_{R}^{\infty}(\mathsf{D}+s)^{d-1}e^{-as^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq C_{d,\mathsf{D},r_{\mathrm{tube}}}\,a^{-1}(1+R^{d-2})e^{-aR^{2}}

for
R\geq r_{\mathrm{tube}}
, and increasing the constant to also cover the bounded-annulus term, there exists

a_{0}<\infty
such that for all
a\geq a_{0}
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{S})\geq\max\{t,r_{\mathrm{tube}}\}}e^{-a\,\mathrm{dist}^{2}(x,\mathcal{S})}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq C_{5}\,a^{-q/2}\exp\!\big(-at^{2}/2\big).

(25)

Indeed, the last step follows because

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\geq a_{0}\\
R\geq r_{\mathrm{tube}}\end{subarray}}a^{q/2}(\mathsf{D}+R)^{d}e^{-aR^{2}/2}<\infty,\qquad\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}a\geq a_{0}\\
R\geq r_{\mathrm{tube}}\end{subarray}}a^{q/2-1}(1+R^{d-2})e^{-aR^{2}/2}<\infty,

and
R\geq t
.
Choose
\lambda_{0}>0
small enough so that the denominator lower bound above holds and

a=\mu_{\mathrm{QG}}/(2\lambda)\geq a_{0}
whenever
0<\lambda\leq\lambda_{0}
.
Combining (
24
) and (
25
),

\int_{\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{S})\geq t}e^{-a\,\mathrm{dist}^{2}(x,\mathcal{S})}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq C_{\mathrm{num}}\,a^{-q/2}\exp\!\big(-at^{2}/2\big)\leq C_{\mathrm{num}}^{\prime}\,\lambda^{q/2}\exp\!\Big(-\tfrac{\mu_{\mathrm{QG}}}{4\lambda}t^{2}\Big).

Dividing this numerator bound by (
23
) yields (
20
).
Integrating (
20
) over
t\geq 0
yields
\mathbb{E}[R]\leq C_{\mathrm{tube}}\sqrt{\lambda}
.
Similarly,

\mathbb{E}[R^{2}]=2\int_{0}^{\infty}t\,\mathbb{P}(R\geq t)\,\mathrm{d}t\leq 2C_{\mathrm{tube}}\int_{0}^{\infty}t\,\exp\!\Big(-c_{\mathrm{tube}}\,\tfrac{t^{2}}{\lambda}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C_{\mathrm{tube},2}\lambda

after increasing the constant if needed.

Finally, set
R_{i}:=\mathrm{dist}(X_{i},\mathcal{S})
. Since
\tilde{x}
is one of the candidates,

\mathrm{dist}(\tilde{x},\mathcal{S})\leq\max_{1\leq i\leq M}R_{i}.

By a union bound and (
20
),

\mathbb{P}\!\Big(\max_{1\leq i\leq M}R_{i}\geq t\Big)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{M}\mathbb{P}(R_{i}\geq t)\leq M\,C_{\mathrm{tube}}\exp\!\Big(-c_{\mathrm{tube}}\,\tfrac{t^{2}}{\lambda}\Big).

To bound the expectation, use the tail integral representation

\mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{1\leq i\leq M}R_{i}\Big]=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\!\Big(\max_{1\leq i\leq M}R_{i}\geq t\Big)\,\mathrm{d}t.

Let
t_{0}:=\sqrt{\frac{\lambda}{c_{\mathrm{tube}}}\log(1+M)}
. Splitting the integral and using the bound above yields

\mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{1\leq i\leq M}R_{i}\Big]\leq t_{0}+\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}M\,C_{\mathrm{tube}}\exp\!\Big(-c_{\mathrm{tube}}\,\tfrac{t^{2}}{\lambda}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}t.

Using
\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}e^{-at^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\frac{1}{2at_{0}}\,e^{-at_{0}^{2}}
for
a>0
(with
a=c_{\mathrm{tube}}/\lambda
) gives

\displaystyle\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}M\,C_{\mathrm{tube}}\exp\!\Big(-c_{\mathrm{tube}}\,\tfrac{t^{2}}{\lambda}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq M\,C_{\mathrm{tube}}\cdot\frac{\lambda}{2c_{\mathrm{tube}}\,t_{0}}\exp\!\Big(-c_{\mathrm{tube}}\,\tfrac{t_{0}^{2}}{\lambda}\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{M}{1+M}\cdot\frac{C_{\mathrm{tube}}}{2c_{\mathrm{tube}}}\cdot\frac{\lambda}{t_{0}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{\mathrm{tube}}}{2c_{\mathrm{tube}}}\,\frac{\sqrt{\lambda}}{\sqrt{\log(1+M)}}.

Since
M\geq 1
,
\log(1+M)\geq\log 2
, and we conclude that

\mathbb{E}[\max_{i}R_{i}]\leq C_{\mathrm{tube}}\sqrt{\lambda\log(1+M)}
after adjusting
C_{\mathrm{tube}}
.
Similarly,

\mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{1\leq i\leq M}R_{i}^{2}\Big]=2\int_{0}^{\infty}t\,\mathbb{P}\!\Big(\max_{1\leq i\leq M}R_{i}\geq t\Big)\,\mathrm{d}t.

Splitting at the same
t_{0}
and using
\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}te^{-at^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}t=\frac{1}{2a}e^{-at_{0}^{2}}
with
a=c_{\mathrm{tube}}/\lambda
, we get

\mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{1\leq i\leq M}R_{i}^{2}\Big]\leq t_{0}^{2}+\frac{MC_{\mathrm{tube}}\lambda}{c_{\mathrm{tube}}}\exp\!\Big(-c_{\mathrm{tube}}\,\tfrac{t_{0}^{2}}{\lambda}\Big)\leq C_{\mathrm{tube},2}\lambda\log(1+M),

which yields (
22
) after increasing
C_{\mathrm{tube},2}
.
∎

The next lemma is the form used for the candidates produced by the ULA/Gibbs instantiation of

Algorithm

1
. Those candidates are not assumed to be exact Gibbs samples: after finitely many ULA steps, the

i
-th candidate has marginal law
\nu_{i}
. The
lemma only requires each
\nu_{i}
to be close to the ideal Gibbs law in order-
2
Rényi divergence, and then transfers
the Gibbs tube tail from
Section

E.3.2
by a Cauchy–Schwarz change-of-measure argument. This Rényi premise
is not an additional modeling assumption for ULA candidates:
Section

E.3.5
below gives the required bound

R_{2}(\nu_{i}\|\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda})\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}
once each ULA chain is run for the corresponding number
of steps. Thus
Section

E.3.2
is the bridge from exact Gibbs concentration to the finite-time sampler used in the
algorithm.

Lemma E.9

(Tube bounds for candidates with Rényi-controlled marginals)
.

Assume the setting of
Section

E.3.2
. Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{M}
be candidates whose marginal laws

\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{M}
are not necessarily identical and satisfy

\max_{1\leq i\leq M}R_{2}(\nu_{i}\|\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda})\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}.

Let
\tilde{x}
be the hard-selection output

\tilde{x}\in\arg\min_{1\leq i\leq M}f(\theta,X_{i}),

with any measurable tie-breaking rule. Then

\mathbb{P}\!\Big(\max_{1\leq i\leq M}\mathrm{dist}(X_{i},\mathcal{S}(\theta))\geq t\Big)\leq M\,e^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}/2}\sqrt{C_{\mathrm{tube}}}\exp\!\Big(-\tfrac{c_{\mathrm{tube}}}{2}\tfrac{t^{2}}{\lambda}\Big),\qquad t\geq 0.

(26)

Consequently,

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{dist}(\tilde{x},\mathcal{S}(\theta))\big]\ \leq\ \mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{1\leq i\leq M}\mathrm{dist}(X_{i},\mathcal{S}(\theta))\Big]\ \leq\ C_{\mathrm{tube}}\,e^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}/2}\sqrt{\lambda\,\log(1+M)},

(27)

and

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{dist}(\tilde{x},\mathcal{S}(\theta))^{2}\big]\ \leq\ \mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{1\leq i\leq M}\mathrm{dist}(X_{i},\mathcal{S}(\theta))^{2}\Big]\ \leq\ C_{\mathrm{tube},2}\,e^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}/2}\lambda\,\log(1+M),

(28)

after increasing
C_{\mathrm{tube}}
and
C_{\mathrm{tube},2}
by universal numerical factors if necessary.

Proof of
Section

E.3.2
.

Fix
\theta
and abbreviate
\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{S}(\theta)
and

R_{i}:=\mathrm{dist}(X_{i},\mathcal{S})
. For any candidate index
i
, let

A_{t}:=\{x:\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{S})\geq t\}
.
Under
R_{2}(\nu_{i}\|\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda})\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}
, Cauchy–Schwarz yields

\nu_{i}(A_{t})=\int\mathbf{1}_{A_{t}}\,\frac{\mathrm{d}\nu_{i}}{\mathrm{d}\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}}\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}\leq e^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}/2}\,\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}(A_{t})^{1/2}.

Combining this with the Gibbs tail (
20
) gives

\mathbb{P}(R_{i}\geq t)\leq e^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}/2}\sqrt{C_{\mathrm{tube}}}\exp\!\big(-\tfrac{c_{\mathrm{tube}}}{2}\tfrac{t^{2}}{\lambda}\big)
,
and hence (by a union bound)

\mathbb{P}\!\Big(\max_{1\leq i\leq M}R_{i}\geq t\Big)\leq M\,e^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}/2}\sqrt{C_{\mathrm{tube}}}\exp\!\Big(-\tfrac{c_{\mathrm{tube}}}{2}\tfrac{t^{2}}{\lambda}\Big).

This proves (
26
). To integrate the tail, set

A_{\mathrm{R}}:=e^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}/2}\sqrt{C_{\mathrm{tube}}},\qquad\beta:=\frac{c_{\mathrm{tube}}}{2\lambda},\qquad t_{0}:=\sqrt{\beta^{-1}\log(1+M)}.

Then (
26
) gives

\mathbb{P}\!\Big(\max_{1\leq i\leq M}R_{i}\geq t\Big)\leq\min\{1,\;MA_{\mathrm{R}}e^{-\beta t^{2}}\}.

Using the tail integral formula and splitting at
t_{0}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{1\leq i\leq M}R_{i}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq t_{0}+MA_{\mathrm{R}}\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}e^{-\beta t^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq t_{0}+\frac{MA_{\mathrm{R}}}{2\beta t_{0}}e^{-\beta t_{0}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C(1+A_{\mathrm{R}})\sqrt{\lambda\log(1+M)}\leq C^{\prime}e^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}/2}\sqrt{\lambda\log(1+M)},

where the constants absorb
C_{\mathrm{tube}}
,
c_{\mathrm{tube}}
, and the fact that
\log(1+M)\geq\log 2
.
After increasing
C_{\mathrm{tube}}
, this proves (
27
).

Similarly,

\mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{1\leq i\leq M}R_{i}^{2}\Big]=2\int_{0}^{\infty}t\,\mathbb{P}\!\Big(\max_{1\leq i\leq M}R_{i}\geq t\Big)\,\mathrm{d}t.

Splitting at the same
t_{0}
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{1\leq i\leq M}R_{i}^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq t_{0}^{2}+2MA_{\mathrm{R}}\int_{t_{0}}^{\infty}te^{-\beta t^{2}}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=t_{0}^{2}+\frac{MA_{\mathrm{R}}}{\beta}e^{-\beta t_{0}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C(1+A_{\mathrm{R}})\lambda\log(1+M)\leq C^{\prime}e^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}/2}\lambda\log(1+M).

After increasing
C_{\mathrm{tube},2}
, this proves (
28
).
Finally, since the hard-selection output
\tilde{x}\in\arg\min_{i}f(\theta,X_{i})
is one of the candidates,

\mathrm{dist}(\tilde{x},\mathcal{S})\leq\max_{1\leq i\leq M}R_{i},

which gives the first inequalities in (
27
) and (
28
).
∎

E.3.3
From hard selection to a value gap on
\mathcal{S}(\theta)

With the tube controls in place, we next bound the value gap of the projected selected point
\bar{x}
. The deterministic superquantile bound is useful for interpreting hard selection, but the final rate below uses a direct hard-min argument to avoid an unnecessary factor
M
.

Tail-min control by an empirical lower superquantile.

Hard selection is a deterministic lower-tail operation, and its output is always below the corresponding
(lower) superquantile under the empirical law.

Lemma E.10

(Tail-selection
\Rightarrow
superquantile control)
.

Fix
\theta
, candidates
X_{1},\dots,X_{M}
, and
\delta\in(0,1)
. Let

\tilde{x}\in\arg\min_{i\in[1:M]}f(\theta,X_{i}).

Then

f(\theta,\tilde{x})\ \leq\ \mathrm{SQ}^{\mathrm{low}}_{\delta}\big(f(\theta,X);\,X\sim\widehat{\nu}_{M}\big).

Proof.

Under the empirical law
\widehat{\nu}_{M}
, the random variable
f(\theta,X)
takes values among

\{f(\theta,X_{i})\}_{i=1}^{M}
. Let
m:=\min_{1\leq i\leq M}f(\theta,X_{i})=f(\theta,\tilde{x})
.
For every
u\in(0,1)
, no value below
m
can have empirical probability at least
u
, hence

q_{u}(f(\theta,X))\geq m
. Averaging this pointwise inequality over
u\in(0,\delta)
gives the claim.
∎

Lifting
\tilde{x}
back to
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
via Lipschitzness.

We will compare
x^{\star}(\theta)
to
\bar{x}\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)
(the projection of
\tilde{x}
) and separate
(i) an off-manifold error
\|\tilde{x}-\bar{x}\|
from (ii) an on-manifold suboptimality gap at
\bar{x}
.

Lemma E.11

(Suboptimality gap for Rényi-controlled candidates)
.

Fix
\theta
and let
x^{\star}(\theta)
be the unique minimizer of
f(\theta,\cdot)
over
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
.
Assume
\texttt{k}\geq 1
and that
x\mapsto f(\theta,x)
is
L_{f,1}
-Lipschitz (as in
4
).
Given candidates
X_{1},\ldots,X_{M}
, let

\widehat{\nu}_{M}:=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{i=1}^{M}\delta_{X_{i}},\qquad\tilde{x}\in\arg\min_{1\leq i\leq M}f(\theta,X_{i}),

with any measurable tie-breaking rule, and let
\bar{x}
be any measurable Euclidean projection of
\tilde{x}
onto

\mathcal{S}(\theta)
.
Then, for every
\delta\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle 0\ \leq\ f(\theta,\bar{x})-f(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))\ \leq\

\displaystyle L_{f,1}\,\mathrm{dist}(\tilde{x},\mathcal{S}(\theta))

\displaystyle\quad+\Big[\mathrm{SQ}^{\mathrm{low}}_{\delta}\big(f(\theta,X);\,X\sim\widehat{\nu}_{M}\big)-f(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))\Big].

(29)

Moreover, for any reference measure
\nu
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with finite first moment,

\displaystyle\mathrm{SQ}^{\mathrm{low}}_{\delta}\big(f(\theta,X);\,X\sim\widehat{\nu}_{M}\big)-\mathrm{SQ}^{\mathrm{low}}_{\delta}\big(f(\theta,X);\,X\sim\nu\big)\ \leq\ \frac{L_{f,1}}{\delta}\;\mathsf{W}_{1}(\widehat{\nu}_{M},\nu),

(30)

and consequently, choosing
\nu=\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}
yields

\displaystyle f(\theta,\bar{x})-f(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))\ \leq\

\displaystyle L_{f,1}\,\mathrm{dist}(\tilde{x},\mathcal{S}(\theta))

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{L_{f,1}}{\delta}\;\mathsf{W}_{1}(\widehat{\nu}_{M},\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda})+\Big[\mathrm{SQ}^{\mathrm{low}}_{\delta}\big(f(\theta,X);\,X\sim\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}\big)-f(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))\Big].

(31)

Moreover, assume additionally that
0<\lambda\leq\lambda_{0}
, where
\lambda_{0}
is chosen small enough for both the
tube bound in
Section

E.3.2
and the projected-Gibbs small-ball bound used below, and that

X_{1},\dots,X_{M}
are independent candidates with marginal laws

\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{M}
, that
\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}
satisfies a Poincaré inequality with constant at most

C_{\mathrm{PI}}
, and that

\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}:=\max_{1\leq i\leq M}R_{2}(\nu_{i}\|\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda})<\infty.

Assume
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}\leq 1
.
Then, for the hard-selection output
\tilde{x}
and projection
\bar{x}
defined above, there exists a constant

C_{1}>0
(depending only on the on-manifold geometry near
x^{\star}(\theta)
; under the uniform assumptions above it can be
chosen uniformly over
\theta
) such that

\mathbb{E}\big[f(\theta,\bar{x})-F(\theta)\big]\ \leq\ 2L_{f,1}\,C_{\mathrm{tube}}\,\sqrt{\lambda\log(1+M)}\ +\ C_{1}\,M^{-1/\texttt{k}}\ +\ 4\sqrt{e-1}\,L_{f,1}\sqrt{M\,C_{\mathrm{PI}}}\,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}},

(32)

where
C_{\mathrm{tube}}
is the tube constant from
Section

E.3.2
.

Proof.

We first prove the deterministic bounds. Since
\bar{x}\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)
and
x^{\star}(\theta)
minimizes

f(\theta,\cdot)
over
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
, the left inequality in (
29
) holds. By Lipschitzness and
the definition of
\bar{x}
,

f(\theta,\bar{x})\leq f(\theta,\tilde{x})+L_{f,1}\,\mathrm{dist}(\tilde{x},\mathcal{S}(\theta)).

Using
Section

E.3.3
to bound
f(\theta,\tilde{x})
by the empirical lower superquantile gives
(
29
).

To prove (
30
), use the variational representation

\mathrm{SQ}^{\mathrm{low}}_{\delta}(Z)=\sup_{\tau\in\mathbb{R}}\left\{\tau-\frac{1}{\delta}\mathbb{E}[(\tau-Z)_{+}]\right\}.

For any coupling of
X\sim\widehat{\nu}_{M}
and
Y\sim\nu
, the map
z\mapsto(\tau-f(\theta,z))_{+}
is

L_{f,1}
-Lipschitz, and therefore, for every
\tau
,

\tau-\frac{1}{\delta}\mathbb{E}[(\tau-f(\theta,X))_{+}]\leq\tau-\frac{1}{\delta}\mathbb{E}[(\tau-f(\theta,Y))_{+}]+\frac{L_{f,1}}{\delta}\mathbb{E}\|X-Y\|.

Taking the supremum over
\tau
and then the infimum over couplings gives (
30
). Combining
(
29
) and (
30
) with
\nu=\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}
gives
(
31
).

The deterministic bound (
31
) is valid for any
\delta
, but when
\delta=1/M
it yields the loose factor

\delta^{-1}=M
multiplying the empirical discrepancy term. We therefore analyze the hard-min selection directly.
Write
\tilde{x}\in\arg\min_{i}f(\theta,X_{i})
.
By Lipschitzness and the definition of
\bar{x}
,

f(\theta,\bar{x})\ \leq\ f(\theta,\tilde{x})+L_{f,1}\,\mathrm{dist}(\tilde{x},\mathcal{S}(\theta)).

Taking expectations gives

\mathbb{E}\big[f(\theta,\bar{x})-F(\theta)\big]\ \leq\ L_{f,1}\,\mathbb{E}\big[\mathrm{dist}(\tilde{x},\mathcal{S}(\theta))\big]+\mathbb{E}\Big[\min_{1\leq i\leq M}f(\theta,X_{i})\Big]-F(\theta).

(33)

Step 1: hard-min bound under exact Gibbs sampling.

Assume first that
X_{1},\dots,X_{M}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}
.
For each
i
, let
\Pi(X_{i})\in\arg\min_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)}\|x-X_{i}\|
be a Euclidean projection onto
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
(choose any measurable selection).
By Lipschitzness,

f(\theta,X_{i})\ \leq\ f(\theta,\Pi(X_{i}))+L_{f,1}\,\mathrm{dist}(X_{i},\mathcal{S}(\theta)).

Taking the minimum over
i
and using
\min_{i}(a_{i}+b_{i})\leq\min_{i}a_{i}+\max_{i}b_{i}
gives

\min_{1\leq i\leq M}f(\theta,X_{i})\ \leq\ \min_{1\leq i\leq M}f(\theta,\Pi(X_{i}))+\ L_{f,1}\max_{1\leq i\leq M}\mathrm{dist}(X_{i},\mathcal{S}(\theta)).

Plugging this into (
33
) and using
\mathrm{dist}(\tilde{x},\mathcal{S}(\theta))\leq\max_{i}\mathrm{dist}(X_{i},\mathcal{S}(\theta))
yields

\mathbb{E}\big[f(\theta,\bar{x})-F(\theta)\big]\ \leq\ 2L_{f,1}\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{1\leq i\leq M}\mathrm{dist}(X_{i},\mathcal{S}(\theta))\Big]+\ \Big(\mathbb{E}\Big[\min_{1\leq i\leq M}f(\theta,\Pi(X_{i}))\Big]-F(\theta)\Big).

(34)

The tube bound in
Section

E.3.2
gives

\mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{1\leq i\leq M}\mathrm{dist}(X_{i},\mathcal{S}(\theta))\Big]\leq C_{\mathrm{tube}}\sqrt{\lambda\log(1+M)}.

For the on-manifold term, note that the projected points
\Pi(X_{i})
lie on
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
. Under our standing
regularity assumptions, the projected Gibbs law converges, as
\lambda\downarrow 0
, to the limiting on-manifold law

\mu_{\theta}^{0}
, whose density with respect to Riemannian volume on
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
is continuous and strictly
positive near
x^{\star}(\theta)
; see
[
48
, Prop. 3.7]
. Therefore, after decreasing

\lambda_{0}
if needed, the projected Gibbs laws satisfy a uniform small-ball lower bound near
x^{\star}(\theta)
.
Combined with geodesic volume scaling on
\mathcal{S}(\theta)

[
48
, Lem. 4.1]
–whose curvature
requirement is verified in
Section

E.3.1
–there are constants
c_{0}>0
and
r_{\mathrm{nn}}>0

such that, for every
0<\lambda\leq\lambda_{0}
,

\mathbb{P}\big(d_{\mathcal{S}(\theta)}(\Pi(X_{1}),x^{\star}(\theta))\leq r\big)\geq c_{0}r^{\texttt{k}},\qquad 0<r\leq r_{\mathrm{nn}}.

Let
D_{M}:=\min_{1\leq i\leq M}d_{\mathcal{S}(\theta)}(\Pi(X_{i}),x^{\star}(\theta))
. The small-ball bound gives

\mathbb{P}(D_{M}>r)\leq(1-c_{0}r^{\texttt{k}})^{M}\leq\exp(-Mc_{0}r^{\texttt{k}}),\qquad 0<r\leq r_{\mathrm{nn}},

Let
\mathrm{diam}_{\mathcal{S}}
denote the finite geodesic diameter of
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
. Integrating this tail bound gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[D_{M}]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\mathrm{diam}_{\mathcal{S}}}\mathbb{P}(D_{M}>r)\,\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{r_{\mathrm{nn}}}\exp(-Mc_{0}r^{\texttt{k}})\,\mathrm{d}r+\mathrm{diam}_{\mathcal{S}}\exp(-Mc_{0}r_{\mathrm{nn}}^{\texttt{k}})

\displaystyle\leq C_{\mathrm{nn}}M^{-1/\texttt{k}},

where the last line uses
\texttt{k}\geq 1
and absorbs the exponentially small term into the same polynomial rate for
all
M\geq 1
.
Since
f(\theta,\cdot)
is
L_{f,1}
-Lipschitz on
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
,

\mathbb{E}\Big[\min_{1\leq i\leq M}f(\theta,\Pi(X_{i}))\Big]-F(\theta)\leq L_{f,1}\mathbb{E}[D_{M}]\leq C_{1}M^{-1/\texttt{k}}.

Moreover, the display preceding (
34
) also gives

\displaystyle L_{f,1}\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{1\leq i\leq M}\mathrm{dist}(X_{i},\mathcal{S}(\theta))\Big]+\mathbb{E}\Big[\min_{1\leq i\leq M}f(\theta,X_{i})\Big]-F(\theta)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 2L_{f,1}\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{1\leq i\leq M}\mathrm{dist}(X_{i},\mathcal{S}(\theta))\Big]+\mathbb{E}\Big[\min_{1\leq i\leq M}f(\theta,\Pi(X_{i}))\Big]-F(\theta).

Substituting the tube and nearest-neighbor bounds into this display gives the exact-Gibbs reference bound

\displaystyle L_{f,1}\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{1\leq i\leq M}\mathrm{dist}(X_{i},\mathcal{S}(\theta))\Big]

(35)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}\Big[\min_{1\leq i\leq M}f(\theta,X_{i})\Big]-F(\theta)

\displaystyle\leq 2L_{f,1}\,C_{\mathrm{tube}}\,\sqrt{\lambda\log(1+M)}+\ C_{1}\,M^{-1/\texttt{k}}.

Step 2: transfer to Rényi-controlled candidates.

We now return to independent candidates
X_{1},\dots,X_{M}
with marginal laws
\nu_{i}
satisfying

R_{2}(\nu_{i}\|\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda})\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}
.
For each
i
, choose an optimal coupling of
X_{i}\sim\nu_{i}
with a reference variable

X_{i}^{\lambda}\sim\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}
so that

\mathbb{E}\|X_{i}-X_{i}^{\lambda}\|^{2}\leq\mathsf{W}_{2}(\nu_{i},\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda})^{2}.

Taking the product of these pairwise couplings gives a joint coupling whose first marginal is

\nu_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\nu_{M}
, the law of the actual independent candidates, and whose second marginal is

(\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda})^{\otimes M}
. Thus the reference variables
X_{1}^{\lambda},\ldots,X_{M}^{\lambda}
are i.i.d. from
\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}
, and expectations of functions of
X_{1},\ldots,X_{M}
are unchanged under this coupling.
We use the two deterministic comparisons

\displaystyle\mathrm{dist}(\tilde{x},\mathcal{S}(\theta))

\displaystyle\leq\max_{1\leq i\leq M}\mathrm{dist}(X_{i},\mathcal{S}(\theta))

\displaystyle\leq\max_{1\leq i\leq M}\mathrm{dist}(X_{i}^{\lambda},\mathcal{S}(\theta))+\max_{1\leq i\leq M}\|X_{i}-X_{i}^{\lambda}\|

and

\min_{1\leq i\leq M}f(\theta,X_{i})\leq\min_{1\leq i\leq M}f(\theta,X_{i}^{\lambda})+L_{f,1}\max_{1\leq i\leq M}\|X_{i}-X_{i}^{\lambda}\|,

and combine them with (
33
), applied to the actual candidates, to obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[f(\theta,\bar{x})-F(\theta)\big]

\displaystyle\leq L_{f,1}\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{1\leq i\leq M}\mathrm{dist}(X_{i}^{\lambda},\mathcal{S}(\theta))\Big]+\mathbb{E}\Big[\min_{1\leq i\leq M}f(\theta,X_{i}^{\lambda})\Big]-F(\theta)

\displaystyle\quad+2L_{f,1}\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{1\leq i\leq M}\|X_{i}-X_{i}^{\lambda}\|\Big].

The first two terms on the right are controlled by the exact-Gibbs reference bound (
35
). It remains
to bound the coupling error

2L_{f,1}\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{1\leq i\leq M}\|X_{i}-X_{i}^{\lambda}\|\Big].

Finally,
\max_{i}a_{i}\leq(\sum_{i}a_{i}^{2})^{1/2}
gives

\mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{1\leq i\leq M}\|X_{i}-X_{i}^{\lambda}\|\Big]\leq\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{M}\mathbb{E}\|X_{i}-X_{i}^{\lambda}\|^{2}\Big)^{1/2}\leq\Bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{M}\mathsf{W}_{2}(\nu_{i},\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda})^{2}\Bigg)^{1/2},

and the Poincaré/Rényi transportation inequality
[
44
]
gives

\mathsf{W}_{2}(\nu_{i},\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda})\leq 2\,C_{\mathrm{PI}}^{1/2}\big(e^{R_{2}(\nu_{i}\|\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda})}-1\big)^{1/2}\leq 2\sqrt{(e-1)C_{\mathrm{PI}}}\,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}.

The last inequality uses
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}\leq 1
, so

e^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}}-1\leq(e-1)\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}
.
Therefore

2L_{f,1}\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{1\leq i\leq M}\|X_{i}-X_{i}^{\lambda}\|\Big]\leq 4\sqrt{e-1}\,L_{f,1}\sqrt{M\,C_{\mathrm{PI}}}\,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}.

Combining this estimate with (
35
) proves (
32
).
∎

E.3.4
Converting the on-manifold gap to distance (link to local identifiability)

Quadratic growth on
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
from Riemannian non-degeneracy.

To convert an on-manifold value gap into a distance bound, we use a local growth inequality for the restriction of

f(\theta,\cdot)
to the manifold
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
around its optimistic minimizer
x^{\star}(\theta)
.
The next lemma is the local deterministic step: positive Riemannian Hessian of the restricted objective implies
quadratic growth along the manifold.

Lemma E.12

(Local quadratic growth from Riemannian non-degeneracy)
.

Fix
\theta
and suppose that
f(\theta,\cdot)
is
\mathcal{C}^{2}
in a neighborhood of
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
and that

x^{\star}(\theta)\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)
is a local minimizer of
f(\theta,\cdot)|_{\mathcal{S}(\theta)}
.
Assume moreover that the Riemannian Hessian of the restricted objective is positive definite at
x^{\star}(\theta)
, i.e.,
there exists
m_{\theta}>0
such that

\big\langle v,\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{S}(\theta)}\bar{f}_{\theta}(x^{\star}(\theta))[v]\big\rangle\ \geq\ m_{\theta}\|v\|^{2},\qquad\forall v\in\mathcal{T}_{x^{\star}(\theta)}^{\theta},

where
\bar{f}_{\theta}:=f(\theta,\cdot)|_{\mathcal{S}(\theta)}
.
Then there exist constants
c_{\mathrm{hg}}>0
and
r_{0}>0
such that for all
x\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)
with

d_{\mathcal{S}(\theta)}(x,x^{\star}(\theta))\leq r_{0}
,

f(\theta,x)-f(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))\ \geq\ c_{\mathrm{hg}}\;d_{\mathcal{S}(\theta)}(x,x^{\star}(\theta))^{2}.

(36)

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{M}:=\mathcal{S}(\theta)
be the embedded submanifold with the induced Riemannian metric, and define the restriction

\bar{f}:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbb{R}
by
\bar{f}(x):=f(\theta,x)
.
Since
f(\theta,\cdot)
is
\mathcal{C}^{2}
on a neighborhood of
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{M}
is
\mathcal{C}^{2}
embedded,

\bar{f}
is
\mathcal{C}^{2}
on
\mathcal{M}
.

By the local-minimizer assumption,
x^{\star}:=x^{\star}(\theta)
is a (local) minimizer of
\bar{f}
on
\mathcal{M}
, so the Riemannian gradient vanishes:

\mathrm{grad}_{\mathcal{M}}\bar{f}(x^{\star})=0
.
Moreover, the assumed Riemannian non-degeneracy gives positive curvature of the restricted objective in every tangent direction.
Define the minimal Riemannian Hessian eigenvalue

m\ :=\ \min_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathcal{T}_{x^{\star}}\mathcal{M}\\
\|v\|=1\end{subarray}}\big\langle v,\ \mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{M}}\bar{f}(x^{\star})[v]\big\rangle\ >\ 0.

Since
\mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{M}}\bar{f}
is continuous, there exists a neighborhood
\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{M}
of
x^{\star}

such that for all
y\in\mathcal{U}
and all
w\in\mathcal{T}_{y}\mathcal{M}
,

\big\langle w,\ \mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{M}}\bar{f}(y)[w]\big\rangle\ \geq\ \tfrac{m}{2}\,\|w\|^{2}.

(37)

Let
r_{0}>0
be such that the closed geodesic ball

\{y\in\mathcal{M}:d_{\mathcal{M}}(y,x^{\star})\leq r_{0}\}
is contained in
\mathcal{U}
.

Fix any
x\in\mathcal{M}
with
d_{\mathcal{M}}(x,x^{\star})\leq r_{0}
.
Since
\mathcal{M}
is compact (and hence complete), the Hopf–Rinow theorem guarantees the existence of a minimizing geodesic

\gamma:[0,1]\to\mathcal{M}
from
x^{\star}
to
x
.
See, e.g.,
[
38
, Hopf–Rinow Theorem]
.
Parameterize
\gamma
at constant speed so that
\|\dot{\gamma}(t)\|=d_{\mathcal{M}}(x,x^{\star})
for all
t\in[0,1]
.
Because
\gamma
is minimizing, each subsegment is minimizing, and hence

d_{\mathcal{M}}(\gamma(t),x^{\star})\leq d_{\mathcal{M}}(x,x^{\star})\leq r_{0}
for all
t\in[0,1]
. Thus

\gamma([0,1])\subset\mathcal{U}
.

Define
\phi(t):=\bar{f}(\gamma(t))
.
Then
\phi^{\prime}(t)=\langle\mathrm{grad}_{\mathcal{M}}\bar{f}(\gamma(t)),\dot{\gamma}(t)\rangle
and in particular
\phi^{\prime}(0)=0
.
Differentiating once more and using that
\gamma
is a geodesic yields the standard identity

\phi^{\prime\prime}(t)\ =\ \big\langle\dot{\gamma}(t),\ \mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{M}}\bar{f}(\gamma(t))[\dot{\gamma}(t)]\big\rangle.

Therefore, by (
37
) and constant speed,

\phi^{\prime\prime}(t)\ \geq\ \tfrac{m}{2}\,\|\dot{\gamma}(t)\|^{2}\ =\ \tfrac{m}{2}\,d_{\mathcal{M}}(x,x^{\star})^{2}\qquad\forall t\in[0,1].

Using
\phi^{\prime}(0)=0
and the integral form of Taylor’s theorem,

\phi(1)-\phi(0)=\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)\,\phi^{\prime\prime}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\geq\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)\,\tfrac{m}{2}\,d_{\mathcal{M}}(x,x^{\star})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t=\tfrac{m}{4}\,d_{\mathcal{M}}(x,x^{\star})^{2}.

Recalling
\phi(1)=\bar{f}(x)
and
\phi(0)=\bar{f}(x^{\star})
gives (
36
) with
c_{\mathrm{hg}}:=m/4
.
∎

Uniform version over
\Theta
.

The restricted-curvature constant from
Section

A.3
makes the quadratic-growth constants uniform over

\Theta
.

Lemma E.13

(Uniform quadratic growth on
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
over
\Theta
)
.

Assume the setting of
Section

5
and let
m_{\mathrm{R}}>0
be the uniform restricted-curvature constant from

Section

A.3
.
Let
x^{\star}:\Theta\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
be the global selected minimizer from
2
.
Then there exist constants
c_{\mathrm{hg}}>0
and
r_{0}>0
such that for all
\theta\in\Theta
and all

x\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)
with
d_{\mathcal{S}(\theta)}(x,x^{\star}(\theta))\leq r_{0}
,

f(\theta,x)-f(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))\ \geq\ c_{\mathrm{hg}}\;d_{\mathcal{S}(\theta)}(x,x^{\star}(\theta))^{2}.

Proof.

If
\texttt{k}=0
, then each connected compact zero-dimensional manifold
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
is a singleton. Hence the only
point
x\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)
is
x^{\star}(\theta)
, and the claim is trivial for any fixed positive

c_{\mathrm{hg}}
and
r_{0}
. We therefore assume
\texttt{k}\geq 1
.

For each
\theta\in\Theta
, let
\mathcal{M}_{\theta}:=\mathcal{S}(\theta)
and define the restriction

\bar{f}_{\theta}:\mathcal{M}_{\theta}\to\mathbb{R}
by
\bar{f}_{\theta}(x):=f(\theta,x)
.
By
2
,
x^{\star}(\theta)
is the unique minimizer of
\bar{f}_{\theta}
on
\mathcal{M}_{\theta}
.
By
Section

A.3
, for every
\theta\in\Theta
,

\big\langle v,\ \mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{M}_{\theta}}\bar{f}_{\theta}(x^{\star}(\theta))[v]\big\rangle\geq m_{\mathrm{R}}\|v\|^{2},\qquad\forall v\in\mathcal{T}_{x^{\star}(\theta)}\mathcal{M}_{\theta}.

We next make the neighborhood on which this lower bound persists uniform. For each
\bar{\theta}\in\Theta
, continuity of
the Riemannian Hessian in local charts gives a parameter neighborhood
V_{\bar{\theta}}
and a radius

r_{\bar{\theta}}>0
such that, for every
\theta\in V_{\bar{\theta}}
, every

y\in\mathcal{M}_{\theta}
with
d_{\mathcal{M}_{\theta}}(y,x^{\star}(\theta))\leq r_{\bar{\theta}}
, and every

w\in\mathcal{T}_{y}\mathcal{M}_{\theta}
,

\big\langle w,\ \mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{M}_{\theta}}\bar{f}_{\theta}(y)[w]\big\rangle\ \geq\ \tfrac{m_{\mathrm{R}}}{2}\,\|w\|^{2}.

Choose a finite subcover
V_{\bar{\theta}_{1}},\ldots,V_{\bar{\theta}_{J}}
of
\Theta
and set

r_{0}:=\min_{1\leq j\leq J}r_{\bar{\theta}_{j}}>0.

Then, for all
\theta\in\Theta
, all
y\in\mathcal{M}_{\theta}
with
d_{\mathcal{M}_{\theta}}(y,x^{\star}(\theta))\leq r_{0}
, and all
w\in\mathcal{T}_{y}\mathcal{M}_{\theta}
,

\big\langle w,\ \mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{M}_{\theta}}\bar{f}_{\theta}(y)[w]\big\rangle\ \geq\ \tfrac{m_{\mathrm{R}}}{2}\,\|w\|^{2}.

Fix any
\theta\in\Theta
and
x\in\mathcal{M}_{\theta}
with
d_{\mathcal{M}_{\theta}}(x,x^{\star}(\theta))\leq r_{0}
.
Since
\mathcal{M}_{\theta}
is compact, the Hopf–Rinow theorem yields a minimizing geodesic
\gamma:[0,1]\to\mathcal{M}_{\theta}
from
x^{\star}(\theta)
to
x
.
Parameterize
\gamma
at constant speed so that
\|\dot{\gamma}(t)\|=d_{\mathcal{M}_{\theta}}(x,x^{\star}(\theta))
for all
t\in[0,1]
.
Since
\gamma
is minimizing,
d_{\mathcal{M}_{\theta}}(\gamma(t),x^{\star}(\theta))\leq r_{0}
for all
t\in[0,1]
, so the
uniform Hessian lower bound above applies along the whole curve.
As in the proof of
Section

E.3.4
, define
\phi(t):=\bar{f}_{\theta}(\gamma(t))
.
Then
\phi^{\prime}(0)=0
and

\phi^{\prime\prime}(t)\ =\ \big\langle\dot{\gamma}(t),\ \mathrm{Hess}_{\mathcal{M}_{\theta}}\bar{f}_{\theta}(\gamma(t))[\dot{\gamma}(t)]\big\rangle\ \geq\ \tfrac{m_{\mathrm{R}}}{2}\,d_{\mathcal{M}_{\theta}}(x,x^{\star}(\theta))^{2},

so the same integral argument yields

f(\theta,x)-f(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta))\ =\ \phi(1)-\phi(0)\ \geq\ \tfrac{m_{\mathrm{R}}}{4}\,d_{\mathcal{M}_{\theta}}(x,x^{\star}(\theta))^{2}.

The claim follows with
c_{\mathrm{hg}}:=m_{\mathrm{R}}/4
.
∎

Removing the locality restriction.

The quadratic growth bound from
Section

E.3.4
only applies within a geodesic neighborhood of radius
r_{0}
around
x^{\star}(\theta)
.
To remove the explicit restriction
d_{\mathcal{S}(\theta)}(\bar{x},x^{\star}(\theta))\leq r_{0}
, we use a value gap away from this neighborhood.

Lemma E.14

(Uniform value gap away from the selected minimizer)
.

Let
(c_{\mathrm{hg}},r_{0})
be the constants from
Section

E.3.4
and define

\mathcal{S}_{r_{0}}(\theta):=\{x\in\mathcal{S}(\theta):d_{\mathcal{S}(\theta)}(x,x^{\star}(\theta))\geq r_{0}\},\qquad\Delta_{r_{0}}:=\inf_{\theta\in\Theta}\ \min_{x\in\mathcal{S}_{r_{0}}(\theta)}\big(f(\theta,x)-F(\theta)\big),

with the convention that empty sets are ignored in the infimum. If all
\mathcal{S}_{r_{0}}(\theta)
are empty, choose any fixed positive value for
\Delta_{r_{0}}
.
Then
\Delta_{r_{0}}>0
.

Proof.

If all
\mathcal{S}_{r_{0}}(\theta)
are empty, the claim is immediate by convention.
Otherwise, consider

\mathcal{A}_{r_{0}}:=\{(\theta,x):\theta\in\Theta,\ x\in\mathcal{S}(\theta),\ d_{\mathcal{S}(\theta)}(x,x^{\star}(\theta))\geq r_{0}\}.

Under the uniform regularity assumptions used in
Section

E.3.4
, the compact manifolds
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
vary continuously in local charts over compact
\Theta
; hence their graph is compact and the geodesic distance to the continuous selected branch
x^{\star}(\theta)
is continuous on this graph. Therefore
\mathcal{A}_{r_{0}}
is compact. It is also disjoint from the selected graph
\{(\theta,x^{\star}(\theta)):\theta\in\Theta\}
.

The gap map
(\theta,x)\mapsto f(\theta,x)-F(\theta)
is continuous and strictly positive on
\mathcal{A}_{r_{0}}
: if it vanished at some
(\theta,x)\in\mathcal{A}_{r_{0}}
, then
x
would be another minimizer of
f(\theta,\cdot)
over
\mathcal{S}(\theta)
, contradicting the uniqueness in
2
. A positive continuous function on a compact set has a positive minimum, which is exactly
\Delta_{r_{0}}
.
∎

Lemma E.15

(Bounding
\mathbb{E}\|\bar{x}-x^{\star}(\theta)\|^{2}
without a locality condition)
.

Let
(c_{\mathrm{hg}},r_{0})
be the constants from
Section

E.3.4
, and let
\Delta_{r_{0}}>0
be the uniform away-gap from
Section

E.3.4
.
Then for any
\theta\in\Theta
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|\bar{x}-x^{\star}(\theta)\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\ \Bigg(\frac{1}{c_{\mathrm{hg}}}+\frac{4\mathsf{D}^{2}}{\Delta_{r_{0}}}\Bigg)\,\mathbb{E}\big[f(\theta,\bar{x})-F(\theta)\big].

Proof.

Fix
\theta\in\Theta
and define the suboptimality gap
\Delta(\bar{x}):=f(\theta,\bar{x})-F(\theta)\geq 0
.
Consider the event
\mathcal{A}:=\{\Delta(\bar{x})<\Delta_{r_{0}}\}
.
By definition of
\Delta_{r_{0}}
, on
\mathcal{A}
we must have
d_{\mathcal{S}(\theta)}(\bar{x},x^{\star}(\theta))<r_{0}
, so

Section

E.3.4
gives

d_{\mathcal{S}(\theta)}(\bar{x},x^{\star}(\theta))\ \leq\ \left(\frac{\Delta(\bar{x})}{c_{\mathrm{hg}}}\right)^{1/2}.

Since the ambient Euclidean distance is bounded by the geodesic distance, this implies

\|\bar{x}-x^{\star}(\theta)\|^{2}\leq\Delta(\bar{x})/c_{\mathrm{hg}}
on
\mathcal{A}
.
On
\mathcal{A}^{c}
, use
\|\bar{x}-x^{\star}(\theta)\|^{2}\leq 4\mathsf{D}^{2}
(since
\mathcal{S}(\theta)\subseteq\mathbb{B}_{d}(0;\mathsf{D})
).
Therefore,

\mathbb{E}\|\bar{x}-x^{\star}(\theta)\|^{2}\ \leq\ \frac{1}{c_{\mathrm{hg}}}\mathbb{E}\big[\Delta(\bar{x})\big]\ +\ 4\mathsf{D}^{2}\,\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}^{c}).

Finally,
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{A}^{c})=\mathbb{P}(\Delta(\bar{x})\geq\Delta_{r_{0}})\leq\mathbb{E}[\Delta(\bar{x})]/\Delta_{r_{0}}
by Markov,
yielding the claim.
∎

E.3.5
Sampler accuracy from Rényi divergence

The hard-selection value-gap bound above is stated directly for candidates whose laws are Rényi-close to the ideal Gibbs law.
The next statements explain how finite-step ULA supplies this Rényi accuracy and recall the Poincaré/Rényi transportation control used in the proof of
Section

E.3.3
.

We restate a quantitative order-
2
Rényi mixing bound for ULA from
48
and record its implication for Wasserstein error under a Poincaré inequality.

Proposition E.16

(ULA convergence in order-
2
Rényi divergence
[
48
, Prop. 5.6]
)
.

Consider the unadjusted Langevin algorithm (ULA) iteration

X^{k+1}=X^{k}-h\nabla G(X^{k})+\sqrt{2h}\,\xi^{k},\qquad\xi^{k}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}),

targeting
\pi(\mathrm{d}x)\propto e^{-G(x)}\,\mathrm{d}x
.
Assume
\nabla G
is
L_{G,2}
-Lipschitz and that
\pi
satisfies a Poincaré inequality with constant
C_{\mathrm{PI}}
.
Let
\widehat{\mu}_{n}
be the law of
X^{n}
.
Then for any sufficiently small
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and an appropriate stepsize choice (as required by the ULA theory),
one can ensure
R_{2}(\widehat{\mu}_{n}\|\pi)\leq\varepsilon^{2}
after

n\;=\;\Theta\!\left(C_{\mathrm{PI}}^{2}L_{G,2}^{2}\,d\,\varepsilon^{-2}\Big(R_{3}(\widehat{\mu}_{0}\|\pi)^{2}+\log^{2}(1/\varepsilon)\Big)\right)

(38)

iterations, where
R_{q}(\cdot\|\cdot)
denotes the order-
q
Rényi divergence.
Moreover, using the transportation-variance implication of the Poincaré inequality
[
44
]
to relate

W_{2}
and
R_{2}
, we obtain

\mathsf{W}_{2}(\widehat{\mu}_{n},\pi)\ \leq\ 2\,C_{\mathrm{PI}}^{1/2}\,\big(e^{R_{2}(\widehat{\mu}_{n}\|\pi)}-1\big)^{1/2}\ \leq\ 2\,C_{\mathrm{PI}}^{1/2}\,C^{1/2}\,\varepsilon,

(39)

where one may take the numerical constant
C:=2(e^{1/2}-1)
.

Lemma E.17

(Order-
3
Rényi warm start for
\lambda
-scaled Gaussian initialization)
.

Let
4
hold, and let
g_{\star}(\theta):=\min_{x}g(\theta,x)
.
Fix a compact set
\mathcal{Z}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and a variance parameter

0<\tau<\frac{3}{2L_{g,2}}.

For
z\in\mathcal{Z}
, let

\widehat{\mu}_{0,z}^{\lambda}:=\mathcal{N}(z,\tau\lambda I_{d}),\qquad 0<\lambda\leq 1.

Then there are constants
C_{\mathrm{init}},C_{\mathrm{init}}^{\prime}<\infty
, independent of

\theta,z,\lambda
, such that

R_{3}\!\left(\widehat{\mu}_{0,z}^{\lambda}\middle\|\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}\right)\leq\frac{C_{\mathrm{init}}}{\lambda}\ +\ C_{\mathrm{init}}^{\prime}\bigl(1+\log(1/\lambda)\bigr).

(40)

Moreover, if
z\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)
, then the stronger bound

R_{3}\!\left(\widehat{\mu}_{0,z}^{\lambda}\middle\|\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}\right)\leq C_{\mathrm{init}}^{\prime}\bigl(1+\log(1/\lambda)\bigr)

(41)

holds after increasing
C_{\mathrm{init}}^{\prime}
if necessary.

Proof.

Write
p_{z,\lambda}
for the density of
\widehat{\mu}_{0,z}^{\lambda}
, and write

Z_{\theta}^{\lambda}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp\{-g(\theta,x)/\lambda\}\,\mathrm{d}x

for the Gibbs normalizing constant. Since

\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}x)=(Z_{\theta}^{\lambda})^{-1}e^{-g(\theta,x)/\lambda}\mathrm{d}x
, the order-
3
Rényi
divergence is

R_{3}\!\left(\widehat{\mu}_{0,z}^{\lambda}\middle\|\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\log\left[(Z_{\theta}^{\lambda})^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p_{z,\lambda}(x)^{3}\exp\{2g(\theta,x)/\lambda\}\,\mathrm{d}x\right].

First, by
Section

E.3.2
and
\mathcal{S}(\theta)\subseteq\mathbb{B}_{d}(0;\mathsf{D})
,

Z_{\theta}^{\lambda}\leq e^{-g_{\star}(\theta)/\lambda}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp\!\left\{-\frac{\mu_{\mathrm{QG}}}{2\lambda}\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{S}(\theta))^{2}\right\}\mathrm{d}x\leq C_{Z}e^{-g_{\star}(\theta)/\lambda}

for all
0<\lambda\leq 1
, with
C_{Z}
uniform in
\theta
. The last inequality follows because

\mathrm{dist}(x,\mathcal{S}(\theta))\geq(\|x\|-\mathsf{D})_{+}
, and the resulting Gaussian tail is integrable uniformly for

\lambda\leq 1
.

Next, set
u=x-z
. By
L_{g,2}
-smoothness in
x
,

g(\theta,z+u)\leq g(\theta,z)+\langle\nabla_{x}g(\theta,z),u\rangle+\frac{L_{g,2}}{2}\|u\|^{2}.

Since

p_{z,\lambda}(z+u)^{3}=(2\pi\tau\lambda)^{-3d/2}\exp\!\left\{-\frac{3}{2\tau\lambda}\|u\|^{2}\right\},

and
a:=3/(2\tau)-L_{g,2}>0
, Gaussian integration gives

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}p_{z,\lambda}(x)^{3}\exp\{2g(\theta,x)/\lambda\}\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq C\,\lambda^{-d}\exp\!\left\{\frac{2g(\theta,z)}{\lambda}+\frac{\|\nabla_{x}g(\theta,z)\|^{2}}{a\lambda}\right\}.

Combining the two displays yields

R_{3}\!\left(\widehat{\mu}_{0,z}^{\lambda}\middle\|\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}\right)\leq\frac{g(\theta,z)-g_{\star}(\theta)}{\lambda}\ +\ \frac{\|\nabla_{x}g(\theta,z)\|^{2}}{2a\lambda}\ +\ \frac{d}{2}\log(1/\lambda)+C.

The first two terms are uniformly bounded by
C_{\mathrm{init}}/\lambda
, because

\Theta\times\mathcal{Z}
is compact and
g_{\star}
is continuous on
\Theta
.
This proves (
40
). If
z\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)
, then

g(\theta,z)=g_{\star}(\theta)
and
\nabla_{x}g(\theta,z)=0
, so only the logarithmic term remains, proving
(
41
).
∎

Corollary E.18

(Specialization to Gibbs sampling (Rényi accuracy implies Wasserstein control))
.

Apply
Section

E.3.5
with
G(x)=g(\theta,x)/\lambda
and
\pi=\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda}
.
Then
L_{G,2}=L_{g,2}/\lambda
and the ULA update

X^{k+1}=X^{k}-h\nabla_{x}g(\theta,X^{k})+\sqrt{2\lambda h}\,\xi^{k}

corresponds to the same ULA scheme with stepsize
h^{\prime}=\lambda h
for
G
.
In particular, for any target Rényi tolerance
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}\in(0,1)
, running the chain for

n=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\!\left(d\,C_{\mathrm{PI}}^{2}\lambda^{-2}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}^{-2}\bigl(1+R_{3}(\widehat{\mu}_{0}\|\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda})^{2}\bigr)\right)

iterations ensures
R_{2}(\widehat{\mu}_{n}\|\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda})\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}
.
If, moreover,
\widehat{\mu}_{0}=\widehat{\mu}_{0,z}^{\lambda}
is the
\lambda
-scaled Gaussian initialization in

Section

E.3.5
with
z\in\mathcal{Z}
, then it suffices to run

n\ =\ \widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\!\left(d\,C_{\mathrm{PI}}^{2}\,\lambda^{-4}\,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}^{-2}\right)

(42)

iterations. If the Gaussian center satisfies
z\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)
, then the sharper logarithmic bound
(
41
) gives the improved sufficient count

n\ =\ \widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\!\left(d\,C_{\mathrm{PI}}^{2}\,\lambda^{-2}\,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}^{-2}\right).

In all cases, once

R_{2}(\widehat{\mu}_{n}\|\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda})\ \leq\ \varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}^{2},

we have

\mathsf{W}_{2}(\widehat{\mu}_{n},\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda})\ \leq\ 2\sqrt{(e-1)C_{\mathrm{PI}}}\,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}},\qquad\text{and hence}\qquad\mathsf{W}_{1}(\widehat{\mu}_{n},\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda})\ \leq\ 2\sqrt{(e-1)C_{\mathrm{PI}}}\,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}.

E.3.6
Final assembly of the selection-error bound

We now assemble the tube bound, the hard-selection value gap, the on-manifold growth estimate, and the ULA accuracy bound into a single bound on the squared (Euclidean) selection error

\mathbb{E}\|\tilde{x}-x^{\star}(\theta)\|^{2}
.

Proof of
Section

5
.

Let
\bar{x}\in\arg\min_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\theta)}\|x-\tilde{x}\|
be a measurable Euclidean projection of
\tilde{x}
onto

\mathcal{S}(\theta)
.
By the Euclidean triangle inequality and
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2a^{2}+2b^{2}
,

\|\tilde{x}-x^{\star}(\theta)\|^{2}\leq 2\|\tilde{x}-\bar{x}\|^{2}+2\|\bar{x}-x^{\star}(\theta)\|^{2}=2\,\mathrm{dist}(\tilde{x},\mathcal{S}(\theta))^{2}+2\|\bar{x}-x^{\star}(\theta)\|^{2}.

Taking expectations and applying (
28
) from
Section

E.3.2
, together with

e^{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}/2}\leq e^{1/2}
since
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}\leq 1
, gives

\mathbb{E}\|\tilde{x}-x^{\star}(\theta)\|^{2}\leq 2e^{1/2}C_{\mathrm{tube},2}\,\lambda\log(1+N)+2\,\mathbb{E}\|\bar{x}-x^{\star}(\theta)\|^{2}.

Then
Section

E.3.4
yields

\mathbb{E}\|\bar{x}-x^{\star}(\theta)\|^{2}\leq\Bigg(\frac{1}{c_{\mathrm{hg}}}+\frac{4\mathsf{D}^{2}}{\Delta_{r_{0}}}\Bigg)\,\mathbb{E}\big[f(\theta,\bar{x})-F(\theta)\big].

Finally, the Rényi-controlled hard-selection bound (
32
) from
Section

E.3.3
gives

\mathbb{E}\big[f(\theta,\bar{x})-F(\theta)\big]\leq 2L_{f,1}\,C_{\mathrm{tube}}\,\sqrt{\lambda\log(1+N)}+C_{1}\,N^{-1/\texttt{k}}+4\sqrt{e-1}\,L_{f,1}\sqrt{N\,C_{\mathrm{PI}}}\,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}},

which gives (
6
).
∎

Plug-in form.

With
Section

E.3.5
, one can run the sampler until the candidate laws satisfy

R_{2}(\nu_{i}\|\mu_{\theta}^{\lambda})\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}
.
This yields an explicit end-to-end bound on
\mathbb{E}\|\tilde{x}-x^{\star}(\theta)\|^{2}
in terms of:
(i) the number of candidates
N
,
(ii) the Gibbs temperature
\lambda
,
(iii) the achieved Rényi tolerance
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}
through the additive

\sqrt{N\,C_{\mathrm{PI}}}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}
contribution,
and (iv) the on-manifold approximation term
N^{-1/\texttt{k}}
.

E.4
Parameter choices and oracle complexity

This appendix states the parameter-level hyper-gradient error bound obtained by combining (
5
) with the selection-error bound (
6
), and then records a convenient parameter choice ensuring

\sup_{t}\mathbb{E}\|e_{t}\|^{2}=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})
.
Together with
Section

5
, this yields the
\varepsilon
-stationarity guarantee and conservative polynomial oracle bound reported in
Section

5
.

Lemma E.19

(Hyper-gradient error in terms of algorithmic parameters)
.

Using the notation of
Sections

5
and
5
, suppose the assumptions therein hold.
Assume additionally that
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}\leq 1
and
0<\gamma\leq 1
.
Then, uniformly over
t
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|e_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\!\Bigg(\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}}\Big[\lambda\log(1+N)\ +\ \sqrt{\lambda\log(1+N)}\ +\ N^{-1/\texttt{k}}\ +\ \sqrt{N\,C_{\mathrm{PI}}}\,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}\ +\ \eta_{t}^{2}\Big]\ +\ \gamma^{2}

(43)

\displaystyle+\ (1+R_{v}^{2})N\big(1+r(\gamma)^{2\mathsf{n}_{\theta x}}+\lambda^{\mathsf{n}_{\theta x}}\big)

\displaystyle\times\exp\!\Big(-\tfrac{c_{\mathrm{tube}}}{4}\tfrac{r(\gamma)^{2}}{\lambda}\Big)\Bigg).

Proof.

We first condition on the current outer iterate
\theta_{t}
. The constants in
Section

5
and

Section

E.3.2
are uniform over
\theta\in\Theta
, so the resulting conditional bounds can be averaged over

\theta_{t}
without changing the constants. Combining (
5
) with (
6
) from

Section

5
and the bound

\mathbb{E}\big[\|\tilde{x}_{t}-x^{\star}(\theta_{t})\|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t}}\big]\leq\mathbb{E}\|\tilde{x}_{t}-x^{\star}(\theta_{t})\|^{2}
,
we obtain the following bound, uniformly over
t
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|e_{t}\|^{2}\leq

\displaystyle 3C_{x}^{2}B_{\gamma}^{2}\,\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{sel}}(N,\lambda,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}})\ +\ \frac{12C_{\mathrm{lin}}^{2}}{\gamma^{2}}\,\eta_{t}^{2}\ +\ 3C_{\mathrm{reg}}^{2}\gamma^{2}

(44)

\displaystyle+\ C_{\mathrm{off}}(1+R_{v}^{2})\,\mathbb{E}\big[(1+\mathsf{D}_{t}^{2\mathsf{n}_{\theta x}})\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t}^{c}}\big],

where

B_{\gamma}:=1+\frac{2}{\gamma}+\frac{2}{\gamma(c+\gamma)}

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{sel}}(N,\lambda,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}):=

\displaystyle 2e^{1/2}C_{\mathrm{tube},2}\,\lambda\log(1+N)

(45)

\displaystyle+2\Bigg(\frac{1}{c_{\mathrm{hg}}}\ +\ \frac{4\mathsf{D}^{2}}{\Delta_{r_{0}}}\Bigg)\Big(2L_{f,1}C_{\mathrm{tube}}\sqrt{\lambda\log(1+N)}\ +\ C_{1}N^{-1/\texttt{k}}

\displaystyle+\ 4\sqrt{e-1}\,L_{f,1}\sqrt{N\,C_{\mathrm{PI}}}\,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}\Big).

To control the off-tube moment, the candidate-marginal tube bound from Appendix
E.3

(Lemma
E.3.2
) and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}\leq 1
yield, for

\mathsf{D}_{t}=\mathrm{dist}(\tilde{x}_{t},\mathcal{S}(\theta_{t}))
,

\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{D}_{t}>s)\leq N\,e^{1/2}\,\sqrt{C_{\mathrm{tube}}}\exp\!\Big(-\tfrac{c_{\mathrm{tube}}}{2}\tfrac{s^{2}}{\lambda}\Big),\qquad s\geq 0.

Indeed,
\mathsf{D}_{t}\leq\max_{i}\mathrm{dist}(X_{t,i},\mathcal{S}(\theta_{t}))
, so the maximum-tail bound in

Section

E.3.2
applies. Integrating the tail gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[(1+\mathsf{D}_{t}^{2\mathsf{n}_{\theta x}})\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{t}^{c}}\big]\leq

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{tail}}\,N\big(1+r(\gamma)^{2\mathsf{n}_{\theta x}}+\lambda^{\mathsf{n}_{\theta x}}\big)

(46)

\displaystyle\times\exp\!\Big(-\tfrac{c_{\mathrm{tube}}}{4}\tfrac{r(\gamma)^{2}}{\lambda}\Big),

where
C_{\mathrm{tail}}
depends only on
\mathsf{n}_{\theta x}
and problem-data constants. One way to see
(
46
) is to use the identity

\mathbb{E}\big[\mathsf{D}_{t}^{2p}\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathsf{D}_{t}>r\}}\big]=r^{2p}\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{D}_{t}>r)\ +\ \int_{r}^{\infty}2p\,s^{2p-1}\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{D}_{t}>s)\,\mathrm{d}s,

with
p=\mathsf{n}_{\theta x}
and
r=r(\gamma)
. In particular, if

\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{D}_{t}>s)\leq K\exp(-as^{2}/\lambda)
, then

\mathbb{P}(\mathsf{D}_{t}>r)\ +\ \mathbb{E}\big[\mathsf{D}_{t}^{2p}\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathsf{D}_{t}>r\}}\big]\leq C_{p}K\big(1+r^{2p}+\lambda^{p}\big)\exp\!\Big(-\tfrac{a}{2}\tfrac{r^{2}}{\lambda}\Big),

which gives (
46
) with
a=c_{\mathrm{tube}}/2
and

K=Ne^{1/2}\sqrt{C_{\mathrm{tube}}}
.

Substituting (
46
) into (
44
) gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|e_{t}\|^{2}\leq

\displaystyle C\,B_{\gamma}^{2}\Big[\lambda\log(1+N)\ +\ \sqrt{\lambda\log(1+N)}\ +\ N^{-1/\texttt{k}}\ +\ \sqrt{N\,C_{\mathrm{PI}}}\,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}\Big]

(47)

\displaystyle+\frac{12C_{\mathrm{lin}}^{2}}{\gamma^{2}}\eta_{t}^{2}\ +\ 3C_{\mathrm{reg}}^{2}\gamma^{2}

\displaystyle+\ C(1+R_{v}^{2})N\big(1+r(\gamma)^{2\mathsf{n}_{\theta x}}+\lambda^{\mathsf{n}_{\theta x}}\big)\exp\!\Big(-\tfrac{c_{\mathrm{tube}}}{4}\tfrac{r(\gamma)^{2}}{\lambda}\Big),

for a problem-data constant
C<\infty
independent of

N,\lambda,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}},\gamma,\eta_{t},R_{v}
, and
t
.
Since
c>0
is fixed and
0<\gamma\leq 1
,

B_{\gamma}^{2}=\mathcal{O}\!\Big(\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}}\Big).

Applying this simplification to (
47
), and absorbing numerical and problem-data constants,
gives (
43
).
Since
Theorem

5.4
is the same bound with the exponentially small off-tube term abbreviated as

\mathsf{OT}_{\gamma,\lambda,N,R_{v}}
, this proves the hyper-gradient error bound stated in
Section

5
.
∎

Choosing parameters to achieve
\mathbb{E}\|e_{t}\|^{2}=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})
.

In the non-singleton regime
\texttt{k}\geq 1
, fix a sufficiently small target
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
.
As in
Section

5
,
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}
denotes the square-root Rényi tolerance, so the sampler accuracy condition is
R_{2}(\nu_{i}\|\mu_{\theta_{t}}^{\lambda})\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}^{2}
.
We take
\varepsilon
small enough that the choices below satisfy
0<\lambda\leq\lambda_{0}
,
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}\leq 1
, and
0<\gamma\leq 1
.
To ensure
\sup_{t}\mathbb{E}\|e_{t}\|^{2}=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})
it suffices to make each term on the right-hand side of (
43
) of order at most
\varepsilon^{2}
.
One convenient choice is:
(i) set
\gamma=\varepsilon
so that
C_{\mathrm{reg}}\gamma=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)
,
(ii) solve the ridge system to residual
\eta_{t}=\gamma\varepsilon=\varepsilon^{2}
, so that
\eta_{t}^{2}/\gamma^{2}=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})
,
(iii) choose
R_{v}\geq(2/\gamma)(L_{f,1}+\eta_{t})
, so
R_{v}=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-1})
, and
(iv) choose the sampling/selection parameters so that the remaining bracketed terms satisfy

\lambda\log(1+N)\ +\ \sqrt{\lambda\log(1+N)}\ +\ N^{-1/\texttt{k}}\ +\ \sqrt{N\,C_{\mathrm{PI}}}\,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{4}).

A sufficient scaling is

N=\left\lceil\varepsilon^{-4\texttt{k}}\right\rceil,\qquad\lambda=\frac{\varepsilon^{8}}{\log(1+N)},\qquad\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}=\varepsilon^{4+2\texttt{k}}.

Then
\lambda\log(1+N)=\varepsilon^{8}
,
\sqrt{\lambda\log(1+N)}=\varepsilon^{4}
, and
N^{-1/\texttt{k}}=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{4})
.
Moreover, treating
C_{\mathrm{PI}}
as a problem-data constant,

\sqrt{N\,C_{\mathrm{PI}}}\,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}=\mathcal{O}\!\Big(\varepsilon^{-2\texttt{k}}\cdot\varepsilon^{4+2\texttt{k}}\Big)=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{4}).

Thus the displayed sampling/selection term is
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{4})
, and the corresponding contribution to

\mathbb{E}\|e_{t}\|^{2}
is
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})
after multiplication by the prefactor
\mathcal{O}(1/\gamma^{2})=\mathcal{O}(1/\varepsilon^{2})
.
It remains to check the off-tube moment term. By Lemma
E.2
,

r(\gamma)=\min\left\{\rho,\frac{\gamma}{2\bar{L}_{g,3}}\right\},

so for all sufficiently small
\varepsilon
,
r(\gamma)\geq c_{r}\varepsilon
for a problem-data constant
c_{r}>0
.
Consequently,

\frac{r(\gamma)^{2}}{\lambda}\ \geq\ c_{r}^{\prime}\,\varepsilon^{-6}\log(1+N).

Since
R_{v}=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{-1})
, the off-tube term

(1+R_{v}^{2})N\big(1+r(\gamma)^{2\mathsf{n}_{\theta x}}+\lambda^{\mathsf{n}_{\theta x}}\big)\exp\!\Big(-\tfrac{c_{\mathrm{tube}}}{4}\tfrac{r(\gamma)^{2}}{\lambda}\Big)

is bounded by a polynomial in
\varepsilon^{-1}
times

\exp(-c\,\varepsilon^{-6}\log(1+N))
for a problem-data constant
c>0
.
It is therefore super-polynomially small in
\varepsilon^{-1}
and, in particular, is
\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{2})
.

Substituting this control into
Section

5
(with stepsize
1/L_{F}
) yields an
\varepsilon
-stationary guarantee after

T=\mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{L_{F}\,(F(\theta_{0})-F_{\star})}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)

outer iterations.

Total oracle complexity.

Per outer iteration,
HG-MS
uses (a)
NK
evaluations of
\nabla_{x}g
for the sampler (
Algorithm

2
),
(b)
N
evaluations of
f(\theta_{t},X_{t,i})
for hard selection, and (c)
\#\mathrm{HVP}
Hessian–vector products for CG to reach residual tolerance
\eta_{t}
.
First consider the sampler/function-evaluation cost,

\mathcal{O}\big(T(NK+N)\big).

Assume each ULA chain is initialized from the
\lambda
-scaled Gaussian warm start in

Section

E.3.5
, with centers in a fixed compact set. If the ULA sampler is run for
K
steps so that the
square-root Rényi tolerance satisfies

\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}=\varepsilon^{4+2\texttt{k}}
, then
Section

E.3.5
gives

K=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d\,C_{\mathrm{PI}}^{2}\,\lambda^{-4}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}}^{-2})
.
Under the above scaling of
(N,\lambda,\varepsilon_{\mathrm{R}})
, this gives

K=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\!\big(d\,C_{\mathrm{PI}}^{2}\,\log^{4}(1+N)\,\varepsilon^{-40-4\texttt{k}}\big)
and hence

T\,N\,K=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\!\big(d\,C_{\mathrm{PI}}^{2}\,N\log^{4}(1+N)\,\varepsilon^{-42-4\texttt{k}}\big)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\!\big(d\,C_{\mathrm{PI}}^{2}\,\varepsilon^{-42-8\texttt{k}}\big),

which dominates the additional
TN
hard-selection evaluations.
If HVPs are counted as oracle calls, the total accounting becomes

\mathcal{O}\big(T(NK+N+\#\mathrm{HVP})\big)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\!\big(d\,C_{\mathrm{PI}}^{2}\,\varepsilon^{-42-8\texttt{k}}\big)+\mathcal{O}(T\,\#\mathrm{HVP}).

The following remark computes the CG/HVP count and shows that the displayed sampler term dominates it under the parameter choice above.

Remark E.20

(HVP count for the CG solve)
.

On the tube event, the ridge system solved by CG has coefficient matrix

A_{t}:=\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})+\gamma I.

Lemma
E.2
gives
\lambda_{\min}(A_{t})\geq\gamma/2
, while the
L_{g,2}
-smoothness of

g(\theta,\cdot)
gives
\lambda_{\max}(A_{t})\leq L_{g,2}+\gamma
. Hence

\kappa(A_{t})\leq\frac{2(L_{g,2}+\gamma)}{\gamma}.

The standard CG estimate for an SPD system
Av=b
,

\|v_{k}-v_{\star}\|_{A}\leq 2\left(\frac{\sqrt{\kappa(A)}-1}{\sqrt{\kappa(A)}+1}\right)^{k}\|v_{0}-v_{\star}\|_{A},

see
54
, implies the residual bound

\|b-Av_{k}\|\leq 2\sqrt{\kappa(A)}\left(\frac{\sqrt{\kappa(A)}-1}{\sqrt{\kappa(A)}+1}\right)^{k}\|b\|,

when initialized at
v_{0}=0
.
Each CG iteration applies
A_{t}
once, which requires one Hessian-vector product with

\nabla^{2}_{xx}g(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})
plus vector operations.
Therefore, to reach the residual tolerance
\eta_{t}
in
\|b_{t}-(H_{t}+\gamma I)\tilde{v}_{t}\|\leq\eta_{t}
, it suffices to take

\#\mathrm{HVP}=\mathcal{O}\!\left(\sqrt{\kappa(A_{t})}\log\frac{\sqrt{\kappa(A_{t})}\,\|\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})\|}{\eta_{t}}\right)=\mathcal{O}\!\left(\gamma^{-1/2}\log\frac{\gamma^{-1/2}L_{f,1}}{\eta_{t}}\right),

where the last step uses
\|\nabla_{x}f(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})\|\leq L_{f,1}
.
With
\gamma=\varepsilon
and
\eta_{t}=\varepsilon^{2}
, this gives

\#\mathrm{HVP}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{-1/2}),\qquad T\,\#\mathrm{HVP}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon^{-5/2}),

which is negligible compared with the sampler-dominated cost

\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(d\,C_{\mathrm{PI}}^{2}\varepsilon^{-42-8\texttt{k}})
.
The final multiplication by
\nabla^{2}_{\theta x}g(\theta_{t},\tilde{x}_{t})v_{t}
requires one additional mixed Hessian-vector product per outer iteration, which is also dominated by the sampler term.

Appendix F
Experimental details

F.1
Common protocol

Metrics.

For data hyper-cleaning we report
accuracy
(% correct).
For imbalanced loss tuning we report
balanced accuracy
(macro recall), i.e., the average of per-class recall,
reported as a percentage.
For controlled GPT-2 source reweighting we report token-level validation/test loss and source-weight recovery error.
For real-world LLM mathematical reasoning we report GSM8K and MATH accuracy (% correct), and for instruction following
we report MT-Bench score on the standard
0
–
10
scale.

Confidence intervals.

For the MNIST experiments, tables/plots report mean
\pm

95\%
confidence intervals across independent seeds, using a two-sided
t
-interval.
The real-world LLM results in
Table

2
are single-seed matched-budget comparisons. We complement them
with uncertainty estimates computed from the saved evaluation files; see
Table

4
. For GSM8K and
MATH, we treat each test example as correct or incorrect and compute a Wilson
95\%
binomial interval. For MT-Bench, we
resample the saved question-level GPT-4o judge scores over the
80
standard questions. The table reports the range of

95\%
confidence-interval half-widths across the cells in each benchmark, measured in accuracy percentage points for
GSM8K/MATH and MT-Bench score points for instruction following. These intervals quantify uncertainty from the finite
evaluation set and do not estimate variance from rerunning source reweighting or final fine-tuning with different seeds.

For time-budget plots and budget-sweep diagnostics, we measure wall-clock elapsed time for each algorithm. Fixed-budget

The experimental code and configuration files are available at

https://github.com/msmasiha1997/Experiment_HG_MS_paper
.

For the MNIST experiments, we compare
HG-MS
against

HG-baseline

[

]
, V-PBGD and G-PBGD
[

, and

, and we report fixed-budget means
\pm

confidence
intervals across seeds. Throughout,
\mathrm{CE}(\cdot,\cdot)
denotes cross-entropy (negative log-likelihood).

F.2
Data hyper-cleaning (MNIST label noise)

Setup.

We study
data hyper-cleaning
on MNIST
[
37
]
, a standard bilevel benchmark in which the upper-level variable assigns a
soft weight to each (potentially corrupted) training label.
The lower-level (follower) trains a classifier by minimizing a weighted training loss on the (possibly noisy) training
set, while the upper-level (leader) minimizes the validation loss on a small clean validation set.
We use a 2-layer sigmoid MLP with architecture
784\to 300\to 10
and follow the split used in our code: we subsample the MNIST
training

split and randomly partition it into
5{,}000
lower-level training points,
500
validation points, and
10{,}000
test
points (all drawn from the same underlying MNIST training set).
We corrupt a fraction
\rho\in\{0.4,0.6,0.8\}
of the training labels uniformly at random.
This yields a challenging regime (small training set with substantial label noise) in which explicit hyper-cleaning is
meaningful and differences between upper-level updates become visible under a fixed time budget.

Bilevel model.

Let
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}
be training-example hyper-weights and let
y
denote the follower network parameters.
Writing
w=\sigma(\theta)
(sigmoid reparameterization), we consider the bilevel problem

\min_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}}\ \min_{y\in\arg\min_{z}g(\theta,z)}f(\theta,y),

which is the optimistic formulation: for each
\theta
, among all minimizers of the weighted training loss
g(\theta,\cdot)
we select
the one with smallest validation loss
f(\theta,\cdot)
.
The lower-level objective is a weighted training loss

g(\theta,y)\;=\;\frac{1}{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\mathrm{tr}}}w_{i}\cdot\mathrm{CE}\big(h_{y}(x_{i}^{\mathrm{tr}}),\ \widetilde{c}_{i}^{\mathrm{tr}}\big)\;+\;\omega_{\mathrm{dhc}}\|y\|_{2}^{2},

and the upper-level objective is the validation loss on clean labels,

f(y)\;=\;\frac{1}{n_{\mathrm{val}}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{\mathrm{val}}}\mathrm{CE}\big(h_{y}(x_{j}^{\mathrm{val}}),\ c_{j}^{\mathrm{val}}\big).

Methods and optimization.

We use the following MNIST baselines:
HG-baseline
, V-PBGD, G-PBGD, and IAPTT-GM.
All methods use the same 2-layer sigmoid MLP architecture.
This label-noise regime can produce lower-level candidates with similar corrupted-training losses but different clean
validation losses.
HG-MS
is designed to select the candidate with the smallest clean validation loss before
computing the hyper-gradient.

Implementation details.

The AdamW/SGD lower-optimizer choice is selected by the validation-loss pilot sweep described in the common protocol.
For
HG-baseline
, the selected lower-level solver is a differentiable AdamW unroll with
20
inner steps per outer update,
lower learning rate
5\times 10^{-3}
, AdamW default betas, and weight decay
0
. The upper optimizer is AdamW on the
data-weight logits with learning rate
0.1
and weight decay
0
. The lower regularization coefficient above is

\omega_{\mathrm{dhc}}=10^{-4}
.
For
HG-MS
, we use
8
candidate branches, and each branch uses the selected lower-optimizer family from the
same AdamW/SGD sweep. Each branch takes
20
lower updates per outer step with branch
learning rate
2\times 10^{-2}
, minibatch size
256
, and branch weight decay
0
. The reported runs use no parameter initialization noise by default

(\texttt{branch\_init\_noise\_std}=0)
and no learning-rate diversity across branches. The upper learning rate starts at

0.1
and uses the default cosine schedule to
0.01
; the explicit
\rho=0.8
,
120
s script uses an inverse
schedule with decay steps
50
. The conjugate-gradient solve in
HG-MS
uses ridge
10^{-2}
, residual tolerance

10^{-3}
, at most
30
iterations, and full-batch HVP/CG when
\texttt{cg\_batch\_size}=0
.

per algorithm

\rho=0.8
,

improves the mean test accuracy from

(

)
to
66.1\%
, and outperforms the penalty/alternating baselines under the same time budget.
